Poisson Centralisers and Polynomial Superintegrability for Magnetic Geodesic Flows on Reductive Homogeneous Spaces


Abstract
. We provide a method for formulating superintegrable magnetic geodesic flows on reductive homogeneous spaces M = G / A M=G/A , with G G a compact semisimple Lie group and A A a closed subgroup of G G . In the twisted cotangent bundle ( T ∗ ​ M , ω ε ) (T^{*}M,\omega_{\varepsilon}) , with ω ε = ω can + ε ​ π ∗ ​ ω KKS \omega_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+\varepsilon\,\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}} being the canonical plus Kirillov-Kostant-Souriau (KKS) forms, we build two canonical and commuting families of polynomial first integrals: one pulled back from the Lie algebra 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} of G G via the magnetic moment map P P , and one pulled back from a Ad ⁡ ( A ) \mathrm{Ad}(A) -invariant affine slice of 𝔪 ≅ T e ​ A ​ M \mathfrak{m}\cong T_{eA}M , where e ​ A eA is the identity of G / A G/A . Their common image generates a reduced Poisson algebra obtained from a fiber tensor product, and the natural multiplication map into a Poisson subalgebra of polynomial functions 𝒪 ⁡ ( T ∗ ​ M ) ⊂ C ∞ ​ ( T ∗ ​ M ) \mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)\subset C^{\infty}(T^{*}M) is Poisson and injective. The center of this fiber tensor product is contained in the Poisson center of the symmetric algebra of 𝔤 \mathfrak{g} . In a dense regular locus, the resulting projection chain realises a superintegrable system. As examples, two SU ⁡ ( 3 ) \mathrm{SU}(3) cases are studied (regular torus and irregular S ⁡ ( U ⁡ ( 2 ) × U ⁡ ( 1 ) ) \mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1)) quotients), which illustrate the construction and produce explicit action-angle coordinates.

1.
Introduction

Integrable and superintegrable systems play an important role and have broad applications in mathematical physics and symplectic geometry
34
;
41
;
22
;
37
;
28
;
21
;
44
;
38
. A classical Hamiltonian system with
n
degrees of freedom is said to be completely integrable if it admits
n
independent first integrals (a.k.a integrals of motion, conserved quantities) in involution, and
superintegrable
if it possesses more than
n
integrals of motion (typically up to
2n-1
integrals in a
2n
-dimensional phase space). In particular, a maximally superintegrable system has
2n-1
independent integrals, which implies that all trajectories are closed
41
;
28
;
27
. Based on the extension of the integrals, superintegrability involves a range of other cases (based on the integrable case), from minimally superintegrable (
n+1
) to quasi maximally superintegrable (
2n-2
). These cases appear in the context of the study of superintegrable systems, usually using explicit differential operator realizations in classical and quantum mechanics. The modern study of integrable Hamiltonian systems was initiated by Liouville and is characterised by the Liouville-Arnold theorem. The notion of noncommutative integrability, as formalized by Nekhoroshev
37
, extends the Liouville-Arnold theorem to the noncommutative case and provides geometric descriptions of how superintegrabilities are realized in symplectic manifolds. A completely integrable system consists of
n
functionally independent conserved quantities on a
2n
-dimensional symplectic manifold and yields action-angle coordinates
37
;
1
. Moreover, in the 1970s, Mishchenko and Fomenko introduced the argument shift method in the study of Euler equations on Lie algebras, constructing a large Poisson-commutative algebra of integrals from invariant polynomials
35
. Their pioneering work established the complete integrability (in a generalized, noncommutative sense) of the geodesic flow in compact symmetric spaces by exhibiting sufficiently many conserved quantities. Independently, Thimm developed a complementary method based on chains of subalgebras and proved that invariant geodesic flows in certain homogeneous spaces are completely integrable
43
.

Recently, additional developments have advanced in the construction of superintegrable systems using both algebraic and geometric approaches. For instance, the Gelfand-Cetlin system, introduced by Guillemin and Sternberg
24
, provides a concrete family of conserved quantities on coadjoint orbits (e.g., flag manifolds) of unitary groups, thereby demonstrating integrability in those cases. These works illustrate how algebraic and geometric techniques (such as exploiting Lie algebra invariants or moment maps along a chain of subgroups) can lead to obtaining explicit first integrals for geodesic flows. These first integrals are usually polynomials in terms of the basis of Lie algebras and need not all Poisson-commute, provided that they generate a commutative subalgebra of sufficiently large dimension. This broader integrability context has been employed in different frameworks. For example, Efimov
20
established the noncommutative integrability of certain magnetic geodesic flows on homogeneous symplectic manifolds, and Reshetikhin
41
formalized the construction to implement the Poisson projection chains and provided the equivalent relations between two superintegrable systems. More recently, novel superintegrable systems have been developed on moduli spaces and in other Lie-theoretic contexts
2
, demonstrating a growing interest in integrable structures associated with symmetric and homogeneous spaces.

In this paper, we give a way to constructing superintegrable geodesic flows on compact symplectic homogeneous manifolds. In particular, we focus on reductive homogeneous spaces
M=G/A
with
G
a compact, simply connected, and semisimple Lie group, and
A
a closed subgroup equipped with twisted symplectic structures, such as those arising from the canonical symplectic form in
T^{*}M
and the Kirillov-Kostant-Souriau (KKS) form in coadjoint orbits. Using invariant polynomial functions in the associated Lie algebra and their Poisson properties, we explicitly build a set of first integrals for the magnetic geodesic flow on
M
. The non-commutative integrability was first proved by Efimov in
20
, where the explicit expression of Hamiltonian vector fields is given with respect to Hamiltonians and first integrals. Also, in
5
, Bolsinov and Jovanović extended Efimov’s result to the homogeneous space with a compact Lie group (not necessarily being semisimple) and applied this result to the magnetic pendulum. The novelty in our approach is that we keep track of these integrals in the algebraic category and then provide a geometric interpretation in terms of a Poisson projection chain: the polynomial integrals generated by the two blocks are organized as the coordinate ring of a reduced fiber product over their common central subalgebra
R_{0}
. This produces a canonical Poisson projection chain through
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}
and makes the rank decomposition and generic isotropic torus fibrations accessible.
Our construction transforms a noncommutative integrable system in the Mishchenko-Fomenko sense into Poisson projection chains. This offers a systematic approach to building superintegrability in a wide class of geometrically defined dynamical systems.

The structure of the paper is as follows: we begin in Section
2
by reviewing the necessary background on superintegrable systems in symplectic manifolds and setting up the notation. Key definitions and classical results (e.g., Liouville integrability and the Mishchenko-Fomenko theorem) are summarized to provide a foundation for our work. Later in Section
3
, we provide the superintegrability on semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and summarize it in the following theorem:

Theorem
.

(Theorem
3.18
) Let
G
be a semisimple and connected Lie group over a field
\mathbb{R}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and let
T\subset G
be a maximal torus of dimension
\dim T=r
.
Let
S(\mathfrak{g})
be the algebra of polynomial functions on the dual space
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
and
S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
be the subalgebras of
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant functions and
\mathrm{Ad}(T)
-invariant functions, respectively.
Then the inclusion of Poisson algebras

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})

makes the chain of affine Poisson varieties

(1.1)

\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\,\chi_{T}\,}\mathfrak{g}//T\xrightarrow{\,\rho\,}\mathfrak{g}//G

a superintegrable system. More precisely:

(i)

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset\mathcal{Z}\big(S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\big),

(ii)

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}=r,\qquad\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}=\dim\mathfrak{g}-r,

(iii)

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})=\bigl(\dim\mathfrak{g}-r\bigr)+r,

where
\mathrm{trdeg}
denotes the transcendence degree of an algebra.

In Section
4
, we present a uniform construction of superintegrable geodesic and magnetic geodesic flows on compact homogeneous symplectic manifolds
M=G/A
with
A=\exp(\ker\mathrm{ad}(W))
for a fixed
W\in\mathfrak{g}
. Working on the twisted cotangent bundle
(T^{*}M,\omega_{\varepsilon})
with
\omega_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+\varepsilon\,\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
, we produce two canonical polynomial families of the first integrals:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=P^{*}S(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A},

where
P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(X-\varepsilon W)
is the magnetic moment map, and
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X)=X-\varepsilon W
is the affine fiber map. Here
(g,X)
is an arbitrary point in
T^{*}M
. The mixed brackets vanish,
P^{*}
is Poisson and
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}
is anti-Poisson, so all bracket computations reduce to Lie-Poisson brackets on
\mathfrak{g}
and on the
A
-invariant affine slice. The Poisson commutative base algebra

\displaystyle R_{0}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}\big(\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{Res}_{W})\big)

controls the intersection of two algebras, where
\mathrm{Res}_{W}:S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
is a Poisson algebra homomorphism. We then show the fiber tensor product
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cong\textbf{Alg}\left\langle F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cup F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right\rangle
, which is a reduced Poisson algebra, and obtain a natural Poisson projection chain in the following theorem:

Theorem
.

(Theorem
4.65
) Let
G
be a compact, simply-connected, semisimple Lie group, and let
A=\exp(\ker\mathrm{ad}(W))\supseteq T
. Suppose that
(T^{*}M,\omega_{\varepsilon})
is the twisted symplectic manifold. On the Zariski open dense regular locus
U\subset T^{*}M
, we then have the following arguments:

(i) if
W
is irregular and
T\subsetneq A
. Then the system of the Poisson chain

(1.2)

\displaystyle T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\times_{R_{0}}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//A\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathrm{Spec}R_{0}

is superintegrable.

(ii) If
W
is regular, then
R_{0}=P^{*}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\right)
and

(1.3)

\displaystyle T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathfrak{g}\times_{\mathfrak{g}//G}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//T\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathfrak{g}//G

is superintegrable.

Then the equivalent relations between the superintegrable chains (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) are also provided. Then, in Section
5
, we focus on concrete examples: we consider the reductive homogeneous spaces
\mathrm{SU}(3)/T
and
\mathrm{SU}(3)/\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1))
and construct an explicit superintegrable system on these spaces. In particular, we compute a complete family of independent integrals in involution for the magnetic geodesic flow on
\mathrm{SU}(3)/T
, illustrating our algebraic construction in a low-dimensional but nontrivial setting. We also examine the role of regular vs. irregular elements in the (super)integrability of these
\mathrm{SU}(3)
examples. Finally, Section
6
provides some concluding remarks and suggests directions for future research.

2.
Preliminary

We begin with some basic terminology from symplectic geometry. For more detailed information, we refer the reader to
31
. Let
(M,\omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional smooth manifold equipped with a symplectic form
\omega
. For any
f
in the algebra
C^{\infty}(M)
of smooth functions on
(M,\omega)
, there exists a unique Hamiltonian vector field
X_{f}
defined by

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{f}}\omega:=\omega(X_{f},-)=df.

Throughout this paper, the Hamiltonian vector fields are assumed to be complete.

For any
f,g\in C^{\infty}(M)
, the natural Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\omega}:C^{\infty}(M)\times C^{\infty}(M)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
on
M
is defined via their associated Hamiltonian vector fields by

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{\omega}=\omega(X_{f},X_{g}).

We call a smooth function
g
a
first integral
(a.k.a, integral of motion, conserved quantity, constant of motion) of a given smooth function
f
if the two functions Poisson commute, i.e.,
\{f,g\}_{\omega}=0
.

From Hamiltonian mechanics, first integrals of
f
are invariant along the integral curves of
X_{f}
, i.e.,
X_{f}(g)=\{f,g\}=0
for any first integral
g
. On the other hand, the first integrals provide the symmetries of the Hamiltonian flow (geodesic flow) generated by
f
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra over
\mathbb{R}
with a non-trivial commutator
[\cdot,\cdot]
, and let
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be its dual, which admits the classical Lie-Poisson bracket: for any
f,g\in C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\{f,g\}(\xi)=\langle\xi,[d_{\xi}f,d_{\xi}g]\rangle,

where
d_{\xi}f,d_{\xi}g\in T_{\xi}^{*}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cong\left(\mathfrak{g}^{*}\right)^{*}\cong\mathfrak{g}
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the dual pair between
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{g}
. We refer the reader to
(
31
, Chapter 7)
for details.

Suppose that

(i) the Poisson algebra
C^{\infty}(M)
has a Poisson subalgebra
\mathcal{A}\subset C^{\infty}(M)
of rank (transcendence degree)
2n-k
for some integer
k
with
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
;

(ii) the Poisson center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
of
\mathcal{A}
is of rank
k
.

Smooth functions in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
are called Hamiltonian, which are smooth functions Poisson-commuting with first integrals.

We now provide a geometric definition of a Hamiltonian dynamical system as being superintegrable. For more details, the reader can refer to
41
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional connected symplectic manifold. A
superintegrable system
on
(M,\omega)
is a sequence consisting of a triple
(M,\mathcal{P}_{2n-k},\mathcal{B}_{k})
of Poisson manifolds with two Poisson maps
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}

(2.2)

\displaystyle M\xrightarrow{\;\pi_{1}\;}\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}\xrightarrow{\;\pi_{2}\;}\mathcal{B}_{k}

such that the following holds:

(i) The Poisson maps
\pi_{i}
are submersions with connected fibers of constant (generic) dimensions
k
and
2n-2k
, respectively,
1\leq k\leq n

(ii) For a generic
b\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
,
\pi_{2}^{-1}(b)
is a union of symplectic leaves in
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
.

(iii)
\dim\mathcal{P}+\dim\mathcal{B}_{k}=2n
.

Remark 2.2
.

In addition to the structures introduced in Definition
2.1
, we define

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle:=\pi_{1}^{*}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2n-k})\ \subset\ C^{\infty}(M),\qquad I:=(\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1})^{*}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{k})\ \subset\ C^{\infty}(M).

That is,
J
is a Poisson algebra of rank
2n-k
and consists of all smooth functions on
M
obtained as pullbacks of smooth functions on
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
along
\pi_{1}
, while
I
is a Poisson algebra of rank
k
formed by pullbacks of smooth functions on
\mathcal{B}_{k}
via the composition
\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1}
.
We define the Poisson centralisers of these integrals in
C^{\infty}(M)
by

\displaystyle C_{I}(M):=\bigl\{f\in C^{\infty}(M):\{f,\phi\}=0\ \text{ for all }\phi\in I\bigr\},

\displaystyle C_{J}(M):=\bigl\{f\in C^{\infty}(M):\{f,\psi\}=0\ \text{ for all }\psi\in J\bigr\}.

Since
\pi_{1}
is Poisson and every component of
\pi_{2}
is a Casimir on
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
, we have the inclusions as follows:

\displaystyle J\subset C_{I}(M)\quad\text{and}\quad I\subset C_{J}(M).

Indeed, for
h\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2n-k})
and
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{B}_{k})
,

\displaystyle\bigl\{\pi_{1}^{*}h,(\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1})^{*}g\bigr\}_{M}=\pi_{1}^{*}\bigl\{h,\pi_{2}^{*}g\bigr\}_{\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}}=0.

Consequently,
I
and
J
form the two mutually Poisson-commuting blocks of a superintegrable system in the standard sense: the integrals in
J
(called integrals of motion) specify the first projection
\pi_{1}
, while the integral
I
(called Hamiltonian) further refines the symplectic foliation of
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
, decomposing it into the connected fibers associated with the second projection
\pi_{2}
.

We will, in Subsection
3
, introduce an algebraic way to define a superintegrable system based on the Poisson centralisers defined in Remark
2.2
.

Remark 2.3
.

We now look at the regular fibers of
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
.

(i) First of all, we describe the generic fibers in
M
. Let
c\in\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
be a regular value of
\pi_{1}
, and choose a local coordinate
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{2n-k})
on
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
such that
J=(J_{1},\ldots,J_{2n-k}):M\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n-k}
with
J_{j}=f_{j}\circ\pi_{1}
. Define the fiber by

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{(c_{1},\ldots,c_{2n-k})}:=\pi_{1}^{-1}(c)=\{m\in M:\text{ }J_{j}(m)=f_{j}(c),\text{ }1\leq j\leq 2n-k\},

which is a
k
-dimensional manifold with
dJ_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dJ_{2n-k}\neq 0
. Let
b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})=\pi_{2}^{-1}(c)
, and choose local coordinates
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})
on
\mathcal{B}_{k}
and set
I_{j}:=g_{j}\circ\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1}\in C_{J}(M)
. Then the Hamiltonian vector fields
X_{I_{1}},\ldots,X_{I_{k}}
are tangent to
\mathcal{L}_{(c_{1},\ldots,c_{2n-k})}
and span
T\mathcal{L}_{(c_{1},\ldots,c_{2n-k})}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{(c_{1},\ldots,c_{2n-k})}
is isotropic. From
36
, on each connected component of a generic fiber, these flows give a canonical affine structure, then
\mathcal{L}_{(c_{1},\ldots,c_{2n-k})}\cong\mathbb{R}^{k-l}\times(S^{1})^{l}
with
0\leq l\leq k
is a diffeomorphism.

(ii) Now, we focus on the generic fibers in
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
. The Poisson manifold
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
has corank
k
, and the map
\pi_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{k}
is Poisson. Similarly to the above analysis, for each
H_{i}:=g_{i}\circ\pi_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{P}_{2n-k})
and any regular
b\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
, we have

(2.4)

\displaystyle\pi_{2}^{-1}(b):=\left\{m_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}:H_{i}(m_{0})=g_{i}(b),\text{ }1\leq i\leq k\right\}.

These are level surfaces determined by the center functions in
C_{I}(M)
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{b,i}\subset\pi_{2}^{-1}(b)
be the connected component. The Poisson structure
\pi_{i}
on
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
is of rank
k
in each
\mathcal{L}_{b,i},
that makes
\mathcal{L}_{b,i}
a symplectic manifold of dimension
\dim\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}-\dim\mathcal{B}_{k}=2n-2k
. Hence, the fibers of
\pi_{2}
form a foliation of
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
such that they partition the regular locus
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
into disjoint submanifolds, each of which being a level surface of the Hamiltonians. That is,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}\right)_{\mathrm{reg}}:=\pi_{2}^{-1}\left((\mathcal{B}_{k})_{\mathrm{reg}}\right)=\bigsqcup_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\pi_{2}^{-1}(b)=\bigsqcup_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\bigsqcup_{i}\mathfrak{L}_{b,i}.

Another important feature of a superintegrable system is that it admits action-angle coordinates.

Definition 2.4
.

41

Let
(M,\omega)
be a symplectic manifold that admits a superintegrable system (
2.2
) with a Poisson structure
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\omega}
. Let
c\in\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
be a regular value of
\pi_{1}
and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{c}=\pi_{1}^{-1}(c)

be a connected and compact fiber defined in (
2.3
). Set
k:=\mathrm{rank}\,C_{I}(M)=\dim\mathcal{B}_{k}
, and choose generators

\displaystyle I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\in C_{I}(M)\quad\text{such that }\{I_{i},F\}_{\omega}=0\text{ for all }F\in C_{J}(M).

Then we have the following arguments:

(i) The Hamiltonian vector fields
X_{I_{1}},\dots,X_{I_{k}}
are tangent to
\mathcal{L}_{c}
and mutually commute. The fiber
\mathcal{L}_{c}
is an isotropic
k
-torus
T^{k}
.

(ii) There exist
S^{1}
-valued functions
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k}
defined in a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{L}_{c}
in
M
such that, on
\mathcal{L}_{c}
,

\displaystyle\{\phi_{i},\phi_{j}\}_{\omega}=\{I_{i},I_{j}\}_{\omega}=0,\qquad\{\phi_{i},I_{j}\}_{\omega}=\delta_{ij},

Equivalently, angle one-forms
d\phi_{j}
satisfy
d\phi_{j}(X_{I_{i}})=\delta_{ji}
on
\mathcal{L}_{c}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
.

(iii) In a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{L}_{c}
, there are local coordinates

\displaystyle(I_{1},\dots,I_{k};\ \phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k};\ y_{1},\dots,y_{2n-2k})

such that the symplectic form splits into:

\displaystyle\omega=\pi_{1}^{*}(\omega_{\mathrm{red}})\ +\ \sum_{j=1}^{k}dI_{j}\wedge d\phi_{j},

where
\omega_{\mathrm{red}}
is a symplectic form on the
\pi_{2}
-leaf through
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{c})
. In particular, the pairs
(I_{j},\phi_{j})
are canonical.

As examples, we will provide detailed constructions on how the action-angle coordinates appearing in the superintegrable systems on the phase space
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)
and
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/A)
are presented in Section
5
.

To conclude this paper, for any two superintegrable systems, we have the following way to determine whether they are equivalent or not.

Definition 2.5
.

41

Let
(M,\omega)
and
(M^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
be symplectic manifolds, and let

(2.6)

\displaystyle M\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{1}\ }\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{2}\ }\mathcal{B}_{k}\ \text{ and }\ M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}^{\prime}}\mathcal{P}_{2n-k^{\prime}}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}

be superintegrable systems on
M
and
M^{\prime}
, respectively. Two such systems (
2.6
) are
spectrally equivalent
if there exist Poisson maps

\displaystyle\phi:M\rightarrow M^{\prime},\qquad\phi_{1}:\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{2n-k^{\prime}}^{\prime},\qquad\phi_{2}:\mathcal{B}_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}

with
\phi_{2}
a diffeomorphism, making the diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{P}_{2n-k^{\prime}}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}_{k^{\prime}}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowright}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowright}

with the following commutative relations:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}^{\prime}\circ\phi=\phi_{1}\circ\pi_{1},\qquad\pi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}\circ\pi_{2}.

They are
equivalent
if, in addition,
\phi
and
\phi_{1}
are diffeomorphisms.

Remark 2.6
.

Note that in Definition
2.5
, we only use the Poisson brackets on
M,\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B}
and the commutative diagram, while the symplectic forms
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
are not implemented. Hence, we are able to extend the notion of spectral equivalence to Poisson manifolds by replacing
(M,\omega)
and
(M^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
with Poisson manifolds
(M,\{\cdot,\cdot\})
and
(M^{\prime},\{\cdot,\cdot\}^{\prime})
, and requiring that
\varphi,\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
be Poisson maps, with
\varphi_{2}
being a (diffeomorphic) Poisson isomorphism.

In Sections
3
and
4
, we will apply this terminology when intermediate spaces
\mathfrak{g}//A=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
are affine Poisson varieties which may be singular. In that case, all statements remain valid after restriction to the corresponding smooth (regular) loci. See the detailed applications in Section
4
. Indeed, we replace each singular Poisson variety
X
with its smooth locus
X_{\mathrm{reg}}
and restrict all maps accordingly, such that the diagram becomes a diagram of smooth Poisson manifolds, and submersion is interpreted in the usual differential-geometric sense on these loci.

3.
Superintegrable systems on
S(\mathfrak{g})

In Section
2
, we provided a generic definition of superintegrability on symplectic manifolds. In Section
3
, we will examine the construction of a superintegrable system on the Poisson manifold
S(\mathfrak{g})
. This section is divided into three distinct subsections, each addressing foundational terminology and the properties of the system. In Subsection
3.1
, we define the Poisson centraliser with respect to the subalgebra chain
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, which is also known as
the commutant
in the physics literature. See, for instance,
13
. The corresponding algebraic superintegrable system is also defined. Then, in Subsection
3.2
, we will provide the spectrum of these algebras, which illustrates the geometric interpretation of the algebraic superintegrable system. We see that such constructions lead to Poisson submersion chains as defined in (
2.1
). Finally, in Subsection
3.3
, we describe the symplectic leaves in the intermediate Poisson manifold of the corresponding superintegrable chain constructed in Subsection
3.2
.

3.1.
Poisson centraliser
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}

In this Subsection
3.1
, we further assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is an
n
-dimensional semisimple Lie algebra over
\mathbb{R}
. Suppose that
\beta_{\mathfrak{g}}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
is an ordered basis for
\mathfrak{g}
, which satisfies the relations

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}C_{ij}^{k}X_{k}

for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
, where
C_{ij}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}
are the structure constants of
\mathfrak{g}
. We now provide the coordinate representation of the Lie-Poisson bracket (
2.1
) in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Define the corresponding coordinate functions
\big(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\big)
on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by
x_{i}(\xi)=\langle\xi,X_{i}\rangle
with
i=1,\ldots,n
. For any coordinate functions
x_{i},x_{j}
, using (
2.1
), one has

(3.1)

\{x_{i},x_{j}\}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}C_{ij}^{k}x_{k}\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq n.

Choosing the canonical linear embedding of
\mathfrak{g}
into the algebra of polynomial functions on its dual space
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, we obtain a canonical algebra isomorphism
S(\mathfrak{g})\cong\mathbb{R}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]:=\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, where the coordinate functions
x_{j}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
correspond to the basis elements of
\mathfrak{g}
. In the rest of this paper, we work on the Poisson polynomial ring
\mathbb{R}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
. Hence, in the following, each
p\in S(\mathfrak{g})
is a polynomial function from
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
to
\mathbb{R}
. For any
p,q\in S(\mathfrak{g}),
a Lie-Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}:S(\mathfrak{g})\times S(\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{g})
is defined by

(3.2)

\{p,q\}=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}C_{ij}^{k}x_{k}\dfrac{\partial p}{\partial x_{i}}\dfrac{\partial q}{\partial x_{j}}.

We now construct the invariant symmetric algebra for an arbitrary subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that the basis of
\mathfrak{a}
is given by
X_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,X_{\ell_{s}}
, where
s=\dim\mathfrak{a}
and
\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{s}\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We now examine the adjoint action of the subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
on
S(\mathfrak{g})
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra with its dual
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and let
\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie subalgebra. Then, for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, the infinitesimal coadjoint representation of
\mathfrak{a}
on
S(\mathfrak{g})
, defined by

\displaystyle(X\cdot p)(\xi)=\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}p(\mathrm{Ad}^{*}(\exp(tX))\xi),\quad X\in\mathfrak{a},\,p\in S(\mathfrak{g})

acts by derivations.That is, for all
X_{m}\in\mathfrak{a}
and
p,q\in S(\mathfrak{g})
,

\displaystyle X_{m}\cdot\{p,q\}=\{X_{m}\cdot p,q\}+\{p,X_{m}\cdot q\},\quad m=\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{s}

Remark 3.2
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}
acts infinitesimally on
S(\mathfrak{g})
by defining the following vector fields:

(3.3)

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{m}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}C_{mk}^{l}\,x_{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}},\quad m=\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s}..

According to Definition
3.1
, and its remark, our primary focus is to explore the kernel of the coadjoint action of
\mathfrak{a}
on
S(\mathfrak{g})
.

Definition 3.3
.

The
commutant
(a.k.a,
centraliser subalgebra
)
S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}
is defined as the centraliser of
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
in
S(\mathfrak{g})
:

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}=

\displaystyle\left\{p\in S(\mathfrak{g}):\text{ }\{x,p\}=0\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}\right\}.

Remark 3.4
.

(i) The
Poisson center
of
\left(S(\mathfrak{g}),\{\cdot,\cdot\}\right)
is the set of all
\mathfrak{g}
-invariant polynomials, i.e.,

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{g}}=\left\{p\in S(\mathfrak{g}):\text{ }\{p,x\}=0\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\right\}.

These elements can be identified with the (polynomial) kernels of the differential operators in (
3.3
) (see
14
;
18
for details). These are the classical Casimir polynomials.

(ii) We note that for any finite-dimensional Lie algebra,
S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}
is not necessarily finitely generated. However, if
\mathfrak{g}
is semisimple or reductive (note that in this manuscript, we assume that
\mathfrak{g}
is semisimple), it can be shown that
S(\mathfrak{g})
is Noetherian
(
18
, Chapter 2)
. Since
A
is assumed to be a reductive subgroup of
G
, the invariant Poisson subalgebra
S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}
is also finitely-generated by the Hilbert-Nagata theorem
(
19
, Theorem 6.1)
. This implies that, once the set consisting of all the indecomposable polynomials
\left\{p^{(k_{1})},\dots,p^{(k_{n})}\right\}
has been constructed via the polynomial ansatz provided later, there always exists some integer
l\in\mathbb{N}
such that
p^{(l+k_{j})}
is decomposable for all
j\geq 1
. We say that a polynomial
p\in S(\mathfrak{g})
is decomposable if there exists another polynomial
p^{\prime}\in S(\mathfrak{g})
of a lower degree such that
p^{\prime}
is a divisor of
p
. Additionally, it is important to note that the elements in the generating set within the centraliser of a subalgebra do not necessarily imply their algebraic independence. See further explanation in Remark
3.9
.

Recall that
S(\mathfrak{g})\cong\mathbb{R}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
. Under this explicit isomorphism,
S(\mathfrak{g})
inherits the standard grading by polynomial degree

(3.4)

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}S^{k}(\mathfrak{g}),

where

(3.5)

\displaystyle S^{k}(\mathfrak{g}):=\mathrm{span}\left\{x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}:a_{1}+\ldots+a_{n}=k,\quad a_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}\right\}

is a subalgebra of
S(\mathfrak{g})
consisting of all homogeneous degree
k
polynomials. It follows that, for any
p\in S(\mathfrak{g})
, the polynomial decomposes as
p=\sum_{k\geq 0}p^{(k)}
, where
p^{(k)}\in S^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
for all
k\geq 0
. We now focus on the
\mathfrak{a}
-invariant homogeneous polynomial subspaces of
\left(S(\mathfrak{g}),\{\cdot,\cdot\}\right)
. For similar constructions, we refer to
4
;
17
;
32
and the citations therein. Define the vector space of
\mathfrak{a}
-invariant degree
k
-homogeneous polynomials as

\displaystyle S^{k}(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}=\left\{p^{(k)}\in S^{k}(\mathfrak{g}):\left\{x,p^{(k)}\right\}=0\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}\right\},

where
p^{(k)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k\geq 0
with the generic form

(3.6)

\displaystyle p^{(k)}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\sum_{i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n}=k}\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\,x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{i_{n}},\qquad\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\in\mathbb{R}.

To find a finite generating set for centraliser subalgebras, all
\mathfrak{a}
-invariant linearly independent and indecomposable homogeneous polynomial solutions of the system of partial differential equations (PDEs)

(3.7)

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{m}\left(p^{(k)}\right)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\left\{x_{m},p^{(k)}\right\}=\sum_{1\leq l,i\leq n}C_{mi}^{l}x_{l}\dfrac{\partial p^{(k)}}{\partial x_{i}}=0,\text{ }\quad m=\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{s}

must be found.

Recall that if
A\leq G
is any Lie subgroup acting on the coadjoint representation, under a fixed
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant bilinear form
B
with
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{g}
, we define:

(3.8)

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}:=\bigl\{f\in S(\mathfrak{g}):f(A\cdot X)=f(X),\text{ for any }X\in\mathfrak{g}\bigr\},

which is also a Poisson subalgebra: Indeed,
\{f,g\}
is
A
-invariant when
f,g
are
A
-invariant. Throughout this work, when we mention
A
-invariant, we refer to
\mathrm{Ad}(A)
-invariant. From the preceding discussion, we can directly state the following proposition. It demonstrates that determining a generating set for
S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}
is, in fact, equivalent to performing computations within the Poisson algebra
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
G
be a
n
-dimensional semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with a Killing form
B
. Let
A\subset G
be a connected closed subgroup with a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. Choose the basis of
\mathfrak{a}
to be
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{s}\}
for some
s\leq n
. For any
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})
, define the adjoint action of
A
on polynomials by

\displaystyle(a\cdot f)(X):=f\big(aXa^{-1}\big),\qquad a\in A.

Then for all
X\in\mathfrak{g},\ H\in\mathfrak{a}
and
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
, the following arguments are equivalent:

(i)

\displaystyle df_{X}\big([H,X]\big)=0\ ;

(ii)

\displaystyle\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}c_{jk}^{i}x_{k}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}=0\ \ \text{for each }j=1,\dots,s;

(iii)

\displaystyle B\big(\nabla f(X),[H,X]\big)=0\ ,

where
\nabla f
is the gradient characterised by
df_{X}(V)=B(\nabla f(X),V)
, and for all
V\in\mathfrak{g}
and
c_{jk}^{i}
are structure tensors. Equivalently, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{jk}^{i}\,x_{k}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\quad j=1,\dots,s,

we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}=S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{s}\ker(\mathcal{L}_{j}).

Proof.

For any
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
, by definition in (
3.8
),
f(\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X)=f(a\cdot X)=f(X)
. For
H\in\mathfrak{a}
and the one-parameter subgroup
a(t)=\exp(tH)
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we now consider the flow generated by
H
as follows,

\displaystyle 0=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}(a(t)\cdot f)(X)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}f\big(\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(-tH))X\big)=-df_{X}\big([H,X]\big).

Conversely, let
a\in A
and choose any smooth path
h:[0,1]\to A
with
h(0)=e
and
h(1)=a
, where
e\in A
is the identity element. Define the flow
\Phi(t):=f(\mathrm{Ad}(h(t)^{-1})X)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then using chain rules, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(t)=-df_{\Phi(t)}\Big(\left[\dot{h}(t)\,h(t)^{-1},\,\mathrm{Ad}(h(t)^{-1})X\right]\Big)=0,

since
\dot{h}(t)h(t)^{-1}\in\mathfrak{a}
and using condition (i) for all
H\in\mathfrak{a}
. Hence,
\Phi
is constant, and
f(\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X)=f(X)
, i.e.,
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
.

Now, suppose that (i) holds. By the definition of the
B
-gradient, we have
df_{X}([H,X])=B(\nabla f(X),

[H,X])=0
. Thus, (iii) is concluded. Finally, we show that
\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}c_{jk}^{i}x_{k}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}=0
. For any
H\in\mathfrak{a}
, write
H=\sum_{j=1}^{s}h_{j}E_{j}
and
X=\sum_{k=1}^{n}x_{k}E_{k}
. Then a direct computation gives

\displaystyle[H,X]=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\sum_{k=1}^{n}h_{j}c_{jk}^{i}x_{k}X_{i},\qquad\nabla f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}\,X_{i},

and the coefficients
h_{j}
are arbitrary, so each PDE displayed must vanish. Hence,

\displaystyle df_{X}([H,X])=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{s,n}c_{jk}^{i}h_{j}x_{k}\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}=\sum_{j}\mathcal{L}_{j}(f)=0.

This gives (
3.9
), and the proof is completed.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

(i) In the case where
A
is not connected. Let
A^{\circ}
be the identity component of
A
. The same argument shows
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A^{\circ}}=S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}=S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
, and hence

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}=\bigl(S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}\bigr)^{\,A/A^{\circ}}.

(ii) Note that it is shown that if
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{g}
, the maximal number of functionally independent solutions of
(
3.7
)
is known to be given by
40
;
39

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{g})=\dim\mathfrak{g}-\mathrm{rank}(A_{ij})_{n\times n}\,,\text{ }

where
A_{ij}:=\sum_{l=1}^{n}C_{ij}^{l}x_{l}
represents the matrix of the commutator table of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
over the given basis. Note that (
3.10
) still holds if
\mathfrak{a}
is an Abelian subalgebra. In this context, since
\mathfrak{a}
is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
, we will consider the labelling problem where functions (not necessarily polynomials) satisfy the system of PDEs (
3.7
). It can be shown that the number of functionally independent solutions in the system (
3.7
) is exactly

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathfrak{a})=\dim\mathfrak{g}-\dim\mathfrak{a}+\ell_{0},

where
\ell_{0}
is the number of
\mathfrak{g}
-invariant polynomials in
S(\mathfrak{a})
(for more details, see
15
;
6
and
(
9
, Chapter 12, §12.1.5)
and references therein).

Finally, we focus on the rank of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
. Recall that for a finitely-generated integral domain
D
over the base field
\mathbb{R}
, the rank of
D
is the transcendence degree defined by

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}D:=\mathrm{trdeg}D.

Throughout this paper, the rank of a finitely-generated algebra is defined in (
3.12
).

Typically, finding a polynomial for the centraliser in relation to a subalgebra can be approached by two methods: solving PDEs systems
(
3.7
)
directly, using the method of characteristics, or employing a polynomial ansatz. In this case, we apply the polynomial ansatz, as
\mathfrak{g}
is assumed to be semisimple, and the commutant can be expressed as a polynomial in dual space variables. This simplifies the analysis by solving sets of linear equations. Note that, in the case of non-semisimple Lie algebras, the solution may be expressed as rational or even transcendental functions. Recall that, by construction, an
\mathfrak{a}
-invariant homogeneous polynomial in
S^{k}(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}
takes the form of
\eqref{eq:ci}.
Subsequently, a list of all polynomials is compiled for each degree, and we examine the decomposability. That is, the degree up to which all polynomials can be expressed in terms of polynomials of lower degrees. Without loss of generality, if indecomposability is achieved up to the degree
\zeta
, the set of polynomials that form the commutant is described by

\textbf{q}_{1}:=\left\{p_{u}^{(1)},\quad u=1,...,l_{1}\right\};

\vdots

\textbf{q}_{\zeta}:=\left\{p_{u}^{(\zeta)},\quad u=1,...,l_{\zeta}\right\},

where
p_{u}^{(k)}
is an indecomposable
\mathfrak{a}
-invariant homogeneous polynomial of degree
k\in\{1,\ldots,\zeta\}
.

Let
\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}:=\left\{p_{u_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,p_{u_{\zeta}}^{(\zeta)}\right\}=\textbf{q}_{1}\sqcup\textbf{q}_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\textbf{q}_{\zeta}
be a finite set consisting of all indecomposable polynomials up to degree
\zeta
, and let
\textbf{Alg}\left\langle\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}\right\rangle
denote the algebra generated by the set
\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}
. It is clear that
\textbf{Alg}\langle\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}\rangle
is infinite-dimensional as a vector space. Notice that the elements in
\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}
are not necessarily algebraically independent. Hence, they are not freely generated, which means that there may exist non-trivial polynomial relations among these generators. Now, for any
p_{u}^{(l)}\in\textbf{q}_{l}
and
p_{v}^{(\ell)}\in\textbf{q}_{\ell}
, there exist some coefficients
\Gamma^{s_{1},...,s_{r}}_{uv}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the Lie-Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}:\textbf{Alg}\langle\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}\rangle\times\textbf{Alg}\langle\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}\rangle\rightarrow\textbf{Alg}\langle\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}\rangle
is given by

(3.13)

\left\{p_{u}^{(l)},p_{v}^{(\ell)}\right\}=\sum_{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{r}=\ell+l-1}\Gamma^{s_{1},...,s_{r}}_{uv}p_{s_{1}}^{(k_{1})}\cdots p_{s_{r}}^{(k_{r})}.

Here
\Gamma^{s_{1},...,s_{r}}_{uv}\in\mathbb{R}
and
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\leq\zeta.
Subsequently, the algebraic structure denoted by
\textbf{Alg}\left\langle\textbf{Q}_{\zeta}\right\rangle
, when equipped with the Lie-Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
, in conjunction with additional polynomial relations
P(\textbf{q}_{1},\ldots,\textbf{q}_{\zeta})=0
, constitutes a finitely-generated Poisson algebra. This algebraic framework is thus characteristic of Poisson algebras, ensuring that it is defined by a finite set of generators. We now provide a standard algebraic construction for
A
-invariant subalgebra in
S(\mathfrak{g})
. See, for instance,
(
33
, Chapter 2)
. We starting with the following definition.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}_{\zeta}=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{\zeta}\}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
be a finite set that generates
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
as a
\mathcal{Z}
-algebra, where
\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{Z}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)
is the Poisson center of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
. We write
\mathbf{Alg}\langle\mathbf{Q}_{\zeta}\rangle
for the
\mathcal{Z}
-subalgebra of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
generated by
p_{1},\dots,p_{\zeta}
. In particular,
\mathbf{Alg}\langle\mathbf{Q}_{\zeta}\rangle=S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}_{\zeta}=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{\zeta}\}
, and let
\mathbb{R}[a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}]
be the free commutative polynomial ring for the variables
a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}
. Define the evaluation homomorphism

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}:\mathbb{R}[a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}]\longrightarrow S(\mathfrak{g})^{A},\qquad\mathrm{ev}(a_{i})=p_{i}

for all
1\leq i\leq\zeta
. Then
\mathrm{ev}
is surjective and

\displaystyle I:=\ker(\mathrm{ev})=\big\{P(a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta})\in\mathbb{R}[a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}]:P(p_{1},\dots,p_{\zeta})=0\ \text{in }S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\big\}.

Consequently,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}[a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}]\big/I\cong S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{Q}_{\zeta}
generates
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
as a
\mathcal{Z}
-algebra by Definition
3.7
, every element of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
is a polynomial in
p_{i}
with
\mathcal{Z}
-coefficients. Hence,
\mathrm{ev}
is surjective. By definition, an element
P(a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta})\in\mathbb{R}[a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}]
lies in
\ker(\mathrm{ev})
if and only if
P(p_{1},\dots,p_{\zeta})=0
is in
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
as polynomial relations contained in
I
. We then show that (
3.14
) holds.

Since
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
is a Poisson subalgebra, for
i,j
, we choose polynomials
F_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}[a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}]
with
\{p_{i},p_{j}\}=F_{ij}(p_{1},\dots,p_{\zeta})\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
and define

\displaystyle\left\{a_{i}+I,a_{j}+I\right\}:=F_{ij}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta})+I,

extending by
\mathbb{R}
-bilinearity and the Leibniz rule. If
F_{ij}^{\prime}
is another choice with the same value as
(p_{1},\dots,p_{\zeta})
, then
F_{ij}-F_{ij}^{\prime}\in I
. Thus, the definition is independent of the choice and hence well-defined. By the First Isomorphism Theorem, there exists a canonical isomorphism of
\mathcal{Z}
-algebras such that
\mathbb{R}[a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}]\big/I\cong S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
. ∎

Remark 3.9
.

(i) Fix a finitely generating set
\mathbf{Q}_{\zeta}=\{p_{1},\dots,p_{\zeta}\}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
. For any
p_{j}\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
, if
S_{0}(\mathfrak{g})^{A}=\bigoplus_{j\geq 1}S_{j}(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
consists of non-constant polynomials (
\deg p>0
), then
p_{j}\notin(S_{0}(\mathfrak{g})^{A})^{2}
, i.e., the class of
p_{j}
is nonzero in the space of indecomposables

Q(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}):=S_{0}(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\big/\left(S_{0}(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)^{2},

where
\left(S_{0}(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)^{2}:=\left\{pq:p,q\in S_{0}(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right\}
is the ideal consisting of decomposable generators. The elements
p_{j}
need
not
be algebraically or functionally independent. They are simply the explicit generators inside
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
.

(ii) The
a_{i}
are indeterminates in the polynomial ring
\mathbb{R}[a_{1},\dots,a_{\zeta}]
. They serve only as formal variables for recording polynomial expressions and relations among
p_{j}
through the evaluation map
a_{i}\mapsto p_{i}
. In particular,
a_{i}
do not denote new invariants within
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
, and any functional dependence of
p_{j}
is simply reflected by nontrivial polynomial relations when substituted with
a_{i}\mapsto p_{i}
.

(iii) In general, a polynomial algebra is a special case of a finitely generated algebra, as it does not contain any polynomial relations. Although
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
may contain a polynomial relation, it is therefore not necessarily a polynomial ring. However, as we constructed above via polynomial ansatz, the Poisson bracket relations in
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
are closed in a polynomial way (
3.13
), and regarding whether or not it contains polynomial relations, we always call
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
a Poisson polynomial algebra. Hence, by a Poisson polynomial algebra, we mean a finitely generated Poisson
\mathbb{R}
-algebra whose underlying commutative algebra is a quotient of a polynomial ring by a Poisson ideal.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}
be the Casimir of
\mathfrak{g}
, where
r=\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{g}
. A Hamiltonian with respect to
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
is given by

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}^{t}\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{k}}+\sum_{i=1}^{t}\gamma_{i}C_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right),

where
1\leq i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\leq t
,
\Gamma_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}},\gamma_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{Z}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)
is the center of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
. Here
t=\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal{Z}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)
.

A superintegrable system can be naturally correlated with the Poisson centraliser
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
through the following considerations:

Definition 3.11
.

A Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
is
algebraic (super)integrable
if the (Poisson) center
\mathcal{Z}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)
contains at most
t
independent functions
c_{1},\ldots,c_{t}
with
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{Z}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)
such that
\mathrm{rank}\,S(\mathfrak{g})

=

\mathrm{rank}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
+
\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal{Z}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)
and the following algebraic inclusion holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right)\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\subset S(\mathfrak{g}).

Remark 3.12
.

Depending on the structure of the subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
,
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
can contain a sufficiently high number of functionally independent polynomials
p_{1},\dots,p_{w}
such that
\mathcal{S}
, consisting of a Hamiltonian and first integrals
\mathcal{H},p_{1},\dots,p_{w}
, forms a superintegrable system, where the maximum number
w=\mathrm{rank}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
of elements commutes with the algebraic Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
.

3.2.
Geometric interpretation on superintegrable systems in
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}

In Subsection
3.2
, we provide the geometric interpretation of the algebraic superintegrable systems defined in Definition
3.11
. The main terminologies we applied in this subsection can be found in
(
19
, Chapter 6)
or
7
. Recall that if
\mathfrak{g}
is connected and semisimple, then
S(\mathfrak{g})^{\mathfrak{a}}=S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
is a finitely generated Poisson algebra. Define

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{*}//A=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}.

In particular,

(3.17)

\displaystyle\dim\mathfrak{g}^{*}//A=\dim G-d_{A}=\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\text{ with }d_{A}={\mathrm{max}}_{X\in\mathfrak{g}}\left\{\dim(\mathrm{Ad}(A)\cdot X)\right\}.

Fixed a
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant inner product
B
, which gives an identification between
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. In what follows, we work on the variety
\mathfrak{g}//A
. By definition (
3.16
), the inclusion
\mathsf{i}_{A}:S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\hookrightarrow S(\mathfrak{g})
induces a unique morphism of the affine variety

(3.18)

\displaystyle\chi_{A}:\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}//A=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{A},\qquad X\longmapsto(f\mapsto f(X))

such that its pullback
\mathsf{i}_{A}^{*}
is equal to
\chi_{A}
for any
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
. It is clear that the quotient mapping in (
3.18
) is well-defined, as for any two points
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
, we have
\chi_{A}(X)=\chi_{A}(Y)
if and only if
f(X)=f(Y)
. In particular,
\chi_{A}
is a Poisson morphism.

An element in
\mathfrak{g}//A
can be written as

(3.19)

\displaystyle[X]_{A}:=\chi_{A}^{-1}\left(\chi_{A}(X)\right)=\left\{Y\in\mathfrak{g}:f(Y)=f(X),\text{ }f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\right\}.

More precisely, if
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
is generated by homogeneous polynomials
f_{1},\dots,f_{\zeta}
, consider the morphism
F:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\zeta}
given by
X\longmapsto(f_{1}(X),\ldots,f_{\zeta}(X))
. Recall that, in Proposition
3.8
, we have a surjection
\mathrm{ev}:\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{\zeta}]\twoheadrightarrow S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
with
I=\ker\mathrm{ev}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}//A\cong V(I)\subset\mathbb{A}^{\zeta}
is an affine subvariety, and
\chi_{A}:\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\ F\ }\mathbb{A}^{\zeta}\twoheadrightarrow V(I)
. Note that
\mathfrak{g}//A\cong\mathbb{A}^{\zeta}
holds if and only if all the generators of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
are algebraically independent. In particular, for all
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{A}(X)=\chi_{A}(Y)\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\big(f_{1}(X),\dots,f_{r}(X)\big)=\big(f_{1}(Y),\dots,f_{r}(Y)\big)\;\Longleftrightarrow\;f(X)=f(Y)

as
f
is a polynomial in
f_{i}
. For a semisimple or compact group
A
acting on affine varieties, each fiber of
\chi_{A}
contains a unique closed
A
-orbit, and
\chi_{A}
maps closed orbits bijectively onto
\mathfrak{g}//A
. If
A
is compact here, all
A
-orbits in
\mathfrak{g}
are closed, so
\chi_{A}(X)=\chi_{A}(Y)
if and only if
A\cdot X=A\cdot Y
.

Remark 3.13
.

The GIT quotient construction introduced above does not rely on Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}
. In general, suppose that
V
is a finite-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{R}
with a linear action of an algebraic group
A
, then
\mathbb{R}[V]=\mathrm{Sym}(V^{*})
and one defines the affine quotient
V//A:=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{R}[V]^{A})
. The quotient morphism
\chi_{A}:V\to V//A
is the unique map induced by the inclusion
\mathbb{R}[V]^{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}[V]
. Consequently, for any non-zero
v,w\in V
,
\chi_{A}(v)=\chi_{A}(w)
if and only if
f(v)=f(w)
for all
f\in\mathbb{R}[V]^{A}
, and the fiber
[v]_{A}:=\chi_{A}^{-1}(\chi_{A}(v))
is the corresponding invariant equivalence class.

Definition 3.14
.

(
25
, Chapter 1, §5)

Let
A
be a semisimple group acting linearly on
\mathfrak{g}
. We define the regular locus of the quotient morphism by

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}:=\{y\in\mathfrak{g}//A:\ \chi_{A}\text{ is smooth over }y\}.

Equivalently, for any
y\in(\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}
, there exists an open neighborhood
y\in U\subset\mathfrak{g}//A
such that
\chi_{A}^{-1}(U)\to U
is a smooth morphism.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}//A
, and
(\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}
be the same as defined above. Then
\mathfrak{g}//A
carries a unique Poisson structure for which
\chi_{A}
is a Poisson morphism. Moreover,
(\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}
is a Zariski open dense subset of
\mathfrak{g}//A
, and the restricted map

\displaystyle\chi_{A}:\ \chi_{A}^{-1}\big((\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}\big)\longrightarrow(\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}

is a smooth Poisson morphism. In particular, the induced map is a Poisson submersion of smooth manifolds.

Proof.

As
\mathfrak{g}
is a Poisson manifold, generic smoothness on
\mathfrak{g}//A
implies that there exists a Zariski open dense subset
U\subset\mathfrak{g}//A
such that
\chi_{A}^{-1}(U)\to U
is smooth. By definition,
U\subset(\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}
, hence
(\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}
is non-empty and open dense. Restricting
\chi_{A}
to
\chi_{A}^{-1}\big((\mathfrak{g}//A)_{\mathrm{reg}}\big)
yields a smooth Poisson morphism.
∎

We now provide a geometric interpretation of the definition of a superintegrable system in
S(\mathfrak{g})
, which can be seen as the combination of both Definition
2.1
and Definition
3.11
.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an affine Poisson variety with coordinate algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X})
. The inclusion chain of finitely generated Poisson subalgebras

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X})

defines a superintegrable system on
\mathcal{X}
if

(a)
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
. That is,
\mathcal{B}_{k}
is in the Poisson center of
\mathcal{A}
;  (b)
\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathcal{A}+\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathcal{B}=\dim\mathcal{X}
.

Equivalently, the induced chain of affine Poisson morphisms

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{\ \pi\ }\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\ \rho\ }\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{B}

has
\dim\mathcal{X}=\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}+\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{B}
.

Remark 3.17
.

Definition
3.16
illustrates an affine-algebraic expression: the chain
X\xrightarrow{\ \pi\ }\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\ \rho\ }\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{B}
is required to be a chain of Poisson morphisms (i.e., the pullbacks
\pi^{*},\rho^{*}
are Poisson algebra morphisms), together with the dimension identity

\displaystyle\dim X=\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}+\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{B}\ \Longleftrightarrow\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathcal{A}+\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathcal{B}=\dim X.

In particular,
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}
and
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{B}
are
not
assumed to be smooth.

We can recapture a Poisson submersion chain defined in Definition
2.1
by restricting to a Zariski open dense
regular locus
on which the relevant Jacobian ranks are maximal and constant. See Definition
3.14
. Concretely, if
f:X\to Y
is a morphism of varieties with
X
smooth, define the maximal-rank locus

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{reg}}(f):=\{x\in X:\ \mathrm{rank}(df_{x})\ \text{is maximal and constant}\}.

Thus,
X_{\mathrm{reg}}(f)
is Zariski open, since it is given by the non-vanishing of Jacobian minors. If
Y
is singular, we say that “
f
is a (Poisson) submersion” can be understood after restricting to the locus where
f
is smooth.

This is precisely the role of the regular loci used later in the paper:

•

In Section
3
, we restrict to the subset
(\mathfrak{g}//T)_{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\mathfrak{g}//T
, characterised by a trivial
T
-stabilizer and the maximal-rank condition for the quotient morphism
\chi_{T}
.

•

In Section
4
, we work on the Zariski open dense subset
U\subset T^{*}M
, where the ranks of
dP
and
d\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
may be assumed maximal (constant). Outside
U
, these ranks can drop.

On such regular loci, the induced maps in the corresponding chains become Poisson submersions between smooth Poisson manifolds, such that the geometric hypotheses of Definition
2.1
apply without modifying the algebraic superintegrability of Definition
3.16
as restricting to a Zariski open dense subset does not change the function-field
level relations.

Let
\chi_{A}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}//A
be the categorical quotient map defined above. Since
\chi_{A}
collapses each
A
-orbit, we have the fundamental identity
\dim\mathfrak{g}//A=\dim\mathfrak{g}-d_{A}
. By Definition
3.16
, to construct a superintegrable system, we seek a chain of affine Poisson varieties

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})\,\xrightarrow{\,\chi_{A}\,}\,\mathfrak{g}//A=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}\,\xrightarrow{\,\rho\,}\,\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{B},

where
\mathcal{B}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
is a finitely-generated Poisson-commutative polynomial ring of transcendence degree
m
, such that
\dim\mathfrak{g}=\dim\mathfrak{g}//A+m
. In what follows, we will concentrate on a specific case, letting
A=T
.

Let
T\subset G
be a maximal torus with
\dim T=r
. Then
d_{T}=r
, and Chevalley’s Restriction Theorem produces

(3.21)

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\cong S(\mathfrak{t})^{W}\cong\mathbb{R}[F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}],

where
F_{1},\ldots,F_{r}
are algebraically independent homogeneous generators. Hence
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
is a polynomial ring and therefore
\mathfrak{g}//G\cong\mathbb{A}^{r}
. The Kostant theorem identifies
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
with the Poisson center
\mathcal{Z}\bigl(S(\mathfrak{g})\bigr)
, and since
T\subset G
, we must have
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
. TWe then claim that the chain

(3.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})\,\xrightarrow{\ \chi_{T}\ }\,\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\,\xrightarrow{\ \rho\ }\,\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}

is superintegrable.

Theorem 3.18
.

Let
G
be a semisimple and connected Lie group over a field
\mathbb{R}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, and let
T\subset G
be a maximal rank torus
r=\dim T
. Then the inclusion of Poisson algebras

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})

makes the chain of affine Poisson varieties

(3.23)

\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\,\chi_{T}\,}\mathfrak{g}//T\xrightarrow{\,\rho\,}\mathfrak{g}//G

a superintegrable system. More precisely:

(i)

\displaystyle S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset\mathcal{Z}\big(S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\big),

(ii)

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}=r,\qquad\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}=\dim\mathfrak{g}-r,

(iii)

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})=\bigl(\dim\mathfrak{g}-r\bigr)+r.

Proof.

We start with (i). From the discussion above, we know
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
. Now, for any
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
, we have
f\in\mathcal{Z}(S(\mathfrak{g}))
. As
S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})
, for any
g\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
, we must have
\{f,g\}=0
. Hence (i) is claimed. Moreover, using (
3.21
), we have
\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}=r
, proving the first identity in (ii). By the definition of the spectrum, we identify
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})
.

To conclude that (
3.23
) is superintegrable, we only need to check the dimension. Consider the
T
-action on
\mathfrak{g}
by the coadjoint representation, which has a generic orbit dimension
r
. Indeed,
\mathfrak{g}
is decomposed into
T
-weight spaces

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},\qquad\left.\mathrm{Ad}(t)\right|_{\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}}=\alpha(t)\cdot\mathrm{id}.

Here,
\Phi
is the set of roots for
\mathfrak{g}
. Similar decomposition holds for
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. The stabiliser in
T
is then finite, so
\dim T\cdot X=r
with
X\in\mathfrak{g}
. By the dimension formula for the GIT quotients in (
3.17
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}=\dim\mathfrak{g}-\dim(\text{generic }T\text{-orbit})=\dim\mathfrak{g}-r.

Hence, (ii) holds. Since
\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})=\dim\mathfrak{g}
, together with (ii), gives

\displaystyle\dim\mathfrak{g}=\bigl(\dim\mathfrak{g}-r\bigr)+r=\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}+\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}.

As
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})
are Poisson subalgebras and
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
lies in the Poisson center of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
, the induced maps in (
3.23
) are Poisson morphisms, and the pair

(3.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset\mathcal{A}=S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}

defines a superintegrable system in the sense of Definition
3.16
. This proves (iii) and the claim.
∎

3.3.
Symplectic leaves in
\mathfrak{g}//T

Let
\left(\mathfrak{g}//T\right)_{\mathrm{reg}}
be the regular subset of
\mathfrak{g}//T
. In this Subsection
3.3
, we will examine the symplectic leaves in
\left(\mathfrak{g}//T\right)_{\mathrm{reg}}
by studying the pull back of the regular element in
\mathfrak{g}//G
via
\rho^{-1}
. Let
G
be a semisimple and connected Lie group with a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Fix an
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant inner product
B(\cdot,\cdot)
on
\mathfrak{g}
. For any
H\in\mathfrak{t}
and
u\in\mathfrak{g}
, define the linear function:

\displaystyle\mu_{H}(u):=B(u,H).

Note that
\mu_{H}
can be considered as linear generators for the Cartan commutant
S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
. Similar argument can be found in
(
11
, Proposition 2.5)
.

Lemma 3.19
.

For every
H\in\mathfrak{t}
, we have
\mu_{H}\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
and
\{\mu_{H},f\}=0
for all
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
.

Proof.

Since
T
is Abelian, the
T
-action on
\mathfrak{t}
is trivial. That is,
\mathrm{Ad}(t)H=H
. By the definition of a
T
-invariant generator, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{H}(\mathrm{Ad}(t)u)=B(\mathrm{Ad}(t)u,H)=B(u,\mathrm{Ad}(t^{-1})H)=B(u,H)=\mu_{H}(u).

Hence,
\mu_{H}\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
. Its Hamiltonian vector field is

\displaystyle X_{\mu_{H}}(u)=[\nabla\mu_{H}(u),u]=[H,u].

If
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
, by the definition of the centraliser, we have
f(\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(tH))u)=f(u)
. Differentiating at
t=0
gives

\displaystyle 0=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}f(\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(tH))u)=B(\nabla f(u),[H,u])=\{\mu_{H},f\}(u).

Hence,
\mu_{H}
Poisson commutes with every
T
-invariant polynomial as required.
∎

Let
\{C_{1},\dots,C_{r}\}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
be a set of homogeneous algebraically independent generators. By Theorem
3.18
,

\displaystyle\chi_{T}:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}//T,\qquad\rho:\mathfrak{g}//T\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}//G,\qquad\chi:=\rho\circ\chi_{T}:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}//G,

such that
\rho
is the morphism induced by the inclusion
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
. Then, for any
c\in(\mathfrak{g}//G)_{\mathrm{reg}}
and the corresponding coadjoint orbit
\mathcal{O}_{c}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, we have

\displaystyle\rho^{-1}(c)=\chi_{T}(\mathcal{O}_{c})\subset\mathfrak{g}//T,\qquad\chi_{T}^{-1}\big(\rho^{-1}(c)\big)=\chi^{-1}(c)=\{u\in\mathfrak{g}:C_{i}(u)=c_{i}\}.

Thus, the fiber of
\rho
is the image of a single coadjoint orbit, and its pullback to
\mathfrak{g}
is the usual Kostant-Chevalley level set.

By Remark
2.3
, we see that
\rho^{-1}(c)
is usually a union of symplectic leaves. To isolate a single leaf inside
\rho^{-1}(c)
, we must fix the
T
moment map component. Consider the
T
-equivariant linear map

(3.25)

\displaystyle\mu:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{t},\qquad\mu(u)=u|_{\mathfrak{t}}.

Since
\mu
is
T
-invariant, it induces a regular map

(3.26)

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}:\mathfrak{g}//T\longrightarrow\mathfrak{t},\qquad\bar{\mu}([u]_{T})=u|_{\mathfrak{t}}.

We then define

\displaystyle J:=(\rho,\bar{\mu}):\mathfrak{g}//T\longrightarrow\left(\mathfrak{g}//G\right)\times\mathfrak{t}

by joining
\rho
and
\bar{\mu}
in (
3.26
) such that each component in the fiber of
\rho
are controlled.

Proposition 3.20
.

Each component of
J
is a Casimir in the center of
\mathfrak{g}//T
. Equivalently, the pullbacks
\rho^{*}(C_{i})
and the coordinate functions of
\bar{\mu}
all lie in
\mathcal{Z}\bigl(S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\bigr)
.

Proof.

The components of
\bar{\mu}
are
\mu_{H}
with
H\in\mathfrak{t}
, which are central in
S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
by Lemma
3.19
.
∎

By Proposition
3.20
, each component of
J:\mathfrak{g}//T\rightarrow(\mathfrak{g}//G)\times\mathfrak{t}
is a Casimir. Hence, all Hamiltonian vector fields on
\mathfrak{g}//T
are tangent to the fibers of
J
. Therefore, the characteristic distribution of the Poisson structure is contained in
\ker dJ
, and every fiber
J^{-1}(c,\alpha)
is a union of symplectic leaves. On
(\mathfrak{g}//T)_{\mathrm{reg}}
, the rank of
dJ
is constant and equal to
2r
, and it is sufficient to show that for every regular pair
(c,\alpha)
, there exists a connected symplectic submanifold whose image is equal to
J^{-1}(c,\alpha)
. Then each connected component of that fiber is a symplectic leaf, and the Poisson rank is constant. At any regular point, the symplectic leaf has dimension

\displaystyle\dim(\mathfrak{g}//T)_{\mathrm{reg}}-\mathrm{rank}(dJ).

Thus, each connected component of a regular fiber is a single symplectic leaf.

We now provide a formal construction of the symplectic leaf in
\mathfrak{g}//T
. Let
\chi:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}//G
be the Chevalley map given by
\chi(u)=(C_{1}(u),\dots,C_{r}(u))
. For a point
c=(c_{1},\dots,c_{r})\in\mathfrak{g}//G
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{c}:=\chi^{-1}(c),

which is a union of
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-orbits. In particular, on the regular locus
\left(\mathfrak{g}//G\right)_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{c}
, there is a single coadjoint orbit. For each
\alpha
, we define the restricted moment map
\mu_{T}:=\left.\mu\right|_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}:\mathcal{O}_{c}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*}\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }\mathfrak{t}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{t}
such that
\iota_{X_{H}}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}=dB(\mu_{T},H)
with
H\in\mathfrak{t}
, given by
\mu_{T}^{c}(\eta)=\eta|_{\mathfrak{t}}
. For
\alpha\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}
, define

\displaystyle J^{-1}(c,\alpha)=\bigl\{[u]_{T}\in\mathfrak{g}//T:C_{i}(u)=c_{i},\text{ with }i=1,\dots,r,\ u|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\alpha\bigr\}.

In the rest of this subsection, we will work on the regular locus
(\mathfrak{g}//T)_{\mathrm{reg}}
, where we assume that the differentials
\{\,d(\rho^{*}C_{i}),\,d(\bar{\mu}_{1}),\dots,d(\bar{\mu}_{r})\,\}
have maximal rank
2r
, and the
T
-action on
\mu_{T,c}^{-1}(\alpha):=\mu^{-1}(\alpha)\cap\mathcal{O}_{c}
is globally free on the regular locus. Then in the following theorem, we illustrate the symplectic leaf in
\mathfrak{g}//T
. Then, on the regular locus,

\displaystyle\rho^{-1}(c)=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\mu_{T}(\mathcal{O}_{c})}J^{-1}(c,\alpha).

In the following theorem, we provide the identification of the symplectic leaf
J^{-1}(c,\alpha)
by identifying these fibers with reduced adjoint orbits. For the Marsden-Weinstein reduction on a submanifold, see, for instance,
16
.

Theorem 3.21
.

Let
\bigl(J^{-1}(c,\alpha)\bigr)_{\varpi}
be the smooth and connected component of
J^{-1}(c,\alpha)
. On
(\mathfrak{g}//T)_{\mathrm{reg}}
, the symplectic leaves are precisely the connected components of the common level sets of
J
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{c,\alpha}=\bigl(J^{-1}(c,\alpha)\bigr)_{\varpi}=\bigl\{[u]_{T}\in\mathfrak{g}//T:\ C_{i}(u)=c_{i},\ u|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\alpha\bigr\}_{\varpi}

Equivalently, for any
c\in(\mathfrak{g}//G)_{\mathrm{reg}}
, if
\mu_{T}:\mathcal{O}_{c}\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
denotes the
T
-moment map on any coadjoint orbit
(\mathcal{O}_{c},\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c})
, the Marsden-Weinstein reduction is then given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{c,\alpha}\cong\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{c}\cap\mu^{-1}(\alpha)\bigr)\big/T.

Proof.

Fix
c\in(\mathfrak{g}//G)_{\mathrm{reg}}
and let
(\mathcal{O}_{c},\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c})
be the coadjoint orbit. For
H\in\mathfrak{t}
, the fundamental vector field generated by
H
at
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{c}
is
X_{H}(\eta)=[H,\eta]
. For any
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
, the KKS form at
c
is given by

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}(\eta)\big([X,\eta],[Y,\eta]\big)=B(\eta,[X,Y]).

We proof this theorem by showing the following parts: we first show that
\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T:=\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{c}\cap\mu^{-1}(\alpha)\bigr)\big/T
is symplectic by providing its symplectic structure. We then show that
\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T
to
\mathfrak{g}//T
is a Poisson immersion. Finally, we explain why
\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T
can be identified by the level set of
J
.

Recall that the
T
-action on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{g}
is Hamiltonian, with the moment map
\mu_{T}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
given by restriction to
\mathfrak{t}
. For a fixed regular value
\alpha\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{t}
such that the
T
-action on

\displaystyle Z_{c,\alpha}:=\mu_{T,c}^{-1}(\alpha)=\mathcal{O}_{c}\cap\mu^{-1}(\alpha)

is free and proper, let
\zeta:Z_{c,\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{c}
be the inclusion, and let

\rho:Z_{c,\alpha}\rightarrow Z_{c,\alpha}/T
be the principal
T
-bundle. Then the pullback of the KKS form to
Z_{c,\alpha}
gives

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{H}}\,\zeta^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}=\zeta^{*}\big(\iota_{X_{H}}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}\big)=\zeta^{*}\big(d\langle\mu_{T},H\rangle\big)=d\big(\langle\mu_{T},H\rangle\circ\zeta\big)=0,

since
\langle\mu_{T},H\rangle
is constant on the level set
Z_{c,\alpha}
. Moreover,
\zeta^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}
is
T
-invariant as
\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}
is
G
-invariant. Hence,
\zeta^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}
is a basic
2
-form for the principal
T
-bundle
\rho
. Since
T
acts freely and properly on
Z_{c,\alpha}
, by Marsden-Weinstein reduction, this action produces a smooth symplectic manifold

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T=Z_{c,\alpha}/T

with a symplectic structure
\omega_{\mathrm{red}}
induced by

\displaystyle\zeta^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}=\rho^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{red}}.

Let
J_{\alpha}:\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}//T
. To show that
\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T
is a symplectic leaf, it is sufficient to show that
J_{\alpha}
is a Poisson immersion. In other words,
dJ_{\alpha}
is injective on the regular locus. Consider the GIT quotient
\chi_{T}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}//T
. By Proposition
3.15
, it is a Poisson submersion. The pullback on the coordinate algebra induces the following surjection
\chi_{T}^{*}:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{g}//T)\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]\cong S(\mathfrak{g})
. The composition
Z_{c,\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ \zeta\ }\mathcal{O}_{c}\xrightarrow{\ \chi_{T}|_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}=\chi_{T}\ }\mathfrak{g}//T
is
T
-invariant, and therefore it factors through a continuous map
J_{\alpha}:\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}//T
. In this way, we form a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z_{c,\alpha}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{c}\subset\mathfrak{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T=Z_{c,\alpha}/T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{g}//T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowright}

with
J_{\alpha}\circ\rho=\chi_{T}\circ\zeta
. We first show that
dJ_{\alpha}:T\left(\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T\right)\rightarrow T\left(\mathfrak{g}//T\right)
is injective. Fix
u\in Z_{c,\alpha}
and set
[u]_{T}:=\rho(u)\in\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T
. Since
\rho
is a principal
T
-bundle, it is therefore a surjective submersion with

\displaystyle\ker(d\rho)_{u}=T_{u}(T\cdot u).

Consequently, by the first isomorphism theorem on the Poisson projection
d\rho
, there is a canonical identification

(3.27)

\displaystyle T_{[u]_{T}}(\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T)\cong T_{u}Z_{c,\alpha}\big/T_{u}(T\cdot u).

We now provide the differentiation of
J_{\alpha}
. Since
\zeta
is an inclusion, for every
v\in T_{u}Z_{c,\alpha}
,
(d\zeta)_{u}:T_{u}Z_{c,\alpha}\to T_{u}\mathcal{O}_{c}
is the inclusion of tangent spaces. That is,
(d\zeta)_{u}(v)=v
. Then the differential of the commutative relations is as follows:

(3.28)

\displaystyle(dJ_{\alpha})_{[u]_{T}}\big((d\rho)_{u}(v)\big)=(d\chi_{T})_{u}(v).

To conclude that
dJ_{\alpha}
at
[u]_{T}
is injective, it is sufficient to show that
\ker(d\chi_{T})_{u}\subset\ker(d\rho)_{u}
. As
\chi_{T}
is
T
-invariant,
(d\chi_{T})_{u}
vanishes on
T_{u}(T\cdot u)
, the right hand side of (
3.28
) depends only on the class of
v
modulo
T_{u}(T\cdot u)
. Thus, (
3.28
) is compatible with (
3.27
). Suppose
(dJ_{\alpha})_{[u]_{T}}(\varsigma)=0
for some
\varsigma\in T_{[u]_{T}}(\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T)
. Let
V=(\mathfrak{g}//T)_{\mathrm{reg}}
. By Proposition
3.15
, the restriction
\chi_{T}:\chi_{T}^{-1}(V)\to V
is smooth. Hence,
(d\chi_{T})_{u}
is surjective for all
u\in\chi_{T}^{-1}(V)
. Choose
v\in T_{u}Z_{c,\alpha}
with
\varsigma=(d\rho)_{u}(v)
. since
\chi_{T}
is
T
-invariant, the identity (
3.28
) gives
(d\chi_{T})_{u}(v)=0
. Hence,
v\in\ker(d\chi_{T})_{u}
, and

T_{u}(T\cdot u)\subseteq\ker(d\chi_{T})_{u}.

Assume now that
J_{\alpha}([u]_{T})\in V
. Then
u\in\chi_{T}^{-1}(V)
. In particular,
\chi_{T}
is a submersion at
u
. Therefore, the fiber
F:=\chi_{T}^{-1}(\chi_{T}(u))
is a smooth embedded submanifold of
\mathfrak{g}
, and the constant rank theorem yields

(3.29)

\displaystyle T_{u}F=\ker(d\chi_{T})_{u}.

Since
T
is compact, invariant polynomials separate
T
-orbits. Hence, the fiber
F
coincides with the
T
-orbit through
u
. That is,
F=T\cdot u
. Hence, (
3.29
) immediately gives

(3.30)

\displaystyle\ker(d\chi_{T})_{u}=T_{u}(T\cdot u).

Finally, since
\rho:Z_{c,\alpha}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T
is a principal
T
-bundle, we have
\ker(d\rho)_{u}=T_{u}(T\cdot u)
. Thus, if
v\in\ker(d\chi_{T})_{u}
, then by (
3.30
) we obtain
v\in T_{u}(T\cdot u)=\ker(d\rho)_{u}
, as claimed.
Therefore,
\varsigma=(d\rho)_{u}(v)=0
implies that
(dJ_{\alpha})_{[u]_{T}}
is injective. Since
[u]_{T}
was arbitrary,
J_{\alpha}
is an immersion.

Define the pullback of
J_{\alpha}
by

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}^{*}(f)\circ\rho=\left.(\chi_{T}^{*}f)\right|_{Z_{c,\alpha}}

for any
f\in\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{g}//T\right)
. Let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\sharp}
be the Poisson structure on
\mathcal{O}\left(\mathfrak{g}//T\right)
. Using the fact that
\chi_{T}
is Poisson, we compute

(3.31)

\displaystyle\rho^{*}\big(J_{\alpha}^{*}\{f,g\}_{\sharp}\big)

\displaystyle=(\chi_{T}\circ\zeta)^{*}\{f,g\}_{\sharp}=\zeta^{*}\big(\chi_{T}^{*}\{f,g\}_{\sharp}\big)=\zeta^{*}\big\{\chi_{T}^{*}f,\chi_{T}^{*}g\big\}_{\mathcal{O}_{c}}.

Comparing the (
3.31
) with the relations
\rho^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{red}}=\zeta^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS},c}
yields

\displaystyle\rho^{*}\big(J_{\alpha}^{*}\{f,g\}_{\sharp}\big)=\rho^{*}\big\{J_{\alpha}^{*}F,J_{\alpha}^{*}G\big\}_{\mathrm{red}}.

Since
\rho
is an injective immersion (on algebras), and
\rho^{*}
is surjective (on functions), the equality holds before apply
J_{\alpha}^{*}
as follows:

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}^{*}\{f,g\}_{\sharp}=\{J_{\alpha}^{*}F,J_{\alpha}^{*}G\}_{\mathrm{red}}.

Let
\pi_{\mathrm{red}}
be the Poisson tensor on
\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T
, and let
\pi_{\sharp}
be the Poisson tensor on
\mathfrak{g}//T
. Since
J_{\alpha}
preserves the Poisson bracket (by the Poisson property), we have that the pushforward of the reduced Poisson tensor equals the Poisson tensor restricted to the image. That is,

{(J_{\alpha})}_{*}\pi_{\mathrm{red}}=\left.\pi_{\sharp}\right|_{\mathrm{Im}\,J_{\alpha}}.

Hence,
\mathrm{Im}J_{\alpha}
is a symplectic submanifold of
\mathfrak{g}//T
. This can be seen as follows: for any
u\in Z_{c,\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle J(J_{\alpha}([u]_{\mathrm{red}}))=(c(u),u|_{\mathfrak{t}})=(c,\alpha).

Therefore,
\mathrm{Im}\,J_{\alpha}\subset J^{-1}(c,\alpha)
. Conversely, if
[u]_{T}\in J^{-1}(c,\alpha)
, then
u\in\mathcal{O}_{c}
and
u|_{\mathfrak{t}}=\alpha
, so
J_{\alpha}([u]_{\mathrm{red}})\in\mathrm{Im}\,J_{\alpha}
. Thus, we have
\mathrm{Im}\,J_{\alpha}=J^{-1}(c,\alpha)
, and each connected component of this fiber is a symplectic leaf in
\left(\mathfrak{g}//T\right)_{\mathrm{reg}}
.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

If either
T
-action is not free or
\alpha
is not regular, then
\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T
is a stratified symplectic space in the sense of
42
.

By Theorem
3.18
, we have

\displaystyle\dim\mathfrak{g}//T=\dim\mathfrak{g}-r.

Fixing the
2r
central coordinates
(C_{1},\dots,C_{r},\bar{\mu}_{1},\dots,\bar{\mu}_{r})
yields the following:

Corollary 3.23
.

Assume that
c
and
\alpha
are regular, and the
T
-action is free in
(\mathfrak{g}//T)_{\mathrm{reg}}
. Then
\dim\mathcal{L}_{c,\alpha}=\dim\mathfrak{g}-3r
.

Proof.

For a regular
c
,
\mathcal{O}_{c}
has a dimension
\dim\mathfrak{g}-r
as the stabiliser is a Cartan of dimension
r
. Moreover, if
\alpha
is a regular value of
\mu_{T}
, and the
T
-action is free, then

\displaystyle\dim\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{c}//_{\alpha}T\bigr)=\dim\mathcal{O}_{c}-2\dim T=(\dim\mathfrak{g}-r)-2r=\dim\mathfrak{g}-3r,

which equals the dimension of the leaf
\mathcal{L}_{c,\alpha}
, is given by Theorem
3.21
.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

(i) In irregular strata (e.g., non-maximum rank of
dJ
), the foliation stratifies further, and the dimensions of the leaves may decrease.

(ii) By Definition
2.1
, together with Theorem
3.21
, we immediately conclude that

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{g}//T\right)_{\mathrm{reg}}=\bigsqcup_{(c,\alpha)\in J\left(\left(\mathfrak{g}//T\right)_{\mathrm{reg}}\right)}\mathcal{L}_{c,\alpha}.

For a fixed
(c,\alpha)\in\left(\mathfrak{g}//G\right)\times\mathfrak{t}
with
c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{r})
. Taking
H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}
to be the basis elements of
\mathfrak{t}
. Define the Poisson ideal

(3.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{c,\alpha}:=\left\langle\rho^{*}(C_{1})-c_{1},\ldots,\rho^{*}(C_{r})-c_{r},\ \mu(H_{1})-\alpha(H_{1}),\ldots,\mu(H_{r})-\alpha(H_{r})\right\rangle

in
S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}
. It is clear that
\mathcal{I}_{c,\alpha}
is a Poisson ideal, as it is generated by fixing the Casimir elements. Then by Theorem
3.21
, we could define the following fiber algebra
\mathcal{P}_{c,\alpha}=S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\big/\mathcal{I}_{c,\alpha}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{P}_{c,\alpha}=\left(J^{-1}(c,\alpha)\right)_{\varpi}=\mathcal{L}_{c,\alpha}.

4.
Superintegrable system on
T^{*}(G/A)

Our preliminary work in this Section
4
is to develop a unified approach for building superintegrable systems on a cotangent bundle of an adjoint orbit, as described in
20
within the context of Mischenko-Fomenko non-commutative integrability. In the rest of this paper,
G
denotes a real, compact, semisimple, simply connected Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
endowed with the canonical Lie-Poisson bracket. For any
f,g\in S(\mathfrak{g})
, (
2.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\{f,g\}(X)=B(X,[\nabla f(X),\nabla g(X)]),\quad X\in\mathfrak{g},

where
\nabla f(X)\in\mathfrak{g}
denotes the gradient of
f
in
X
, similar to
\nabla g(X)
. Let
B
be the (negative definite) Killing form on
\mathfrak{g}
such that
\mathfrak{g}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Fix
W\in\mathfrak{g}
and write

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}:=\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}}(W)=\left\{X\in\mathfrak{g}:[W,X]=0\right\}.

Let
A:=Z_{G}(W)
be the centraliser of
W
in
G
. Then
A
is a closed subgroup of
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}
. In this way, we can decompose the Lie algebra as
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
reductively, where
\mathfrak{a}=\mathrm{Lie}(A)
and
\mathfrak{m}=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{ad}W)
is the
B
-orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{a}
in
\mathfrak{g}
. That is, the reductive commutator relations are
[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}]\subset\mathfrak{a}
and
[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{a}]\subset\mathfrak{m}
. In particular, under this construction, we have
\mathfrak{a}
such that
A=\exp\left(\ker\mathrm{ad}W\right)
. Define the homogeneous space by

(4.1)

\displaystyle M:=G/A.

Using left translations, we may identify
T_{[e]}M\cong\mathfrak{m}
. Moreover, through
B
, we have the identification
\mathfrak{m}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{m}
. Note that the homogeneous space defined in (
4.1
) can be realised as the adjoint orbit
\mathcal{O}_{W}:=\mathrm{Ad}(G)\cdot W
. In other words,
G/A\cong\mathrm{Ad}(G)\cdot W
. In the rest of the sections, we always realise the homogeneous space
M
as an adjoint orbit.

Let
T\subset G
be a maximal torus with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
. Let
\Phi\subset\mathfrak{t}^{*}
be the root system of
(\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{t}^{\mathbb{C}})
, and fix a positive system
