\Phi^{+}\subset\Phi
. For
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\mathbb{C}}
be the corresponding root space. Define the vanishing root set of
W
by

(4.2)

\displaystyle\Phi_{W}:=\{\alpha\in\Phi:\alpha(W)=0\},\quad\Phi_{W}^{+}:=\Phi_{W}\cap\Phi^{+}.

Definition 4.1
.

The element
W
is called
regular
if
\Phi_{W}=\emptyset
and
irregular
if
\Phi_{W}\neq\emptyset
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
M=\mathrm{Ad}(G)\cdot W
be the adjoint orbit. Under the notation above, we have the following characterization of the regular and irregular points:

(a) If
W
is regular, then
\dim\mathfrak{a}=\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{g}
. In particular,
\dim M=\dim G-\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{g}
.

(b) If
W
is irregular, then
\dim\mathfrak{a}>\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{g}
such that the subalgebra
\mathfrak{a}
has the following decomposition:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi_{W}^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}^{\mathbb{R}},

where
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}^{\mathbb{R}}:=\big(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}\big)\cap\mathfrak{g}
. In particular,
\dim M<\dim G-\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{g}
.

Remark 4.3
.

In the regular case,
A=T
and
M=G/T
is the full flag manifold of
G
. In the irregular case,
A\supsetneq T
and
M=G/A
is a compact, smooth homogeneous space of smaller dimension: a partial flag manifold. Hence,

\displaystyle\dim M=\dim G-\dim A=\dim G-\big(\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{g}+2\,\left|\Phi_{W}^{+}\right|\big).

So
\dim M<\dim(G/T)=\dim G-\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{g}
if
\Phi_{W}\neq\emptyset
. Many constructions used later, such as moment maps, invariant polynomials on affine slices of the form
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
, and Poisson quotients, work uniformly in both cases. Also, any rank-drop phenomena are constrained to the algebraic subset defined by the linear equations
\alpha(W)=0
for some
\alpha\in\Phi
.

Let
T^{*}M
be the cotangent bundle of
M
. Assume that it is equipped with a twisted symplectic form

(4.3)

\displaystyle\omega_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+\varepsilon\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}:T^{*}(T^{*}M)\times T^{*}(T^{*}M)\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

where
\pi:T^{*}M\rightarrow M
is the canonical projection and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}/\{0\}
. Note that for a sufficiently small value of
\varepsilon
, the compatibility of the Poisson bracket always holds. Hence, there always exists a twisted symplectic structure if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. Hence, from this point onward,
(T^{*}(G/A),\omega_{\varepsilon})
is always a twisted symplectic manifold.

Section
4
is split into two subsections. In Subsection
4.1
, we study the magnetic Hamiltonian flow on the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
and provide explicit formulas for the first integrals and their corresponding polynomial Poisson algebras on
T^{*}M
. In Subsection
4.2
, we then demonstrate how these geometric structures yield alternative perspectives on the construction of magnetic geodesic flows from
20
and generate superintegrable systems on
T^{*}(G/A)
in the sense of Definition (
2.1
).

4.1.
Construction of the first integrals on
T^{*}(G/A)

Now, we shall focus on the construction of superintegrable systems in phase space
T^{*}M
. The concept of superintegrability in Hamiltonian mechanics has significant implications for mathematical physics and symplectic geometry. In
20
, the author studied magnetic geodesic flows in homogeneous spaces, establishing algebraic integrability conditions using invariant polynomial functions. In Subsection
4.1
, we first revisit some results, such as Poisson structures and vector fields, and then provide the description of the corresponding polynomial Poisson algebras generated by the first integrals. Let
G,\mathfrak{g}
and
M
be the same as defined above. In a left trivialization, we have

(4.4)

\displaystyle T^{*}M\,\cong\,\left(G\times\mathfrak{m}\right)/A,\qquad\mathsf{p}\longmapsto[g,X],

where the covector at
[g]:=gA
corresponds to
X\in\mathfrak{m}
. Hence, the left-trivialization in (
4.4
) identifies the points of
T^{*}M
as
[g,X]
with
g\in G
and
X\in\mathfrak{m}
. Let us now look at the tangent space of
T^{*}M
. Recall that from
20
, under the explicit local coordinate
[g,X]
, a generic tangent vector at
(g,X)
may be written as

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{a})\oplus\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\cong T_{[g,X]}(T^{*}M),

(4.5)

\displaystyle\ (\bar{v},\delta X)

\displaystyle\longmapsto g_{*}\big|_{X}\left(v,\,-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]+\delta X\right),

where
\bar{v}\in\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{a}\cong T_{eA}(G/A)
for any
v\in\mathfrak{m}
,
\delta X\in\mathfrak{m}
is the vertical direction, and
g_{*}|_{X}:\mathfrak{m}\oplus\mathfrak{m}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }T_{(g,X)}T^{*}M
denotes the left-trivialization isomorphism. For more details on the derivation of this local coordinate (
4.5
), we refer the reader to
43
;
20
. Formally, if
(g(t),X(t))
is a flow of a tangent vector at
[g,X]
with
g(0)=g
and
X(0)=X
, then
\delta X=\left.\dfrac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}X(t)\in\mathfrak{m}
. Moreover, with the Killing form
B
, we can identify
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{a})^{*}\cong\mathfrak{a}^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{m}
. Hence,

\displaystyle T^{*}_{[g,X]}(T^{*}M)\cong\mathfrak{a}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{m},\qquad\langle(\eta,\mu),(\bar{v},\delta X)\rangle=B(\eta,\bar{v})+B(\mu,\delta X).

Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:T_{[g,X]}^{*}(T^{*}M)\times T_{[g,X]}(T^{*}M)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a canonical dual pair. Thus, the first component
\eta
pairs with the base direction
\bar{v}
, and the second component
\mu
pairs with the vertical direction
\delta X
.
Note that, due to the identification
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{a}\cong\mathfrak{m}
, throughout this paper we will drop the bar on
\bar{v}
and the identity
\bar{v}
with
v\in\mathfrak{m}
, such that
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
is identified with
\mathfrak{m}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
. Next, we define the twisted Poisson structures on
T^{*}(T^{*}M)
.

Definition 4.4
.

Fix
W\in\mathfrak{g}
with
\mathrm{Ad}(A)W=W
, and a real parameter
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}/\{0\}
. Let
\omega_{\mathrm{can}}
be the canonical symplectic form on
T^{*}M
, and let
\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
be the Kirillov-Kostant-Souriau (KKS) two-form on
M
at
[e]=eA
by

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}([e])\big([X],[Y]\big)=B\big(W,[X,Y]\big),\qquad X,Y\in\mathfrak{m}

and extend
\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
to all of
M
by left
G
-invariance. Define
\omega_{\varepsilon}:=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+\varepsilon\,\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}},
where
\pi:T^{*}M\to M
is the canonical projection. In a local section
\sigma:U\subset\mathfrak{m}\rightarrow G
, one finds

(4.6)

\displaystyle\omega_{\varepsilon}=dB(X,\sigma^{-1}d\sigma)+\varepsilon B(W,[\sigma^{-1}d\sigma,\sigma^{-1}d\sigma]),

which is an expression of the local coordinates. We now consider the induced Poisson bracket from
\omega_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.6
). In local coordinates
(x_{i},p_{i})
on
T^{*}M
adapted to a coordinate chart on
M
, we can write
\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}F_{ij}(x)\,dx_{i}\wedge dx^{j}
. Then the associated twisted Poisson bracket in
T^{*}M
is

(4.7)

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{\varepsilon}=\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\partial_{x_{i}}f\,\partial_{p_{i}}g-\partial_{x_{i}}g\,\partial_{p_{i}}f\big)}_{\text{$=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}^{-1}$}}+\varepsilon\underbrace{\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq n}F_{ij}(x)\,\partial_{p_{i}}f\,\partial_{p_{j}}g}_{\text{$=\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}^{-1}$}},

where
F_{ij}(x)=-F_{ji}(x)
.

We now compute Hamiltonian vector fields in the coordinates given in Definition
4.4
. By the definition of Hamiltonian vector fields, a non-zero vector field
X_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathfrak{X}(T^{*}M)
satisfies
\iota_{X_{\mathcal{H}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}=d\mathcal{H}
. Let
\mathcal{H}(\textbf{x},\textbf{p})=x_{k}
be a coordinate function. We find
X_{x_{k}}=\partial_{p_{k}}
, where
\partial_{p_{k}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{k}}
. Indeed, the Wedge product formula gives
\iota_{X}(\alpha\wedge\beta)=\iota_{X}\alpha\wedge\beta+(-1)^{\deg\alpha}\alpha\wedge\iota_{X}\beta
for any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(T^{*}M)
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega^{1}(T^{*}M)
. Then

\displaystyle\iota_{\partial_{p_{k}}}(dx_{j}\wedge dp_{j})=\left(\iota_{\partial_{p_{k}}}dx_{j}\right)\wedge dp_{j}-dx_{j}\wedge\left(\iota_{\partial_{p_{k}}}dp_{j}\right)=-\delta_{kj}dx_{j},

where
\delta_{kj}
is the Kronecker delta. Using (
4.7
), we obtain
\iota_{\partial_{p_{k}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}=dx_{k}
. Similarly, for
\mathcal{H}(\textbf{x},\textbf{p})=p_{k}
, solving
\iota_{X_{p_{k}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}=dp_{k}
gives
X_{p_{k}}=-\partial_{x_{k}}+\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{n}F_{jk}(x)\partial_{p_{j}}
. Alternatively, it is an easy but computational exercise to show
20

\{x_{i},x_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=0,\ \{x_{i},p_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=\delta^{i}_{j},\ \{p_{i},p_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon F_{ij}(x).

This can be proved by a direct calculation. Given a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}(\textbf{x},\textbf{p})\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}M)
, the
magnetic Hamiltonian equations
are

(4.8)

\displaystyle\dfrac{dx_{i}}{dt}=\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial p_{i}},\qquad\dfrac{dp_{i}}{dt}=-\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial x_{i}}+\varepsilon F_{ij}(x)\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial p_{j}}.

Let

(4.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(g,X)=\frac{1}{2}\,B(X,X).

In the rest of this work, we will write
[g,X]:=(g,X)\in(G\times\mathfrak{m})/A
. Note that
\mathcal{H}
is well-defined on
T^{*}M
as, for any
a\in A
,

\mathcal{H}(ga,\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})(X))=\frac{1}{2}B(\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X,\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X)=\frac{1}{2}B(X,X)=\mathcal{H}(g,X).

Hence,
\mathcal{H}
is both left
G
-invariant and
\mathrm{Ad}(a)
-invariant. Let us remark that in
20
, the author implements the magnetic term into the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}(g,X)
, and defines a twisted Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(g,X)=\frac{1}{2}B(X-\varepsilon W,X-\varepsilon W)
. Since
B(X,W)=0
, expanding the square shows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(g,X)=\frac{1}{2}B(X,X)+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}B(W,W)=\mathcal{H}(g,X)+c.

Here,
c=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}B(W,W)
is a constant. Hence,
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
differs from
\mathcal{H}
by the constant
c
, and they have identical dynamics and, therefore, generate the same Hamiltonian vector fields and first integrals. We now introduce two maps: a left moment map
P
and a right affine projection map
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
. The pullback of these maps lifts the first integrals to the smooth function space of
T^{*}M
.

Definition 4.5
.

Define smooth maps
P:T^{*}M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}:T^{*}M\to\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
by

\displaystyle P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr),\qquad\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X)=X-\varepsilon W.

Note that
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
takes values in the affine subspace
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W:=\{X-\varepsilon W:X\in\mathfrak{m}\}\subset\mathfrak{g}
.

Remark 4.6
.

(i) Fix an
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form
B
on
\mathfrak{g}
, and use it to identify
\mathfrak{g}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{*}
via the musical isomorphisms
\flat:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
x^{\flat}:=B(x,\cdot)
,
\sharp:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}
,
\sharp=\flat^{-1}
. Let

\displaystyle P:T^{*}M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*},\qquad\begin{matrix}J:=P^{\sharp}=B^{-1}\circ P:T^{*}M\to\mathfrak{g},\\
J(g,X):=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr).\end{matrix}

Hence,
P
can be viewed equivalently as a
\mathfrak{g}
valued moment map.
For
Y,X_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}
, define the component functions

(4.10)

\displaystyle P_{Y}:=\langle P,Y\rangle=B\bigl(J,Y\bigr)\text{ and }P_{i}:=P_{X_{i}}=\langle P,X_{i}\rangle=B\bigl(J,X_{i}\bigr),

where
\langle\eta,Y\rangle=\ell_{Y}(\eta)
is the dual pair. In what follows, we adopt the moment map convention

(4.11)

\displaystyle\iota_{\widehat{u}}\,\omega_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=dP_{u},\qquad P_{u}:=\langle P,u\rangle=B\bigl(J,u\bigr),\qquad u\in\mathfrak{g},

where
\widehat{u}
is the fundamental vector field generated by
u
and
\omega_{\varepsilon}
is the magnetic symplectic form.

(ii) By the
G
-invariant metric induced by
B
, we identify
TM\cong T^{*}M
. Under this identification, the twisted symplectic form
\omega_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+\varepsilon\,\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
on
T^{*}M
corresponds to the metric twist on
TM
obtained by adding
\varepsilon\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
to the canonical symplectic form given by the metric. In other words, we can equivalently work on
TM
with a metric
B
and a magnetic term. From Section
4
onward, we continue to work on
T^{*}M
, not
TM
, and consistently use the convention
P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(X-\varepsilon W)
as defined in Definition
4.5
.

Up to this point, let us remark that in this work, we identify
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{g}
and
T^{*}M\cong TM
via
B
. Under these identifications, the map
P
(in Definition
4.5
) serves as the standard moment map for the left
G
-action, satisfying
\iota_{\widehat{u}}\omega_{\varepsilon}=dP_{u}
, and there is no differences between the dual pair
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and the Killing form
B(\cdot,\cdot)
. In the rest of this work, to maintain formality, we will still use the dual pair to define the functional induced by the twisted moment map
P
.

We now have the following lemma to illustrate the well-definedness of the maps defined in Definition
4.5
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
M,P,\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
be the same as defined above, and let
\xi=X-\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{g}

1
1

1

Throughout this paper, we fix the notation
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
to be a regular element. Moreover, we further assume that
X\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{reg}}
and
W
could be either regular or irregular.
be a regular element for some
X\in\mathfrak{m}
and a fixed
W\in\mathfrak{g}
. We then have the following arguments:

(i)
P
is left
G
-equivariant. That is,
P(h\cdot(g,X))=\mathrm{Ad}(h)P(g,X)
.

(ii) The affine translation
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}:\mathfrak{m}\to\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
is
\mathrm{Ad}(A)
-equivariant and
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\cong S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
via the translation
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}:\mathfrak{m}\rightarrow\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
.

(iii) Let
A
act on
G\times\mathfrak{m}
on the right by
(g,X)\cdot a=(ga,\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X)
. Define the quotient map
\widetilde{\pi}_{m}:G\times\mathfrak{m}\to\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
by
\widetilde{\pi}_{m}(g,X)=X-\varepsilon W
. Then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{m}\big((g,X)\cdot a\big)=\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})\,\widetilde{\pi}_{m}(g,X).

Consequently, for any
A
-invariant polynomial
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
, the polynomial
\theta\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{m}
is
A
-invariant on
G\times\mathfrak{m}
and descends to a well-defined smooth function
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}M)
given by

\displaystyle(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta)((g,X))=\theta\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr).

Moreover,
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is left
G
-invariant:
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(h\cdot(g,X))=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}((g,X))
for all
h\in G
.

Proof.

We first show part (i). In the left trivialisation
T^{*}M\cong(G\times\mathfrak{m})/A
, for any
h\in G
, the left
G
-action is
h\cdot(g,X)=(hg,X)
. By Definition
4.5
,
P((g,X))=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(X-\varepsilon W)
. Therefore, a direct computation shows that

\displaystyle P\bigl(h\cdot(g,X)\bigr)=P((hg,X))=\mathrm{Ad}(hg)(X-\varepsilon W)=\mathrm{Ad}(h)\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}(g)(X-\varepsilon W)\bigr)=\mathrm{Ad}(h)\,P((g,X)).

Hence,
P
is
G
-equivariant.

Now consider the second part. Define the translation
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}:\mathfrak{m}\to\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
by
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}(X)=X-\varepsilon W
. Since by construction,
W\in\mathfrak{g}
is
\mathrm{Ad}(A)
-stable, we compute for every
a\in A
and
X\in\mathfrak{m}
,

\displaystyle\tau_{-\varepsilon W}\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}(a)X\bigr)=\mathrm{Ad}(a)X-\varepsilon W=\mathrm{Ad}(a)\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr)=\mathrm{Ad}(a)\,\tau_{-\varepsilon W}(X).

Thus,
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}
is
A
-equivariant. Moreover, note that the inverse of
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}
is simply given by
\tau_{+\varepsilon W}:\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W\to\mathfrak{m}
. It induces an isomorphism of invariant polynomial rings via pullback:

\displaystyle\tau_{+\varepsilon W}^{*}:S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}\xrightarrow{\cong}S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A},\qquad\tau_{-\varepsilon W}^{*}:S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\xrightarrow{\cong}S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}.

Thus,
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\cong S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. Indeed,
\tau_{+\varepsilon W}^{*}
and
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}^{*}
are inverse
A
-equivariant ring isomorphisms.

Finally, we show part (iii). For any
a\in A
, by the definition of the quotient map
\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\mathrm{Ad}(A)W=W
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}\big((g,X)\cdot a\big)=\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}\big(ga,\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X\big)=\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X-\varepsilon W=\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})(X-\varepsilon W).

Thus,
\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is right
A
-equivariant. If
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
, then
\theta\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is
A
-invariant. Consequently,
\theta\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is constant on
A
-orbits in
G\times\mathfrak{m}
and thus descends to the quotient
(G\times\mathfrak{m})/A\cong T^{*}M
. This implies that
(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta)((g,X))=\theta(X-\varepsilon W)
, and therefore, the left
G
-invariance of
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is immediate from the trivialization. Indeed, for all
h\in G
,
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(h\cdot(g,X))=h\cdot X-\varepsilon W=X-\varepsilon W
as
h\cdot W=W
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

The map
\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X)=X-\varepsilon W
is right
A
-equivariant, and the composition
\theta\circ\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is
A
-invariant and descends to a function
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta([g,X])
on
T^{*}M
.

Let us now recall some basic notation and terminology discussed in
20
, which help us process the explicit coordinate computation. With the standard
G
-invariant metric on
M
induced by
B
and the magnetic term
\varepsilon\,\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}(g,X)(gv_{1},gv_{2})

=\varepsilon B(W,[v_{1},v_{2}])
, under the local coordinates on
T_{(g,X)}^{*}T^{*}M
defined in (
4.5
), the twisted symplectic form at
(g,X)
is

(4.12)

\displaystyle\omega_{\varepsilon}\left(g_{*}|_{X}\left(v_{1},-\frac{1}{2}[v_{1},X]+w_{1}\right),\ g_{*}|_{X}\left(v_{2},-\frac{1}{2}[v_{2},X]+w_{2}\right)\right)=B(w_{1},v_{2})-B(w_{2},v_{1})+\varepsilon\,B(W,[v_{1},v_{2}]).

Note that the magnetic symplectic form has the
G
-invariant expression in (
4.12
). Let
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
. The differential of the moment map
P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\xi
at
(g,X)
, applied to a tangent vector
(v,\delta X)
, produces the following:

(4.13)

\displaystyle dP_{(g,X)}(v,\delta X)=\left.\dfrac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}P(g\exp(tv),X+t\delta X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)([v,\xi]+\delta X)

since
P((g\exp(tv),X))=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\bigl(\exp(t( \mathrm{ad}v)\xi)\bigr)
(with
X
fixed) and
P([g,X+t\,\delta X])=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(\xi+t\,\delta X)
(with
g
fixed). Note that
dP\in T\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{g}
as
v\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
[v,\xi]\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\delta X\in\mathfrak{m}
, hence
[v,\xi]+\delta X\in\mathfrak{g}
. On the other hand, for any
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
, consider the local coordinates
g_{*}|_{X}\left(v,w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right)
used in (
4.12
). Similar to what we did in (
4.13
), simple computation shows that the differential at
(g,X)
is

\displaystyle d(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}})_{(g,X)}\left(g_{*}|_{X}\left(v,w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right)\right)=w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\in T_{\xi}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\cong\mathfrak{m}.

Since
\theta
is
A
-invariant and the
B
-gradient
\nabla\theta(\xi)\in\mathfrak{m}
is characterised by
d\theta(\xi)\left(\left(v,w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right)\right)=B(\nabla\theta(\xi),w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X])
, we then have

(4.14)

\displaystyle d(\theta\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}})_{(g,X)}\left(g_{*}|_{X}\left(v,w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right)\right)=B\left(\nabla\theta(\xi),w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right).

In the following lemma, we will provide the Hamiltonian vector fields of the moment map
P
and, therefore, present the twisted Poisson bracket relations of the linear first integrals.

Lemma 4.9
.

(
20
, Proposition 3)

Let
G
and
A\subset G
be the same as defined above. Let
P:T^{*}M\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be defined by
P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr)
as the magnetic moment map. For
\eta\in\mathfrak{g}
, define

(4.15)

\displaystyle P_{\eta}(g,X):=\langle P(g,X),\eta\rangle,\qquad P_{k}:=P_{\eta_{k}}\quad\text{ with }k=1,\dots,\dim\mathfrak{g}.

Then

(4.16)

\{P_{\eta},P_{\eta^{\prime}}\}_{\varepsilon}=P_{[\eta,{\eta^{\prime}}]}\quad\text{ with }\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}.

Proof.

Write
\xi:=X-\varepsilon W
and left-trivialize
T^{*}M\cong G\times_{A}\mathfrak{m}
. From (
4.15
), we have

\displaystyle P_{\eta}(g,X)=\langle P(g,X),\eta\rangle=B(P^{\sharp}(g,X),\eta),\quad\text{ with }P^{\sharp}(g,X):=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\,\xi.

Using (
4.13
), differentiating
P_{\eta}(g,X)
in the direction
(v,\delta X)
gives:

(4.17)

\displaystyle dP_{\eta}\left(g_{*}|_{X}\left(v,w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right)\right)=

\displaystyle\,B\left(\nabla P_{\eta}(g,X),\mathrm{Ad}(g)\left(\left[v,\xi+w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right]\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,B\left(w,\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta)\right)-B\left(v,[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta,\zeta]\right).

By definition, the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{P_{\eta}}
is characterised by
\iota_{X_{P_{\eta}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}=dP_{\eta}
. The explicit expression of
X_{P_{\eta}}
at
(g,X)
is of the form
g_{*}|_{X}\bigl(v_{\eta},-\frac{1}{2}[v_{\eta},X]+w_{\eta}\bigr)
. Using (
4.12
), without loss of generality, we find that for any
(0,w)\in\mathfrak{m}\times\mathfrak{m}
,
\omega_{\varepsilon}(X_{P_{\eta}},g_{*}|_{X}(0,w))=dP_{\eta}(g_{*}|_{X}(0,w))
. Together with (
4.17
), we deduce

\displaystyle-B(w,v_{\eta})=B\left(w,\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta)\right)\Longrightarrow v_{\eta}=-\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta\bigr)_{\mathfrak{m}},

where
(\cdot)_{\mathfrak{m}}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{m}
is the
B
-orthogonal projection onto
\mathfrak{m}
. Moreover, the coordinate form of
\omega_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.17
) implies that

(4.18)

\displaystyle-B(w_{\eta},v)+\varepsilon\,B\big(W,[v_{\eta},v]\big)=-B\big(v,[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta,\xi]\big).

\mathrm{Ad}
-invariance of
B
gives
B(W,[v_{\eta},v])=-B([W,v_{\eta}],v)
. Then the identity (
4.18
) becomes

\displaystyle B\big(w_{\eta}+[\varepsilon W,v_{\eta}]-[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta,\xi],\,v\big)=0\quad\text{ for all }v\in\mathfrak{m},

which yields, after substituting
v_{\eta}=-(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta)_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
,

\displaystyle w_{\eta}=[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta,\xi]_{\mathfrak{m}}+\varepsilon[W,(\eta)_{\mathfrak{m}}]=[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}-\varepsilon[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta,W]_{\mathfrak{m}}+\varepsilon\big[(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta)_{\mathfrak{m}},X\big]_{\mathfrak{m}}=[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}.

Note that the last equality is deduced by observing
[\left(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta\right)_{\mathfrak{a}},W]=0
and
[W,\left(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta\right)_{\mathfrak{m}}]=-[\left(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta\right)_{\mathfrak{m}},W]
Thus,

(4.19)

\displaystyle X_{P_{\eta}}(g,X)=g_{*}|_{X}\Big(-(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta)_{\mathfrak{m}},\,[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}-\frac{1}{2}\big[(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta)_{\mathfrak{m}},X\big]_{\mathfrak{m}}\Big),

which is exactly the fundamental vector field of the left
G
-action for the magnetic form
\omega_{\varepsilon}
. Using (
4.19
), the twisted Poisson bracket is

\displaystyle\{P_{\eta},P_{\eta}\}_{\varepsilon}(g,X)=dP_{\eta}\big(X_{P_{\eta}}(g,X)\big)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}P_{\eta}\big(\Phi^{\eta}_{t}(g,X)\big),

where
\Phi^{\eta}_{t}
is the flow of
X_{P_{\eta}}
, i.e., the left action by
\exp(t\eta)
. Along this flow,

\displaystyle P^{\sharp}\bigl(\Phi_{t}^{\eta}(g,X)\bigr)=\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(-t\eta))P^{\sharp}(g,X),\qquad P\bigl(\Phi_{t}^{\eta}(g,X)\bigr)=\mathrm{Ad}^{*}(\exp(t\eta))P(g,X).

So,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}P_{\eta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{t}^{\eta}(g,X)\bigr)=

\displaystyle\,\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\langle\mathrm{Ad}^{*}(\exp(t\eta))P(g,X),\eta^{\prime}\rangle=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\langle P(g,X),\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(t\eta))(\eta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\langle P(g,X),\,[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]\rangle=P_{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]}(g,X),

where the last equality uses
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariance and the definition of
P_{\cdot}
. This proves (
4.16
).
∎

Remark 4.10
.

(i) Let
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)\subset C^{\infty}(T^{*}M)
denote the real algebraic polynomial functions on
T^{*}M
. That is,
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M):=\left(\mathbb{R}[G]\otimes S(\mathfrak{m})\right)^{A}\cong\mathbb{R}[G\times\mathfrak{m}]^{A}
. Let
P:T^{*}M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be the left moment map defined in Definition
4.5
. For any
h\in S(\mathfrak{g})
with
I_{h}:=h\circ P
, the pullback
P^{*}:S(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
is a Poisson algebra homomorphism:

\displaystyle I_{\{h_{1},h_{2}\}}=\{I_{h_{1}},I_{h_{2}}\}_{\varepsilon}.

In particular, for linear functions
h_{\eta}(X)=B(\eta,X)
, one deduces from
(
20
, Proposition 3)
that

\displaystyle\{P_{\eta},P_{\eta^{\prime}}\}_{\varepsilon}=P_{[\eta,\eta^{\prime}]},\qquad P_{\eta}:=h_{\eta}\circ P.

(ii) From
20
, the Hamiltonian vector fields are
X_{P_{u}}(g,X)
in (
4.19
) and

(4.20)

\displaystyle X_{f_{\theta}}(g,X)=g_{*}|_{X}\left((\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},-\frac{1}{2}[(\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},X]_{\mathfrak{m}}-\varepsilon[W,(\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}]\right).

In what follows, let
\xi:=X-\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. By construction, there exists a
W\in\mathfrak{g}
that generates the adjoint orbit
M=\mathcal{O}_{W}
. Note that the orbit
\mathrm{Ad}(G)\xi
is usually larger than
M
. We first clarify two polynomial families that are given in
20
as the integrals of motion defined, which are denoted by
F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, respectively. This consists of the polynomial functions in
T^{*}M
obtained by pulling back the polynomial functions in
\mathfrak{g}
through the moment map. Equivalently,
F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\{h\circ P:h\in S(\mathfrak{g})\}
, where
h\circ P\in F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is determined by its restriction to
P(T^{*}M)
. On the other hand, the polynomial integrals come from
A
-invariant polynomials evaluated at the shifted element
X-\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
, denoted by
F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. In particular, from Lemma
4.7
and Remark
4.8
, for each
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
, we define

(4.21)

\displaystyle(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta)(g,X):=\theta\bigl(\tau_{-\varepsilon W}(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X))\bigr)=\theta\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr).

Since
\theta
is
A
-invariant and
W
is
\mathrm{Ad}(A)
-fixed, the right hand side of (
4.21
) is well-defined on
T^{*}M
. We then define
F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\bigl(S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\bigr)
. Moreover, the
A
-action on
G\times\mathfrak{m}
is
(g,X)\cdot a=(ga,\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X)
. Since
W
is
\mathrm{Ad}(A)
-fixed,
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
transforms to
\xi\mapsto\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})\xi
. Thus,

\displaystyle P((ga,\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X))=\mathrm{Ad}(ga)\big(\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X-\varepsilon W\big)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\big(X-\varepsilon W\big)=P((g,X)),

so
P
is
A
-invariant and descends to a well-defined smooth map on the quotient
P:\,(G\times\mathfrak{m})/A\cong T^{*}M\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}
.

Hence, based on the previous discussion, we summaries all the results above into the following definition.

Definition 4.11
.

(4.22)

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\left\{f\circ P:f\in S(\mathfrak{g})\right\},\quad F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\left\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}\circ\theta|_{\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W}:\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\right\}.

In what follows, we first prove that the sets introduced in Definition
4.11
are preserved by the magnetic geodesic flow. After establishing conservation, we return to their algebraic realization as pullbacks and to a clear notation for the resulting polynomial Poisson algebras. Now, we denote the finitely generated algebras

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\textbf{Alg}\left\langle F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right\rangle\text{ and }\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\textbf{Alg}\left\langle F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right\rangle

with the pullback of the moment map
P
and the projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
, it is clear that they are subalgebras of
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
. We now show that the polynomials from the
P
and
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
-pullbacks are conserved with respect to the Hamiltonian in (
4.8
).

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
\xi=X-\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. For every
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})
,
\{P^{*}f,\mathcal{H}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
.

Proof.

For any
\eta\in\mathfrak{g}
, by the definition of the Hamiltonian action, we have
\iota_{\hat{\eta}}\omega_{\varepsilon}=d\langle P,\eta\rangle
. Using
P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, for all
(g,X)\in T^{*}M
, we rewrite
\langle P,\eta\rangle=B(\mathrm{Ad}(g)(\xi),\eta)=B(\xi,\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\eta)
. Thus,
P_{\eta}=\langle P,\eta\rangle
generates the fundamental vector field
\hat{\eta}
. Since the left
G
-action on
T^{*}M
is Hamiltonian with the magnetic moment map
P
(under
B
), and
\mathcal{H}(g,X)=\frac{1}{2}B(X,X)
is left-invariant, we have

\mathcal{L}_{\hat{\eta}}\mathcal{H}=\{d\langle P,\eta\rangle,\mathcal{H}\}_{\varepsilon}=0.

By linearity and Leibniz’s rule, every polynomial
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})
yields a conserved function
P^{*}f
. So,
P^{*}f
is indeed the first integral of
\mathcal{H}
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

By Proposition
4.12
,
\{P^{*}f,P^{*}h\}_{\varepsilon}=P^{*}\{f,h\}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Hence,
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is not an Abelian Poisson algebra unless
\mathfrak{g}
is Abelian.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(g(t),X(t))
be the solution of the Hamilton equations (
4.8
) for
\mathcal{H}
with respect to
\omega_{\varepsilon}
. Then there exists a curve
\Omega(t):=\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)=X(t)-\varepsilon W
satisfies

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)=[\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t),\Omega(t)],

where
\dot{f}(t)=\dfrac{d}{dt}f(t)
for any smooth function
f
. In particular,
\dot{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)\in T_{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)}\big(\mathrm{Ad}(A)\cdot\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)\big)
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.11
,
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X)=X-\varepsilon W
, so
\dot{\pi}_{m}=\dot{X}
as
W
is fixed along any trajectories. To compute
\dot{X}
, we use the explicit form of the magnetic symplectic structure at
(g,X)\in T^{*}M
. Let
X_{\mathcal{H}}
denote the Hamiltonian vector field of
\mathcal{H}
. Recall that the local coordinate expression is given by

(4.23)

\displaystyle X_{\mathcal{H}}(g,X)=g_{*}|_{X}\left(v_{H},\,-\frac{1}{2}[v_{H},X]+w_{H}\right),\quad v_{H},w_{H}\in\mathfrak{m}.

Then, with the magnetic symplectic form in (
4.12
), the magnetic Hamilton’s equation
d\mathcal{H}=\iota_{X_{\mathcal{H}}}\omega_{\varepsilon}
evaluated at an arbitrary point
g_{*}|_{X}\bigl(v,-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]+w\bigr)\in T^{*}(T^{*}M)
gives

(4.24)

\displaystyle B(w,v_{H})-B(w_{H},v)+\varepsilon\,B\left(W,\,[v,v_{H}]\right)\,=\,d\mathcal{H}(g,X)\left(g_{*}|_{X}\bigl(v,-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]+w\bigr)\right).

Since
\mathcal{H}(g,X)=\frac{1}{2}B(X,X)
depends only on
X
and is quadratic, (
4.23
) becomes

\displaystyle d\mathcal{H}(g,X)\left(g_{*}|_{X}\bigl(v,-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]+w\bigr)\right)=B(X,w).

Therefore, for all
v,w\in\mathfrak{m}
, comparing the identity of
d\mathcal{H}
gives

(4.25)

\displaystyle B(w,v_{H})

\displaystyle=B(X,w),

(4.26)

\displaystyle-B(w_{H},v)

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\,B\left(W,\,[v,v_{H}]\right).

From (
4.25
), we obtain
v_{H}=X
. Substituting into (
4.26
) and using
B
-invariance
B(W,[v,X])=-B(v,[W,X])=B(v,[X,W])
,

\displaystyle-B(w_{H},v)+\varepsilon\,B\left(W,\,[v,X]\right)

\displaystyle=-B(w_{H},v)+\varepsilon\,B\left(v,\,[X,W]\right)\,=\,0\qquad\text{ for all }v\in\mathfrak{m}.

Hence,
w_{H}=\varepsilon[X,W]=-\varepsilon[W,X]\in\mathfrak{m}
. Consequently, with all the discussion above, (
4.23
) becomes
X_{\mathcal{H}}(g,X)=g_{*}|_{X}\bigl(X,\,-\varepsilon[W,X]\bigr)
, and the Hamilton equations are

(4.27)

\displaystyle\dot{g}=gX,\quad\dot{X}=-\varepsilon[W,X].

Finally, using (
4.27
)

\displaystyle\dot{\pi}_{m}=\dot{X}=-\varepsilon[W,X]=[X-\varepsilon W,\varepsilon W]=[\pi_{\mathfrak{m}},\Omega],

with the (constant) choice
\Omega(t)\equiv\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{g}
. This shows both the bracket form and that
\dot{\pi}_{m}
is tangent to the
A
-orbit through
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
as required.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

For every
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
,
\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta,\mathcal{H}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
.

Proof.

Since
\theta
is
A
-invariant on the affine space
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
, its differential annihilates the tangent vectors to
A
-orbits. That is, for all
\xi
and
\eta\in\mathfrak{a}
, differentiating
\theta
along the flow gives

\displaystyle 0=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\theta\big(\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(t\eta))\xi\big)=B\big(\nabla\theta(\xi),[\eta,\xi]\big)=B\big([\nabla\theta(\xi),\xi],\eta\big)

with
\xi=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(0)
and
\dot{\xi}=[\xi,\Omega]
. By Lemma
4.14
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\theta\big(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)\big)=B\big(\nabla\theta(\xi),[\xi,\Omega]\big)=B\big([\nabla\theta(\xi),\xi],\Omega\big)=0.

Hence,
\theta\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is constant along the flow. Equivalently,
\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta,\mathcal{H}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

Similar to Remark
4.13
, we do
not
claim
\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}h_{1},\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}h_{2}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
for all
h_{i}
with
i=1,2
. In general,
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is not Poisson-commutative.

To study the algebro-geometric description of the algebra generated by these finitely-generating sets
F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, we need to first show that
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
are Poisson algebras, and then describe the spectrum of these two Poisson algebras. Starting with the following lemma:

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
be the sets defined in (
4.22
). Then the polynomials by the pullbacks of
P
and
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
induce the following subalgebras of
\mathcal{O}((T^{*}M))
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}(S(\mathfrak{g})),\quad\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\bigl(S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\bigr),

Here
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta:=\theta\circ(\tau_{-\varepsilon W}\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}})
. In particular, the pullback of
P
is surjective, and the pullback of
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is injective.

Proof.

For
(g,X)\in T^{*}M\cong(G\times\mathfrak{m})/A
, by Definition
4.5
,

\displaystyle\xi:=X-\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W,\quad P(g,X):=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\,\xi,\quad\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X):=\xi.

We now provide the construction of these two algebras case by case.

By the definition of
F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
in (
4.22
), the generators in
F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
are of the form
(h\circ P)(g,X)=h\bigl(P(g,X)\bigr)
with
h\in S(\mathfrak{g})
. Equivalently,
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\textbf{Alg}\left\langle F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right\rangle
is the image of the algebra homomorphism given by the pullback
P^{*}
as follows:

\displaystyle P^{*}:S(\mathfrak{g})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M),\quad h\longmapsto h\circ P.

A direct calculation shows that
P^{*}
preserves sums and products, its image is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
, and
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}\bigl(S(\mathfrak{g})\bigr)
. On the other hand, by the definition of
F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
in (
4.22
) and (
4.21
), every element of
F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is of the form
\theta(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X))=\theta(X-\varepsilon X)
with
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. Thus, similar to the construction of
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, we find that
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\textbf{Alg}\left\langle F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right\rangle
is the image of the algebra homomorphism

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}:S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M),\qquad\theta\longmapsto\theta\circ(\tau_{-\varepsilon W}\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}),

which again preserves sums and products. Hence, its image is a subalgebra and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\bigl(S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\bigr)
. We then show that
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}
is injective. For any
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
, assume that
\theta\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}=0
. Then
\theta(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X))=\theta(X-\varepsilon W)=0
. Hence
\theta=0
, which implies that
\ker\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}=0
. Therefore,
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}
is injective.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

(i) Note that this isomorphism is canonical, as
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
is the Poisson subalgebra. For any real algebraic morphism
P:T^{*}M\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, the kernel of the pullback
P^{*}:S(\mathfrak{g})\cong\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
is the vanishing ideal of the real Zariski closure of the image:
\mathcal{I}(\overline{P((g,X))})
for all
(g,X)\in T^{*}M
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\overline{P((g,X))}):=\left\{f\in S(\mathfrak{g}):f(P(g,X))=0,\text{ for all }(g,X)\in T^{*}M\right\}=\ker(P^{*})

Hence, by the first isomorphism theorem, the image algebra is given by the following isomorphism

(4.28)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}(P^{*})\cong\frac{S(\mathfrak{g})}{\mathcal{I}(\overline{P(g,X)})}\cong\mathbb{R}[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)}]:=S[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)}].

Here,
S[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)}]
is the coordinate ring of the affine variety
\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
. Thus,
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\mathrm{Im}(P^{*})
is canonically Poisson isomorphic to the real coordinate ring of the closed subvariety
\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. For the rest of this manuscript, for abbreviation, we write
\mathbb{R}[\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)]
to mean
\mathbb{R}[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)}]
.

(ii) Although
\mathfrak{m}
is a vector space, in general, it is not a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. Therefore,
S(\mathfrak{m})
does not admit a Lie-Poisson structure induced from
S(\mathfrak{g})
. In order to define a Poisson structure on it, we transport the Poisson bracket from
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
to
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
via the injective pullback
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}
, and denote the resulting bracket by
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{2}
(see Proposition
4.19
below).

We now show that
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
defined in Lemma
4.17
are Poisson subalgebras of
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
.

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
h_{1},h_{2}\in S(\mathfrak{g})
and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. Then

\displaystyle\{P^{*}h_{1},P^{*}h_{2}\}_{\varepsilon}=P^{*}\left(\{h_{1},h_{2}\}_{1}\right)\text{ and }\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{1},\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{2}\}_{\varepsilon}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\bigl(\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}_{2}\bigr),

where
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{1}=\{\cdot,\cdot\}
is a Lie-Poisson bracket and
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{2}
is the Poisson bracket on
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
given by

\displaystyle\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}_{2}(\xi):=-B\left(\xi,\bigl[(\nabla\theta_{1}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},(\nabla\theta_{2}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}\bigr]\right),\qquad\xi\in\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W.

In particular,
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}

is a Poisson subalgebra of
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
.

Proof.

Fix
(g,X)\in T^{*}M
and put
\xi:=X-\varepsilon W
. For
i=1,2
, write
v_{i}:=(\nabla\theta_{i}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}
and let
X_{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{i}}
be as in (
4.21
). Using the
G
-invariant expression of
\omega_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{1},\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{2}\}_{\varepsilon}(g,X)=

\displaystyle\,\omega_{\varepsilon}\bigl(X_{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{1}},X_{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-B(X,[v_{1},v_{2}])+\varepsilon B(W,[v_{1},v_{2}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-B(X-\varepsilon W,[v_{1},v_{2}])=-B\left(\xi,\bigl[v_{1},v_{2}\bigr]\right),

which depends only on
\xi
and is
A
-invariant because each
\theta_{i}
is
A
-invariant. Hence, it lies in
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. This proves the formula and closure.
∎

From Proposition
4.19
, we see that the first family is built from the pullback of
P
, the second is from the pullback of
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
, and the
A
-invariants on
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. We then define the following Poisson polynomial algebras.

Definition 4.20
.

From Lemma
4.17
, we introduce the notation for Poisson polynomial algebras

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=P^{*}S(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\big(S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\big).

We now have a look at the Poisson commutative relations between
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. As all generators
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
are left
G
-invariant, we deduce the following lemma.

Lemma 4.21
.

For
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})
and
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\{P^{*}f,\ \pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta\}_{\varepsilon}=0.

That is,
\left\{\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right\}_{\varepsilon}=0
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be the canonical pairing
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. For any
u\in\mathfrak{g}
, recall that the linear functional on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by
u\mapsto\ell_{u}
, with
\ell_{u}(\eta):=\langle\eta,u\rangle
. Since
S(\mathfrak{g})\cong\mathbb{R}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
, for any
f\in S(\mathfrak{g})
, we have

\displaystyle f(\eta)=F\big(\ell_{u_{1}}(\eta),\ldots,\ell_{u_{k}}(\eta)\big),\qquad\eta\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}.

For
u\in\mathfrak{g}
, from (
4.10
), we could set

\displaystyle P_{u}:=\ell_{u}\circ P=\langle P,u\rangle\in\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M).

The pullback of
P
gives

(4.29)

\displaystyle P^{*}f=f\circ P=F\big(P_{u_{1}},\ldots,P_{u_{k}}\big).

We first show that the linear components in (
4.29
) satisfy the argument. By Lemma
4.7
,
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta
is
G
-invariant. That is,
(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta)(L_{a}\cdot(g,X))=\theta(X)=(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta)((g,X))
, where
L_{a}
denotes the left-translation. Hence
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta(\exp(tu)\cdot(g,X))=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta(g,X)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that here we use the conventions
\iota_{X_{f}}\omega_{\varepsilon}=df
and
\iota_{\hat{u}}\omega_{\varepsilon}=d\langle P,u\rangle
.
Hence,
X_{P_{u}}=\widehat{u}
. Therefore, for any
u\in\mathfrak{g}
, by the definition of the Lie derivative,

(4.30)

\displaystyle\{P_{u},\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta\}_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{\varepsilon}(X_{P_{u}},X_{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta})=d(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta)(\hat{u})=\mathcal{L}_{\hat{u}}(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta)=0.

Here, in (
4.30
), we use the Cartan formula
\mathcal{L}_{\hat{u}}=\iota_{\hat{u}}d+d\iota_{\hat{u}}
and the fact that
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta
is
G
-invariant. We now focus on the higher degree components. Note that the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\varepsilon}
is bilinear and has a derivation in each slot. That is,

\displaystyle\{fg,h\}_{\varepsilon}=f\{g,h\}_{\varepsilon}+g\{f,h\}_{\varepsilon},\qquad\{f,g+h\}_{\varepsilon}=\{f,g\}_{\varepsilon}+\{f,h\}_{\varepsilon}.

Moreover, from the discussion in the previous paragraph,
S(\mathfrak{g})
is generated by the linear functionals
\ell_{u}
in sums and products. Since
\{P_{u_{i}},\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta\}_{\varepsilon}=0
for all
i
, an induction on the total degree of
F
by using Leibniz and linearity, yields

\displaystyle\{\,F(P_{u_{1}},\ldots,P_{u_{k}}),\ \pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta\,\}_{\varepsilon}=0.

Hence, we conclude

\displaystyle\{\,P^{*}f,\ \pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta\,\}_{\varepsilon}=0\qquad\text{ for all }\,f\in S(\mathfrak{g}),\ \theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}

as required.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

By the definition of
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, let
P_{u}=\ell_{u}\circ P
and
f_{\theta}=\theta\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Then, from
20
, the Hamiltonian vector fields are
X_{P_{u}}(g,X)
in (
4.19
) and

(4.31)

\displaystyle X_{f_{\theta}}(g,X)=g_{*}|_{X}\left((\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},-\frac{1}{2}[(\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},X]_{\mathfrak{m}}-\varepsilon[W,(\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}]\right).

A direct calculation shows that
\{P_{u},f_{\theta}\}_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{\varepsilon}(X_{P_{u}},X_{f_{\theta}})=0
.

In Lemma
4.14
, we see that
F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is also the set of first integrals as the curve
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)=X(t)-\varepsilon W
evolves by
\dot{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}=[\pi_{\mathfrak{m}},\,\Omega(t)]
with
\Omega(t)\equiv\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{g}
. Hence,
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)
remains on its
A
-orbit. Therefore, every
A
-invariant polynomial
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
is constant along the flow and
\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta,\mathcal{H}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
by Proposition
4.15
. In conclusion, with all the discussions above, we now present two families of the first integrals in the phase space
T^{*}M
with magnetic twists. This can be concluded into the following theorem:

Theorem 4.23
.

In
\big(T^{*}M,\omega_{\varepsilon}\big)
with
\mathcal{H}(g,X)=\frac{1}{2}B(X,X)
, the two polynomial families

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}S(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\big(S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\big)

consist of first integrals:

\displaystyle\{\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathcal{H}\}_{\varepsilon}=0,\qquad\{\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathcal{H}\}_{\varepsilon}=0.

Remark 4.24
.

When
W
is irregular,
P^{*}
may have a non-trivial kernel as its image lies in the cone
\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
. In contrast,
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is surjective, hence
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}
is injective on
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\cong S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. Thus,
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
provides a block of conserved quantities independent of the regularity of
W
.

4.2.
Poisson projection chains and superintegrabilities on
T^{*}(G/A)

In this Subsection
4.2
, we provide the full superintegrability on
T^{*}(G/A)
by verifying the Poisson projection chains starting from
T^{*}(G/A)
. We first state the regularity of the subset on which we are working. Let
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\mathfrak{g}
denote the set of regular elements. That is, the elements whose centraliser has a dimension
\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{g}
. Since
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}
is Zariski open and dense, its complement defines the zero locus of a nonzero polynomial Let
f:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathbb{R}
be the polynomial whose nonvanishing locus is the set of regular elements
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}:f(\xi)\neq 0\}
. For fixed
W\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}/\{0\}
, define the open dense subsets

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{W}:=\left\{X\in\mathfrak{m}:\xi=X-\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}\right\},\qquad U:=\left\{(g,X)\in T^{*}(G/A):X\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{W}\right\}.

Thus,
U\subset T^{*}M
is Zariski open and dense. In the complement of
U
, the rank of the differential of
P
and
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
can drop. Hence, all the geometric statements in the section below require regularity.

Remark 4.25
.

The phase space is the symplectic manifold
\big(T^{*}M,\omega_{\varepsilon}\big)
. The set
U
is an open dense submanifold when constant rank or independence of differentials is required. Working on
U
does not affect the integrability:
U
is a symplectic submanifold, and the restrictions of
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
to
U
have the same functional relations as in
T^{*}M
.

We now show that working on
U
does not change any geometric or algebraic properties of
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
Z_{P}:=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
and
(Z_{P})_{\mathrm{reg}}:=Z_{P}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}=\mathrm{Ad}(G)\big(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{W}\big)
. Then
(Z_{P})_{\mathrm{reg}}
is the Zariski open dense in
Z_{P}
and

\displaystyle\bigl\{f|_{Z_{P}}:f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]\bigr\}=\left\{f|_{(Z_{P})_{\mathrm{reg}}}:f\in\mathbb{R}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]\right\}.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}
is Zariski open dense,
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{W}
is Zariski open dense in
\mathfrak{m}
. Define the map
\phi:G\times\mathfrak{m}\xrightarrow{\ \rho\ }(G\times\mathfrak{m})/T\xrightarrow{\ P\ }\mathfrak{g}
by
\phi(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(X-\varepsilon W)
for all
g\in G
. By construction,
\phi
is a regular morphism of affine varieties. Hence, the maps
\rho
,
P
, and
\phi
are continuous. Since
\phi
is regular and
(G\times\mathfrak{m}_{W}^{\mathrm{reg}})/A
is Zariski open dense in
T^{*}M
, we have
\phi\left((G\times\mathfrak{m}_{W}^{\mathrm{reg}})/A\right)=\phi(T^{*}M)=Z_{P}
. Hence,
\overline{(Z_{P})_{\mathrm{reg}}}=\overline{Z_{P}}
.
∎

Remark 4.27
.

On the regular stratum

(4.33)

\displaystyle U=\{(g,X)\in G\times_{A}\mathfrak{m}:\xi=X-\varepsilon W\ \text{is regular in }\mathfrak{g}\}\subset T^{*}M,

Lemma
4.26
states that
\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{W})
is Zariski dense in
\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
. All algebraic constructions for finding the finitely generated algebras
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, and their intersection and union, are unchanged by replacing
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
with
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{reg}}^{W}
. However, differential arguments, such as constant ranks of functions on the intermediate Poisson manifold or variety
\mathcal{P}_{2n-k}
, are based on
U
.

Define the shift restriction by

(4.34)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{W}:S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\longrightarrow S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}\text{ given by }\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(X)=C(X-\varepsilon W)

for any
X\in\mathfrak{m}
. We have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Res}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowright}

We first show that the restriction map is well-defined in the following lemma:

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
be the restriction mapping defined above. Then
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
is a well-defined
\mathbb{R}
-algebra homomorphism. In particular,
\mathrm{ImRes}_{W}
is a subalgebra of
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.7
,
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}\cong S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. For any
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
, the composition
C\circ\tau_{\varepsilon W}\in S(\mathfrak{m})
. Hence,
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)\in S(\mathfrak{m})
. Note that
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)
is also
A
-invariant. Indeed, for any non-zero
a\in A
, by the definition of the Poisson centraliser,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(\mathrm{Ad}(a)X)=C(\mathrm{Ad}(a)X-\varepsilon W)=C(\mathrm{Ad}(a)(X-\varepsilon W))=C(X-\varepsilon W)=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(X).

Hence,
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
.

We now show that
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)
is a
\mathbb{R}
-algebra isomorphism. This can be verified as follows: For any
C,C^{\prime}\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{W}(CC^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\,CC^{\prime}\circ\tau_{\varepsilon W}=\left(C\circ\tau_{\varepsilon W}\right)\left(C^{\prime}\circ\tau_{\varepsilon W}\right)=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C+\lambda C^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\,(C+\lambda C^{\prime})\circ\tau_{\varepsilon W}=\left(C\circ\tau_{\varepsilon W}+\lambda C^{\prime}\circ\tau_{\varepsilon W}\right)=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)+\lambda\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C^{\prime}).

Therefore, it follows that
\mathrm{ImRes}_{W}
is a subalgebra of
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
.
∎

Remark 4.29
.

Fix
W\in\mathfrak{g}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}/\{0\}
. By Lemma
4.7
, we define the affine translation

\displaystyle\tau_{-\varepsilon W}:\mathfrak{m}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W,\quad X\longmapsto X-\varepsilon W.

For
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
, Lemma
4.28
shows that
\mathrm{ImRes}_{W}
is a subalgebra and
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(X):=C(X-\varepsilon W)=C\big(\tau_{-\varepsilon W}(X)\big)
.

By Lemma
4.28
,
C|_{\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W}\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. By Lemma
4.7
(ii), the pullback along
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}
induces an isomorphism of invariant algebras via its pullback as follows:

\displaystyle\tau_{-\varepsilon W}^{*}:S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }S(\mathfrak{m})^{A},\quad(\tau_{-\varepsilon W}^{*}\theta)(X)=\theta(X-\varepsilon W).

With this notation
\mathrm{Res}_{W}=\tau_{-\varepsilon W}^{*}\circ\big(C\mapsto C|_{\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W}\big)
, we may regard
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
as a map
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\to S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. The choice of
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
as the codomain is simply the identification of the affine slice
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
with the linear space
\mathfrak{m}
via
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}
.

If
\xi=X-\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
, then
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(X)=C(\xi)
. Therefore, whenever expressions such as
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(\xi)
appear later, they are to be understood through the identification
\mathfrak{m}\xrightarrow{\tau_{-\varepsilon W}\ }\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
.

From the construction in Subsection
3.2
, we see that
\mathfrak{g}//G
is the spectrum of the Poisson center
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
. Choosing homogeneous generators
C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
, we define a canonical quotient map

(4.35)

\displaystyle\chi:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}//G\cong\mathbb{A}^{r},\ \text{ }\zeta\longmapsto(C_{1}(\zeta),\ldots,C_{r}(\zeta)).

By the definition of the point in
\mathfrak{g}//A
, defined in (
3.19
), for a non-zero regular value
\zeta\in\mathfrak{g}
, the fiber
\chi^{-1}\left(\chi(\zeta)\right)
exactly defines the adjoint orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\zeta}
and
\dim\mathcal{O}_{\zeta}=n-r
. That is, for a generic value
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})\in\mathrm{Im}\,\chi_{Z_{P}}
, the fiber

\displaystyle\left(\chi|_{Z_{P}}\right)^{-1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{r})

is a dimension
n-r
adjoint orbit. We first deduce the rank of the subalgebra
\mathrm{ImRes}_{W}
from the following proposition.

Proposition 4.30
.

Let
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
be the same as defined above. Suppose that
C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}
are algebraically independent generators of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
such that
\chi:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r}
is given by
\chi(\zeta)=\left(C_{1}(\zeta),\ldots,C_{r}(\zeta)\right)
. Here,
\mathbb{A}^{r}
is a
r
-dimensional affine space. Define
s:=\mathrm{trdeg}\,\left(\mathrm{ImRes}_{W}\right)
and suppose that
C
restricts to an algebraically independent set on
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. Then, for any
X\in\mathfrak{m}
such that
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,d(\mathrm{Res}_{W})_{X}=\dim\left(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}\right)_{\mathfrak{m}}=s,

where
(\cdot)_{\mathfrak{m}}
is the projection on the
\mathfrak{m}
-component.

Proof.

Fix
B
as a
G
-invariant Killing form on
G
. Recall that
B|_{\mathfrak{m}}
is also non-degenerate such that
T_{X}^{*}\mathfrak{m}\cong\mathfrak{m}
. We prove this argument by computing the Jacobian of
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
. We first show that the gradients of invariant polynomials lie in the centraliser. For any
\zeta,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
and
G
-invariant
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
, using the Ad-invariant property, we deduce

(4.36)

\displaystyle 0=\left.\dfrac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}C(\mathrm{Ad}(\exp(-tY))\zeta)=dC(\zeta)[Y,\zeta]=B(\nabla C(\zeta),[Y,\zeta])=B([\nabla C(\zeta),\zeta],Y).

Therefore, we observe that
dC_{i}(\zeta)
annihilates
T_{\zeta}(G\cdot\zeta)
. In addition, the last equality in (
4.36
) implies that
[\nabla C(\zeta),\zeta]=0
and
\nabla C(\zeta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{\zeta}
. Hence, via
B
, the differential
dC_{i}(\zeta)
corresponds to the gradient
\nabla C_{i}(\zeta)
defined by
dC(\zeta)(w)=B(\nabla C(\zeta),w)
for all
w\in\mathfrak{g}
. Without loss of generality, take
\zeta=\xi=X-\varepsilon W
. By definition,
T_{\xi}(G\cdot\xi)=[\mathfrak{g},\xi]:=\{[Y,\xi]:Y\in\mathfrak{g}\}
. Recall that the annihilator of this tangent space is given by
\widehat{[\mathfrak{g},\xi]}:=\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}:\text{ }\lambda([\mathfrak{g},\xi])=0\}
. For a
\lambda_{\nabla C(\xi)}\in\widehat{[\mathfrak{g},\xi]}
, let
\lambda_{\nabla C(\xi)}([\mathfrak{g},\xi])=B(\nabla C(\xi),[\mathfrak{g},\xi])
. Using (
4.36
) again, we obtain
\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}\cong\widehat{[\mathfrak{g},\xi]}
. Hence, the set of gradients
\{\nabla C_{i}(\xi)\}_{i}
forms the basis of
\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}
.

Now, consider the map

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathfrak{m}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{r},\quad X\longmapsto\varphi(X):=(\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{1})(X),\ldots,\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{r})(X))=\left((C_{1})|_{\mathfrak{m}},\ldots,(C_{r})|_{\mathfrak{m}}\right)

To find the rank of
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
, it is sufficient to compute the rank of
d\varphi_{X}
. For any
\delta X=u\in\mathfrak{m}
, the differential of the restriction map in (
4.36
) is given as follows:

(4.37)

\displaystyle d(\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{i}))_{X}(u)=dC_{i}(\xi)(u)=B(\nabla C_{i}(\xi),u)=B(\left(\nabla C_{i}(\xi)\right)_{\mathfrak{m}},u).

Thus, under the identification of
T_{X}^{*}\mathfrak{m}
with
\mathfrak{m}
, the covector
d\varphi_{i}|_{\xi}
is the vector
(\nabla C_{i}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathfrak{m}
. Equivalently, in any
B
-orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{m}
, the
i
-th row of the Jacobian matrix
J_{F}(\xi)\in M_{r\times\dim\mathfrak{m}}(\mathbb{R})
is the coordinate row of
(\nabla C_{i}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Since each
C_{i}
is
G
-invariant,
\nabla C_{i}(\xi)\in\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}:=\{Y\in\mathfrak{g}:[Y,\xi]=0\}
, and for regular
\xi
, the
\nabla C_{i}(\xi)
spans
\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}
for all
i
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathrm{span}\big\{(\nabla C_{i}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}\big\}_{i=1}^{r}=(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}}\subset\mathfrak{m}.

Using the
B
-invariance and (
4.37
), for any
X\in\mathfrak{m}
,

(4.38)

\displaystyle\ker d\varphi_{X}

\displaystyle=\big\{u\in\mathfrak{m}:B\big((\nabla C_{i}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},\,u\big)=0\ \text{ for all }i\big\}

\displaystyle=\big\{u\in\mathfrak{m}:B(Y,u)=0\text{ for all }Y\in(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}}\big\}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}\cap\big((\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})^{\perp}\big)=\mathfrak{m}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\xi].

Here, in the last line of (
4.38
), we use the orthogonal decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}\oplus[\mathfrak{g},\xi]
with respect to
B
, which is standard for regular
\xi
as
B\big([\mathfrak{g},\xi],\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}\big)=0
and
\dim\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}+\dim[\mathfrak{g},\xi]=\dim\mathfrak{g}
. Hence, inside
\mathfrak{m}
,
((\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}})^{\perp}=\mathfrak{m}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\xi]
. Therefore, by the rank-nullity theorem,

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,d\varphi_{X}=\dim\mathfrak{m}-\dim\big(\mathfrak{m}\cap[\mathfrak{g},\xi]\big)=\dim\big((\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}}\big)

as required.
∎

Remark 4.31
.

(i) Define
Z_{P}:=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
. Choose any finitely-generating set
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{s}\}\subset\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{Res}_{W})
and form the compressed map

\displaystyle\varphi^{(s)}:Z_{P}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{s},\qquad\varphi^{(s)}(\xi):=\bigl(u_{1}(\xi),\ldots,u_{s}(\xi)\bigr).

Then, generically,

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,d\varphi^{(s)}=s,\qquad\ker d\varphi=\ker d\varphi^{(s)}

due to the algebraic dependence on the generators in
\mathrm{Im}\,\varphi
.

(ii) For arbitrary
X\in\mathfrak{m}
with
\xi:=X-\varepsilon W
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,d(\mathrm{Res}_{W})_{X}\leq\dim\big((\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}}\big).

Indeed, the
i
-th row of the Jacobian of
(\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{1}),\ldots,\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{r}))
at
X

is
(\nabla C_{i}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}}
as
C_{i}
is
G
-invariant and hence

\nabla C_{i}(\xi)\in\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}
. Therefore, the row space is contained in
(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
giving the inequality. On the regular locus
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}
, the rows span

(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Thus, as presented in the proof above, the equality holds there. The locus where the rank is less than
s
is Zariski closed (the vanishing of the
s\times s
minors of the Jacobian) and is disjoint from a Zariski open dense subset of the regular slice.

Corollary 4.32
.

Let
A=T
such that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
. Then
\mathrm{Res}_{W}:S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}
is injective. In particular,
\mathrm{Res}_{W}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\right)\subset S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}
.

Proof.

By Chevalley’s theorem,
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\cong\mathbb{A}^{r}
. Hence, fix a finite set
\{C_{1},\dots,C_{r}\}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
such that
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}=\mathbb{R}[C_{1},\dots,C_{r}]
. By assumption,
A=T
and
W
is regular. Using Proposition
4.30
, the restricted elements
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{r})\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}
are algebraically independent.

Let
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
such that
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)=0
. Since
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}=\mathbb{R}[C_{1},\dots,C_{r}]
, there exists a polynomial
F\in\mathbb{R}[C_{1},\dots,C_{r}]
such that
C=F(C_{1},\dots,C_{r})
.
Applying
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
and Lemma
4.28
, we obtain

(4.39)

\displaystyle 0=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)=\mathrm{Res}_{W}\bigl(F(C_{1},\dots,C_{r})\bigr)=F\bigl(\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{r})\bigr)\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}.

Since
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{r})
are algebraically independent, the only polynomial
F
with
F(\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{1}),\dots,\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{r}))=0
is the zero polynomial. Hence,
F=0
, so
C=0
. Therefore,
\ker(\mathrm{Res}_{W})=\{0\}
and
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
is injective.
∎

Remark 4.33
.

Let us illustrate an example for the
A=T
case. Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(2)
, and
\mathfrak{t}\cong\mathbb{R}
be Cartan subalgebra. We further assume that
\{x,y,z\}
is the coordinate for
\mathfrak{su}^{*}(2)
such that
x
is the coordinate function on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
and
\{y,z\}
is the coordinate functions on
\mathfrak{m}^{*}
. Then the Casimir
C
is defined by
x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}
, and
S(\mathfrak{su}(2))^{\mathrm{SU}(2)}
and
S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}=\mathbb{R}[y^{2}+z^{2}]
. After the shifting, the restriction map is then given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{W}(\Omega)(y,z)=\Omega((0,y,z)-\varepsilon W)=x^{2}+y^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}||W||^{2}\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{T}\cong S(\mathfrak{m})^{T},

which is clearly injective.

We now determine the rank of
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, which helps us prove the dimensional characterization of superintegrable systems.

Lemma 4.34
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and let
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
be the same as defined above, and let
r=\mathrm{rank}\,G
. Let
\mathrm{Res}_{W}:S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
be the restriction given by
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(X)=C(X-\varepsilon W)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W),\quad\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//A\cong\mathfrak{m}//A.

In particular,

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}n&\text{ if }A=T\\
n-r+s&\text{ if }T\subsetneq A\end{matrix}\right.\text{ and }\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\dim\mathfrak{m}-\dim A+\dim A_{y},

where
\dim A_{y}
denotes the dimension of a generic stabiliser of
A
acting on
\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

Let
Z_{P}=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)=\bigcup_{X\in\mathfrak{m}}\mathrm{Ad}(G)(X-\varepsilon W)
. In particular,
Z_{P}
is
G
-invariant. That is,
\mathrm{Ad}(G)Z_{P}=Z_{P}
. We first look at the case where
A\neq T
. By the definition of
P
,
P^{*}:S(\mathfrak{g})\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is a ring homomorphism. By Lemma
4.17
and its remark, the pullback identifies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cong S(\mathfrak{g})\big/\mathcal{I}(Z_{P}).

Hence,
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=Z_{P}
2
2

2

Formally, we should write
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\overline{Z_{P}}
as a Zariski closed subset. However, recall that from Remark
4.18
, we will omit the Zariski closure.
, and therefore,
\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\dim Z_{P}
. It remains to compute
\dim Z_{P}
. By definition,
Z_{P}
consists of the adjoint orbit
\mathrm{Ad}(G)(X-\varepsilon W)
through the affine subspace
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. Let
\chi:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}//G\cong\mathbb{A}^{r}
be the canonical quotient defined by (
4.35
). Restrict
\chi
to
Z_{P}
and set
\Gamma:=\overline{\chi(Z_{P})}\subset\mathbb{A}^{r}
. Since
C_{j}\circ P=(\mathrm{Res}_{W}C_{j})\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
on
T^{*}M
, with
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X)=X-\varepsilon W
, the coordinate ring is identified as

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}[\Gamma]\cong\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{Res}_{W})\subset S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}

as
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}=\mathbb{R}[C_{1},\dots,C_{r}]
and we restrict the generators to
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. Hence,

\displaystyle\dim\Gamma=\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathbb{R}[\Gamma]=\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{Res}_{W})=s.

By Proposition
4.30
, on the regular locus
U
, we also have

\displaystyle s=\mathrm{rank}\,d(\mathrm{Res}_{W})(X)=\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}},

so
s
is the generic differential rank of
\mathrm{Res}_{W}
.

On the Zariski open dense regular locus
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, the Chevalley map
\chi
is smooth, with all fibers of dimension
n-r
, and
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}\cap Z_{P}\neq\emptyset
by Lemma
4.26
. Thus, for any
\xi\in Z_{P}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}
, the fiber
\chi^{-1}(\chi(\xi))=\mathrm{Ad}(G)\cdot\xi
has dimension
n-r
and lies in
Z_{P}
since
Z_{P}
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant. Therefore, the generic fiber of
\chi|_{Z_{P}}:Z_{P}\to\Gamma
has dimension
n-r
. By the dimension theorem for morphisms
(
25
, Chapter III, §9)
, we have

(4.40)

\dim Z_{P}=\dim\Gamma\ +\bigl(\text{generic fiber dimension of }\chi|_{Z_{P}}\bigr)\ =\ s+(n-r).

It follows that
\dim Z_{P}=n-(r-s)
. Since
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=Z_{P}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\dim Z_{P}=n-(r-s),

as claimed.

On the other hand, if
A=T
with
W
is regular, from the construction above, we find that the
G
-invariant generators
C_{1},\ldots,C_{r}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the restricted algebraically independent functions in
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{T}
. By Corollary
4.32
,
\chi(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
is Zariski dense in
\mathbb{A}^{r}
. In this case, by the definition of
\chi
, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma=\overline{\chi(Z_{P})}=\mathbb{A}^{r}\text{ and }s=\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathrm{Im\,Res}_{W}=r.

Hence, (
4.40
) becomes
\dim Z_{P}=r+(n-r)=n
. Since
\mathfrak{g}
is
n
-dimensional, any subset of dimension
n
must be Zariski dense. Hence,
Z_{P}
is Zariski dense in
\mathfrak{g}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\dim Z_{P}=n\text{ if }A=T.

We now focus on the spectrum of
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and its rank. The image of the map
P
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
, and thus its coordinate ring is equal to the quotient of
S(\mathfrak{g})
by the vanishing ideal of that cone. Since
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}
is injective, we have an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
with the invariant ring
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. Thus,
\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,(\mathfrak{m}//A)
. ∎

Remark 4.35
.

(i) When
W
is regular and
A=T
, as we presented in the proof, the restriction
\mathrm{Res}
is generically of full rank
s=r
. Hence,
\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=n
recovers the familiar regular case.

(ii) In the irregular case
s<r
, the adjoint image
Z_{P}
sits inside
\mathfrak{g}
with codimension
r-s
entirely accounted for by the invariant equations
C_{j}=\text{const}
, whose restrictions
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{j})
are algebraically dependent or constant on the slice. No extra non-invariant equations contribute to the codimension, as
\chi(Z_{P})=\Gamma
and the generic fiber of
\chi|_{Z_{P}}
already have the maximal orbit dimension
n-r
.

(iii) Later in Section
5
, we will see an example with
G=\mathrm{SU}(3)
and
A=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(1)\times\mathrm{U}(2))
. In this case, we have
n=8
,
r=2
, and
s=1
. Hence,
\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=8-(2-1)=7
. This matches the explicit coordinate computations and the adjoint-quotient dimension count in Lemma
4.34
.

In what follows, we focus on constructing the intersection and union of two algebras
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. Now, let us recap the basic terminology from algebraic geometry. See, for instance,
(
25
, Chapter II, §3)
Let
X_{1}:=\mathrm{Spec}\,A_{1}
and
X_{2}:=\mathrm{Spec}\,A_{2}
be affine varieties with algebraic morphisms
\pi_{i}:X_{i}\to Y:=\mathrm{Spec}\,R
for
i=1,2
. Equivalently, we have the
k
-algebra homomorphisms
\varphi_{i}:R\to A_{i}
that induce
\pi_{i}
. The
fiber product

X_{1}\times_{Y}X_{2}
is the affine variety with the coordinate ring
A_{1}\otimes_{R}A_{2}
and is equipped with natural projections to
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. In particular,
X_{1}\times_{Y}X_{2}
satisfies the universal property that any pair of morphisms
Z\to X_{1}
and
Z\to X_{2}
to
X_{1},X_{2}
that agree on
Y
factors uniquely through a morphism
Z\to X_{1}\times_{Y}X_{2}
. Now, suppose
V//G_{1}
and
W//G_{2}
are GIT quotients for algebraic group actions
G_{1}\circlearrowright V
and
G_{2}\circlearrowright W
. If there are morphisms
f:V//G_{1}\to Y
and
g:W//G_{2}\to Y
between the quotient varieties, then we define the fiber product of the GIT quotients
V//G_{1}
and
W//G_{2}
over
Y
to be
V//G_{1}\times_{Y}W//G_{2}
. We will determine the finitely-generated algebra consisting of generators from the set
F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. Let
F,A,B
be a commutative
\mathbb{R}
-algebra. Suppose further that
A,B
are subalgebras of
F
via fixed embeddings
A\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{A}}F\xhookleftarrow{\iota_{B}}B
. Identifying
A
and
B
with their images in
F
, we define the intersection algebra by

\displaystyle A\cap B=\left\{f\in F:f\in A\text{ and }f\in B\right\},

which is canonically isomorphic to the fiber product as follows

(4.41)

\displaystyle A\cap B\cong\left\{(a,b)\in A\times B:\iota_{A}(a)=\iota_{B}(b)\right\}:=A\times_{F}B.

This is summarised into the following lemma.

Lemma 4.36
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
F
a commutative
R
-algebra, and let
\iota_{A}:A\hookrightarrow F
and
\iota_{B}:B\hookrightarrow F
be injective
R
-algebra homomorphisms. Identify
A,B
with their images in
F
. Then
A\cap B\cong A\times_{F}B
as
\mathbb{R}
-algebras, via
\Phi:A\cap B\to A\times_{F}B
,
\,\Phi(f)=(f,f)
, with inverse
(a,b)\mapsto\iota_{A}(a)=\iota_{B}(b)\in F
.

Proof.

If
f\in A\cap B
, then
(f,f)\in A\times_{F}B
, since the two images in
F
coincide. Conversely, for
(a,b)\in A\times_{F}B
we have
\iota_{A}(a)=\iota_{B}(b)\in F
, so this common element lies in
A\cap B
. Both maps are algebra homomorphisms and inverses of each other.
∎

Now, in our case, let
F=\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
,
A=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
, and
B=\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
, and let
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
be the intersection algebra. Using Lemma
4.36
, we have the following formal definition of intersection algebra

(4.42)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\left\{f\in\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M):h\in S(\mathfrak{g}),\ \theta\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}\text{ such that }f=h\circ P=\theta\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}\right\}\cong\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\times_{\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}.

We now focus on the identification of the intersection algebra. Starting with the following lemma:

Lemma 4.37
.

7
;
29

Let
G
be a compact Lie group, and let
W,V,T
be the
G
-modules. Then the short exact sequence of
G
-modules

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow W\xrightarrow{\,\iota\,}T\xrightarrow{\,q\,}V\longrightarrow 0

induces the following
G
-invariants short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow W^{G}\xrightarrow{\,\iota\,}T^{G}\xrightarrow{\,q\,}V^{G}\longrightarrow 0

Proposition 4.38
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
be as defined above, and let
Z_{P}=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\subset\mathfrak{g}
. Let
\mathrm{Res}_{W}:S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
denote the restriction mapping. Then

(4.43)

\displaystyle R_{0}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}\left(\mathrm{Im\,Res_{W}}\right)=\left\{h\circ P:\ h\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G},h|_{Z_{P}}\text{ well-defined}\right\}.

Equivalently,
R_{0}=\{f\in\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M):\text{ there exist }C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G},\ \theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\text{ such that }\ f=C\circ P=\theta\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. By Definition
4.5
, there are
h\in S(\mathfrak{g})
and
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
with

(4.44)

\displaystyle\varphi=P^{*}h=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta.

Evaluating at
(g,X)
and letting
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
, using (
4.44
), we deduce
h\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}(g)\,\xi\bigr)=\theta(\xi)
for all
g\in G
. Hence
h
is constant on each
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-orbit contained in
Z_{P}
, i.e.,
h|_{Z_{P}}\in S(Z_{P})^{G}
. Consider the short exact sequence of
G
-modules

(4.45)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}(Z_{P})\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }S(\mathfrak{g})\xrightarrow{\ q\ }S(Z_{P})\cong S(\mathfrak{g})/\mathcal{I}(Z_{P})\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mathcal{I}(Z_{P})
is the ideal of
Z_{P}
and
S(Z_{P})\cong S(\mathfrak{g})/\mathcal{I}(Z_{P})
. Note that
S(\mathfrak{g})=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}S^{j}(\mathfrak{g})
with each
S^{j}(\mathfrak{g})
is finite-dimensional. Moreover, since
G
is compact and semisimple, all morphisms
\iota
and
q
are morphisms of graded
G
-modules. For a homogeneous polynomial
h\in S(\mathfrak{g})
, fixed
d:=\deg h\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we define

\displaystyle S_{d}(\mathfrak{g}):=\bigoplus_{k\leq d}S^{k}(\mathfrak{g}),\quad S_{d}(Z_{p}):=q\left(S_{d}(\mathfrak{g})\right),\quad\mathcal{I}_{d}(Z_{p}):=\mathcal{I}(Z_{p})\cap S_{d}(\mathfrak{g}),

where
S^{k}(\mathfrak{g})
consists of degree
k
homogeneous polynomials. See, (
3.5
). Then, using (
4.45
), we deduce a short exact sequence of finite-dimensional
G
-modules as follows:

(4.46)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{d}(Z_{P})\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }S_{d}(\mathfrak{g})\xrightarrow{\ q\ }S_{d}(Z_{P})\longrightarrow 0.

Since
G
is compact, by Lemma
4.37
, the invariant of a finite-dimensional
G
-module will also induce a short exact sequence given by

(4.47)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{d}(Z_{P})^{G}\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }S_{d}(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\xrightarrow{\ q\ }S_{d}(Z_{P})^{G}\longrightarrow 0

with
q:S_{d}(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\rightarrow S_{d}(Z_{P})^{G}
being surjective.

We now lift the surjectivity of
q
from a finite-dimensional
G
-module into a finitely generated
G
-algebra. Let
h^{\prime}=h|_{Z_{P}}\in S(Z_{P})^{G}
for some
h\in S(\mathfrak{g})
. For fixed
d
with
h^{\prime}\in S_{d}(Z_{P})^{G}
, since
q
in (
4.47
) is surjective, there exists a
f_{d}\in S_{d}(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
such that
q(C_{d})=h^{\prime}
. That is,
C_{d}|_{Z_{P}}=h^{\prime}
. Using the inclusion
S_{d}(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\subset S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
and
S_{d}(Z_{P})^{G}\subset S(Z_{P})^{G}
, the identity
C_{d}|_{Z_{P}}=h^{\prime}
still holds in
S(Z_{P})
Then, taking
C=C_{d}\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
, we must have
q(C)=h^{\prime}\in S(Z_{P})
. Hence,
q
defines the surjectivity from
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
to
S(Z_{P})^{G}
. In this case, Lemma
4.37
can be applied to the whole algebra. Therefore, the induced sequence

(4.48)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}(Z_{P})^{G}\xrightarrow{\,\iota\,}S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\xrightarrow{\,q\,}S(Z_{P})^{G}\longrightarrow 0

is exact. In particular, the restriction map
q
is surjective, so there exists
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
with
C|_{Z_{P}}=h|_{Z_{P}}
. Since
P(T^{*}M)=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
, for all
(g,X)\in T^{*}M
, we have
C(P(g,X))=C(\mathrm{Ad}(g)\xi)=h(\mathrm{Ad}(g)\xi)=\theta(\xi)
. It follows that

\displaystyle P^{*}C=P^{*}h=\varphi.

In particular, evaluating at
g=e
yields
C(\xi)=h(\xi)=\theta(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. We thus obtain
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
such that
C(\zeta)=h(\zeta)
for all
\zeta\in Z_{P}
. In particular,
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(h)
on
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. Hence,
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)=\theta
and

\displaystyle\varphi=P^{*}C=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)\in P^{*}(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W}).

Thus,
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\subseteq P^{*}(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W})
.

For the reverse inclusion, let
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
and put
\theta:=\mathrm{Res}(C)\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. Then, for all
(g,X)
with
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
, the pullback of these generators through the moment map
P
is given by

(4.49)

\displaystyle P^{*}C(g,X)=C\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}(g)\,\xi\bigr)=C(\xi)=\theta(\xi)=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta(g,X),

so
P^{*}C\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, and therefore
P^{*}(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W})\subseteq\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. The equality (
4.49
) implies that
\theta
is the restriction of
C
in the subspace
\mathfrak{m}
. Therefore, we have explicitly constructed a polynomial
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
such that
\theta=\mathrm{Res}(C)
, and thus
\theta\in\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W}
. Consequently, the original polynomial
\varphi
is equal to
P^{*}(C)=P^{*}(\theta)
, which shows that all the elements
\varphi\in R_{0}
are in the image of the pullback of the restriction map. Thus, we have shown inclusion
R_{0}\subseteq P^{*}(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W})
.
∎

Corollary 4.39
.

Let
R_{0},\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
be the same as defined above. Then
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
,
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, and
R_{0}
are finitely generated integral domains. Moreover, the inclusions
