\displaystyle\iota_{1}:R_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\qquad\iota_{2}:R_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}

make each factor a torsion-free
R_{0}
-module.

Proof.

Write
Z_{P}=P(T^{*}M)=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\subset\mathfrak{g}
. By Lemma
4.7
, we have a morphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{\mathfrak{m}}:\ G\times(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g},\quad(g,\xi)\longmapsto\mathrm{Ad}(g)\xi

that has an irreducible image. Therefore, its ideal
\mathcal{I}(Z_{P})\subset S(\mathfrak{g})
is prime, and
\mathbb{R}[Z_{P}]:=S(\mathfrak{g})/\mathcal{I}(Z_{P})
is a domain. By Lemma
4.17
, we have
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}S(\mathfrak{g})\cong\mathbb{R}[Z_{P}]
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is a domain. On the other hand, the map
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}:T^{*}M\cong G\times_{A}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\to\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
is surjective, so
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}:S(\mathfrak{m})\to\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
is injective and remains injective upon restriction to the
A
-invariants. By Lemma
4.17
again,
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\cong S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
, a subring of the domain
S(\mathfrak{m})
. Hence,
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is a domain.

By Proposition
4.38
, we have

\displaystyle R_{0}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}\big(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}\big)\cong\mathbb{R}[Z_{P}]^{G}.

Therefore,
R_{0}
is a subring of the domain
\mathbb{R}[Z_{P}]^{G}
and hence is itself a domain.

Finally, by the definition of torsion-free: If
0\neq r\in R_{0}
and
x\in\mathfrak{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{poly}}
satisfy
rx=0
in
\mathfrak{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, then since
\mathfrak{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is a domain, we must have
x=0
. Thus, each
\mathfrak{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is torsion-free over
R_{0}
.
∎

We will directly show that
R_{0}
is indeed in a Poisson center in the following corollary through direct computation.

Corollary 4.40
.

For every
r\in R_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\{r,f\}_{1}=0\quad\text{ for all }f\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\qquad\{r,\phi\}_{2}=0\quad\text{ for all }\phi\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}.

Equivalently,
\iota_{i}(R_{0})\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{poly}})
for
i=1,2
.

Proof.

Fix
\ell\in R_{0}
. By the definition of the intersection, there exist
h\in S(\mathfrak{g})
and
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
such that, as functions on
T^{*}M
,

\displaystyle\ell=h\circ P=\theta\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr).

Let
f=h^{\prime}\circ P\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
for some
h^{\prime}\in S(\mathfrak{g})
. Using the expression of
\ell
coming from the second block and Lemma
4.21
,

\displaystyle\{\ell,f\}_{1}=\bigl\{\theta(X-\varepsilon W),\,h^{\prime}\circ P\bigr\}_{\varepsilon}=0.

Similarly, let
\phi=\theta^{\prime}(X-\varepsilon W)\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
for some
\theta^{\prime}\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. The same reason shows that

\displaystyle\{\ell,\phi\}_{2}=\bigl\{h\circ P,\,\theta^{\prime}(X-\varepsilon W)\bigr\}_{\varepsilon}=0.

Thus,
\ell
Poisson commutes with each factor.
∎

Remark 4.41
.

By Proposition
4.38
, Corollary
4.39
and Corollary
4.40
, we have an identification
R_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}[Z_{P}]^{G}
, i.e.,
R_{0}
consists of the restrictions to
Z_{P}=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
of the global
G
-invariants on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.
3
3

3

In particular,
R_{0}
is a quotient of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
by the ideal of invariant polynomials vanishing on
Z
. It is not literally a subring of
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
.
With the block Poisson bracket on

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}},

every
r\in R_{0}
Poisson commutes with both blocks. Thus,
R_{0}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
. Another side of inclusion
\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})\subset R_{0}
and equality

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})=R_{0}

(on a Zariski open dense subset) is non-trivial and is proved later in Theorem
4.64
via the Jacobian argument.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\textbf{Alg}\left\langle F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cup F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right\rangle
. In what follows, we will provide a more detailed description of the union algebra
\mathcal{A}
. We begin by analyzing the fiber tensor algebra

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}},\qquad\mu:\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M),

obtained via the canonical multiplication map.

We now establish the Poisson structures on the reduced tensor product
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. We first consider the tensor product
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. Define a bilinear operation
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\otimes}:\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\times\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
by

\displaystyle\{h\otimes\theta,h^{\prime}\otimes\theta^{\prime}\}_{\otimes}:=\{h,h^{\prime}\}_{1}\otimes(\theta\theta^{\prime})+hh^{\prime}\otimes\{\theta,\theta^{\prime}\}_{2}.

Here
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{i}
is the twisted Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\varepsilon}
restricted on
\mathfrak{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{poly}}
for all
i=1,2
. It is straightforward to show that
\left(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\otimes}\right)
is a Poisson algebra by checking the Leibniz rule and Jacobi identity. Let
J
be the two-sided ideal generated by the following relations

(4.50)

\displaystyle(hr)\otimes\theta-h\otimes(r\theta),\quad h\otimes(\theta r)-(hr)\otimes\theta,

where
h\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\theta\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
r\in R_{0}
. The fiber tensor product is

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}})/J.

Lemma 4.42
.

For every
r\in R_{0}
, we have
\{r,f\}_{1}=0
for all
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\{r,\varphi\}_{2}=0
for all
\varphi\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.38
and Corollary
4.39
, we can write
r=P^{*}h=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta
with
h\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
,
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. For any
f=P^{*}h^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, we then deduce

\displaystyle\{r,f\}_{1}=\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta,\;P^{*}h^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}=0

by the vanishing of the mixed-block
\{\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
stated in Lemma
4.21
. Similarly, we deduce
\{r,\varphi\}_{2}=0
.
∎

We now show that the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\otimes}
descends into the bilinear operator
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
such that
\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\right)
forms a Poisson algebra.

Lemma 4.43
.

The ideal
J
is a Poisson ideal of
\left(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\otimes}\right)
. Hence
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\otimes}
descends uniquely to a bilinear bracket

\displaystyle\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}:\ \widehat{\mathcal{A}}\times\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}}.

Proof.

Let
x=(fr)\otimes\varphi-f\otimes(r\varphi)
and
y=f_{1}\otimes\varphi_{1}
with
f,f_{1}\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
,
\varphi,\varphi_{1}\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
r\in R_{0}
. Using bilinearity, Leibniz, and the factor-wise centrality of
r
proved above,

\displaystyle\{(fr)\otimes\phi,\ y\}_{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\{fr,f^{\prime}\}_{1}\otimes\phi\phi^{\prime}+(frf^{\prime})\otimes\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}_{2}

(4.51)

\displaystyle=r\,\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{1}\otimes\phi\phi^{\prime}+ff^{\prime}r\otimes\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}_{2},

(4.52)

\displaystyle\{f\otimes(r\phi),\ y\}_{o}u

\displaystyle=\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{1}\otimes r\phi\phi^{\prime}+ff^{\prime}\otimes r\,\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}_{2}.

Subtracting gives the sum of the generators of
J
. Then, using (
4.51
) and (
4.52
), we have

\displaystyle\{x,y\}_{\otimes}=

\displaystyle\,\{r,f_{1}\}_{1}\otimes\varphi\varphi_{1}-ff_{1}\otimes\varphi\{r,\varphi_{1}\}_{2}+\big(r\{f,f_{1}\}_{1}\otimes\varphi\varphi_{1}-\{f,f_{1}\}_{1}\otimes r\varphi\varphi_{1}\big)

\displaystyle\,+\big(frf_{1}\otimes\{\varphi,\varphi_{1}\}_{2}-ff_{1}\otimes r\{\varphi,\varphi_{1}\}_{2}\big).

By Lemma
4.42
, the first two terms vanish, and the remaining two lie in
J
. Hence,
J
is a Poisson ideal, and the bracket descends.
∎

We then examine the Poisson bracket on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and the block rules.

Proposition 4.44
.

The descended operation
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
makes
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
a Poisson algebra. It is characterised by

\displaystyle\{f\otimes_{R_{0}}1,\ f^{\prime}\otimes_{R_{0}}1\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}=\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{1}\otimes_{R_{0}}1,

(4.53)

\displaystyle\{1\otimes_{R_{0}}\phi,\ 1\otimes_{R_{0}}\phi^{\prime}\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}=1\otimes_{R_{0}}\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}_{2},

\displaystyle\{f\otimes_{R_{0}}1,\ 1\otimes_{R_{0}}\phi\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}=0,

and the Leibniz rule. Then
\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\right)
is a Poisson algebra.

Proof.

Bilinearity, antisymmetry, and the Leibniz rule descend from the tensor product as
J
is a two-sided Poisson ideal. The Jacobi identity holds by direct computational check. The relations in (
4.53
) follow directly from the definition of
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\otimes}
.
∎

The constructions above inspire the following definition.

Definition 4.45
.

Consider the algebraic tensor product

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\big/J.

The Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}:\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\times\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is given by

\displaystyle\{f\otimes\phi,\ f^{\prime}\otimes\phi^{\prime}\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}:=\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}\otimes(\phi\phi^{\prime})+(ff^{\prime})\otimes\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon},\quad f,f^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\ \phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}.

Let

(4.54)

\displaystyle\mu:\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M),\qquad\mu\Bigl(\sum_{i}f_{i}\otimes\phi_{i}\Bigr)=\sum_{i}f_{i}\cdot\phi_{i}

be an algebra homomorphism, where the product on the right is the pointwise product in
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
. Here we consider
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
. We can further show that
\mu
is a Poisson homomorphism. Although
\ker\mu
could initially be nonzero, the field-intersection argument below will show that, in our setting,
\ker\mu=0
.

Proposition 4.46
.

The map
\mu
is a homomorphism of Poisson algebras

\displaystyle\mu(gg^{\prime})=\mu(g)\mu(g^{\prime}),\qquad\mu\bigl(\{g,g^{\prime}\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\bigr)=\{\mu(g),\mu(g^{\prime})\}_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{for all }g:=f\otimes\phi,g^{\prime}:=f^{\prime}\otimes\phi^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}.

In particular, its kernel
\mathcal{I}:=\ker\mu
is a Poisson ideal of
\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\right)
.

Proof.

We first show the algebra homomorphism. For any
g=f\otimes\phi,g^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\otimes\phi^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, by the definition of (
4.54
),

\displaystyle\mu(gg^{\prime})=\mu(ff^{\prime}\otimes\phi\phi^{\prime})=(ff^{\prime})(\phi\phi^{\prime})=\mu(g)\mu(g^{\prime}).

On the other hand, we show that the Poisson property holds. In the twisted Poisson bracket,

(4.55)

\displaystyle\{\mu(g),\mu(g^{\prime})\}_{\varepsilon}=\{f\phi,f^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}=\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}\phi\phi^{\prime}+\{f,\phi^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}\phi f^{\prime}+\{\phi,f^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}f\phi^{\prime}+ff^{\prime}\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}

It suffices to check for simple tensors. By Lemma
4.21
, we have
\{\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
. Then the identity (
4.55
) becomes
\{\mu(g),\mu(g^{\prime})\}_{\varepsilon}=\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}\phi\phi^{\prime}+ff^{\prime}\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}
. On the other hand, using the Leibniz rule for
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\varepsilon}
and restricted to
\{\cdot,\cdot\}|_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mu\left(\{g,g^{\prime}\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\right)=\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}\,\phi\phi^{\prime}+ff^{\prime}\,\{\phi,\phi^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}=\{f\phi,\ f^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}.

We then show that
\mathcal{I}
is an ideal by using the definition of the Poisson ideal. Indeed, if
a\in\ker\mu
and
b\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, then

\mu(\{a,b\}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}})=\{\mu(a),\mu(b)\}_{\varepsilon}=0
.
∎

Remark 4.47
.

Let
\mathcal{A}:=\mu(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
. Define
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{A}}
as the unique Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{A}
such that the inclusion

\displaystyle\iota:(\mathcal{A},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{A}})\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M),\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\varepsilon})

is a Poisson algebra embedding. Equivalently,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{A}})\cong\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}/\ker\mu,\ \overline{\{\cdot,\cdot\}}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\bigr).

In particular, for any
h,h^{\prime}\in S(\mathfrak{g})
and
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
, together with Lemma
4.17
and Lemma
4.21
, we deduce that the Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{A}
is closed in the following way:

\displaystyle\{P^{*}h,P^{*}h^{\prime}\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=P^{*}\left(\{h,h^{\prime}\}_{1}\right),

(4.56)

\displaystyle\{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta,\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta^{\prime}\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\ \pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\Bigl(\{\theta,\theta^{\prime}\}_{2}\Bigr),

\displaystyle\{P^{*}h,\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=0.

Here
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{1}
defines the canonical Lie-Poisson bracket, and
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{2}
defines the Poisson structure on
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
.

In general, the kernel of
\mu
does not need to be zero. In what follows, we aim to prove that
\ker\mu=\{0\}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. We now review some basic terminology from the theory of commutative algebras as a generalization of the Poisson polynomial algebras studied above. Let
R
be a finitely generated integral domain over
\mathbb{R}
. Consider two other finitely generated integral domains
A
and
B
that contain
R
as a subring. We have inclusions of
k
-algebra homomorphisms
R\hookrightarrow A
and
R\hookrightarrow B
. We are interested in the tensor product of these algebras over the common subdomain
R
,

\displaystyle A\otimes_{R}B.

Remark 4.48
.

Suppose that
A=\textbf{Alg}\langle a\rangle
and
B=\textbf{Alg}\langle b\rangle
are finitely generated algebras with the finite sets
a\subset A
and
b\subset B
. Then

\displaystyle\textbf{Alg}\langle A\cup B\rangle=\textbf{Alg}\langle a\cup b\rangle.

Indeed,
\textbf{Alg}\langle a\cup b\rangle
is a subalgebra containing
A
and
B
. Hence, it contains
\textbf{Alg}\langle A\cup B\rangle
. Conversely,
\textbf{Alg}\langle A\cup B\rangle
contains
a\cup b
and therefore also contains
\textbf{Alg}\langle a\cup b\rangle
.

On the other hands, the intersection
A\cap B
is a set-theoretic intersection within the fixed algebra
F
and is not determined by the intersections of chosen generating sets. In general, we only have
\textbf{Alg}\langle a\cap b\rangle\subseteq A\cap B
, and equality may not hold.
Moreover, the notation
A\cap B
is meaningful only after fixing the embeddings of
A
and
B
into a common ambient algebra. See Lemma
4.36
. For abstract
R
-algebras
A
and
B
, the expression
A\cap B
is not defined.

Definition 4.49
.

(
30
, Chapter VIII, §3)

Let
A,B
be finitely-generated integral domains containing a common integral subdomain
R
with fraction field
K_{R}
. Let

\displaystyle\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}\subset\mathrm{Frac}(A),\quad\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}\}\subset\mathrm{Frac}(B)

be the transcendence bases over
K_{R}
. We say that
A
and
B
are
algebraically disjoint
(
linearly disjoint
) over
R
if and only if the canonical map

\displaystyle\iota:\mathrm{Frac}(A)\otimes_{K_{R}}\mathrm{Frac}(B)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Frac}(A)\mathrm{Frac}(B)=\mathrm{Frac}(AB)

is injective, where
\mathrm{Frac}(A)\otimes_{K_{R}}\mathrm{Frac}(B)
forms a domain and
\mathrm{Frac}(A)\mathrm{Frac}(B)
is the smallest subfield containing both
\mathrm{Frac}(A)
and
\mathrm{Frac}(B)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Frac}(\mathrm{Frac}(A)\otimes_{K_{R}}\mathrm{Frac}(B))=\mathrm{Frac}(A)\mathrm{Frac}(B),

and the transcendence bases
\{X_{i}\}
for
\mathrm{Frac}(A)/K_{R}
and
\{Y_{j}\}
for
\mathrm{Frac}(B)/K_{R}
are algebraically independent within
\mathrm{Frac}(A)\mathrm{Frac}(B)
.

Definition 4.50
.

(
3
, Chapter 1)

For any commutative algebra
S
, the nilradical of
S
is defined as the set of nilpotent elements

\displaystyle\sqrt{0}_{S}:=\{\,f\in S:f^{n}=0\text{ for some integer }n\geq 1\,\}\,.

We say
S
is
reduced
if it does not have nonzero nilpotent elements. Equivalently,
S
is reduced if and only if
\sqrt{0}_{S}=\{0\}
. In a reduced algebra, the equality
f^{n}=0
always implies
f=0
.

We aim to show that, under appropriate hypotheses, the tensor product of two integral domains does not introduce any new algebraic polynomial relations between elements of
A
and
B
beyond those already coming from
R
. Equivalently, we will see that
A\otimes_{R}B
is a reduced
R
-algebra whose transcendence degree is exactly the sum of the transcendence degrees of
A
and
B
minus that of
R
.

We now introduce a proposition that extends the integral domain
\mathcal{A}
into its rational field, such that we can compute the transcendence degree at the field level.

Proposition 4.51
.

Let
R\subseteq A
and
R\subseteq B
be inclusions of finitely generated integral domains over
R
. Let
K_{R}=\mathrm{Frac}(R),K_{A}=\mathrm{Frac}(A)
and
K_{B}=\mathrm{Frac}(B)
. Suppose that
A
and
B
are algebraically disjoint over
R
. Let
S:=R\setminus\{0\}
be the localization of
R
. Then
S^{-1}(A\otimes_{R}B)
is a domain, and

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}_{K_{R}}\mathrm{Frac}\,S^{-1}(A\otimes_{R}B)=\mathrm{trdeg}_{K_{R}}K_{A}+\mathrm{trdeg}_{K_{R}}K_{B},

the algebra of the canonical tensor product
\mathcal{A}:=A\otimes_{R}B
does not introduce new algebraic relations. In particular,
\mathcal{A}
has a transcendence degree

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}A+\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}B-\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}R\,,

and
\mathcal{A}
is a reduced
R
-algebra.

Proof.

Since
A
and
B
are integral domains containing
R
, we consider their fraction fields
K_{A}=\mathrm{Frac}(A)
,
K_{B}=\mathrm{Frac}(B)
, and
K_{R}=\mathrm{Frac}(R)
. In other words, we have the field extensions
K_{R}\subseteq K_{A}
and
K_{R}\subseteq K_{B}
. Under the assumption that
A
and
B
are algebraically disadjoint over
R
, by Definition
4.49
, if
{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}}
is a transcendence basis for
K_{A}
over
K_{R}
and
{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}}
is a transcendence basis for
K_{B}
over
K_{R}
, then

(4.57)

\displaystyle K_{A}\otimes_{K_{R}}K_{B}\hookrightarrow K_{A}K_{B}\,,

is injective, where
K_{A}K_{B}
is a transcendence extension of
K_{R}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m},\,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})
. Here
n=\mathrm{trdeg}\,A
and
m=\mathrm{trdeg}\,B
. Then there is a natural
R
-algebra homomorphism
\mathcal{A}\to K_{A}\otimes_{K_{R}}K_{B}
, which factors through the localization at
S=R\setminus\{0\}
:

\displaystyle S^{-1}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

\displaystyle\cong(A\otimes_{R}K_{R})\otimes_{K_{R}}(B\otimes_{R}K_{R})

(4.58)

\displaystyle\cong K_{A}\otimes_{K_{R}}K_{B}\xhookrightarrow{\ \varrho\ }\text{(a field algebraic over $K_{R}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})$).}

Here
S^{-1}(A)=A\otimes_{R}K_{R}
and
S^{-1}(B)=B\otimes_{R}K_{R}
. In the composition chain (
4.58
), the isomorphisms are given by the localization and associativity of the tensor. The inclusions
(A\otimes_{R}K)\hookrightarrow L
and
(B\otimes_{R}K)\hookrightarrow M
are
K
-linear injections, and
L
and
M
are linearly disjoint over
K
by assumption, so the third arrow
\varrho
is injective. Hence
S^{-1}(\mathcal{A})
embeds into a field and, in particular, is an integral domain. Therefore, through the embedding
\varrho
, the elements of
\mathcal{A}
can be regarded as rational functions in the independent transcendental
X_{i}
and
Y_{j}
over
K_{R}
. In particular, any potential algebraic relation among elements of
\mathcal{A}
would produce a polynomial equation in
X_{i}
and
Y_{j}
of over
K_{R}
that vanishes in
K_{R}(X_{i},Y_{j})
.

Suppose, by contradiction, that there exist new algebraic relations in
\mathcal{A}
. Without loss of generality, assume that there is a nonzero polynomial

\displaystyle f(t_{1,1},\ldots,t_{1,n},\,t_{2,1},\ldots,t_{2,m})\in R[x_{1,1},\ldots,x_{1,n},\,x_{2,1},\ldots,x_{2,m}],

where the indeterminates
x_{1,i}
and
x_{2,j}
are such that if we substitute
x_{1,i}=X_{i}\in K_{A}
and
x_{2,j}=Y_{j}\in K_{B}
, the polynomial
f
vanishes in
\mathcal{A}
. In other words, we assume
f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},\,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})=0
to be an identity that holds in the ring
\mathcal{A}
. By applying the embedding of
\mathcal{A}
into the field
K_{R}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})
, the same polynomial relation also holds in the field
K_{R}(X_{i},Y_{j})
. However, since
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}
are algebraically independent over
K_{R}
, a nonzero polynomial cannot vanish on them identically in the field of rational functions. The only polynomial in
R[x_{1,i},x_{2,j}]
that vanishes for such a substitution into
K_{R}(X_{i},Y_{j})
is the zero polynomial. Hence, no new algebraic polynomial relations can appear in
\mathcal{A}
beyond those that are already present in
R
.

As a consequence, we can determine the transcendence degree on the generic fiber
\mathcal{A}
over
R
. The natural maps
A\to S^{-1}(\mathcal{A})
,
a\mapsto a\otimes 1
,
B\to S^{-1}(\mathcal{A})
, and
b\mapsto 1\otimes b
induce inclusions

\displaystyle K_{A},\;K_{B}\subseteq\mathrm{Frac}\left(S^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\right).

Hence,
\mathrm{Frac}\left(S^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\right)
is the composite of
K_{A}
and
K_{B}
over
K_{R}
, which is algebraic over
K_{R}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})
. Since
{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}}
forms a transcendence basis for the fraction field of
\mathcal{A}
over
K_{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}_{K_{R}}\mathrm{Frac}(\mathcal{A})=n+m\,,

where
n=\mathrm{trdeg}_{K_{R}}\mathrm{Frac}(A)
and
m=\mathrm{trdeg}_{K_{R}}\mathrm{Frac}(B)
. But
\mathrm{trdeg}_{K_{R}}\mathrm{Frac}(A)=\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}A
as
K_{R}
is the fraction field of
R
. The argument holds for the transcendence degree of
B
. Also note
\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}R=\mathrm{trdeg}_{K_{R}}K_{R}=0
by definition. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}\,\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}\,A+\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}\,B\,,

since here
\mathrm{trdeg}_{R}R=0
. And more generally, if
R
already has some transcendence degree over
\mathbb{R}
, that would be subtracted as stated. This shows that the transcendence degree of
\mathcal{A}
is exactly the sum of those of
A
and
B
over
R
, indicating no reduction in freedom (i.e., no new algebraic constraints).

Finally, we deduce the nilradical of
\mathcal{A}
. By embedding (
4.58
),
S^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\hookrightarrow K_{A}K_{B}
. A field contains no nonzero nilpotent elements as the only solution to
\alpha^{N}=0
in a field is
\alpha=0
. Therefore, if an element
z\in\mathcal{A}
is nilpotent (i.e., there exists a
N
such that
z^{N}=0
), its image in
K_{A}K_{B}
would also satisfy equation
(\text{image of }z)^{N}=0
in a field. This forces the image of
z
to be
0
in the field and hence
z=0
in
\mathcal{A}
. This shows that
\mathcal{A}
does not have nonzero nilpotents. In other words, the nilradical
\sqrt{0}
of
\mathcal{A}
is zero, so
\mathcal{A}
, by Definition
4.50
, is reduced.

In conclusion,
\mathcal{A}
introduces no new algebraic relations and is reduced, as claimed.
∎

Remark 4.52
.

Let
R\subset A,B
and
S
be the same as defined above. Using the canonical chain defined in (
4.58
), we have the composition of injectives from the integral domain
S^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\hookrightarrow K_{A}\otimes_{K_{R}}K_{B}\xrightarrow{\ \varrho\ }K_{A}K_{B}
. Therefore, any polynomial relation that holds in
\mathcal{A}
must also hold in
K_{A}\otimes_{K_{R}}K_{B}
. Thus, checking for new relations reduces to the generic fiber over
K_{R}
. If
\varrho
is injective, then
S^{-1}(\mathcal{A})
embeds into the domain
K_{A}K_{B}
, so the localization is reduced.

Corollary 4.53
.

Let
R\subset A,B\subset C
be
R
-subalgebras which are finitely generated integral domains. Let

\displaystyle\mu:A\otimes_{R}B\longrightarrow C,\quad a\otimes b\longmapsto ab

be the multiplication map. Then
\mathrm{Im}(\mu)=\mathrm{\textbf{Alg}}\langle A\cup B\rangle
. Moreover, if the induced map

\displaystyle K_{A}\otimes_{K}K_{B}\longrightarrow LM

is injective, then
S^{-1}(\ker\mu)=\{0\}
. In particular, if
A\otimes_{R}B
is
R
-torsion-free, then
\ker\mu=\{0\}
, and hence

\displaystyle\textbf{Alg}\langle A\cup B\rangle\cong A\otimes_{R}B.

As discussed above, we now apply the construction to our polynomial Poisson algebras
\mathfrak{F}_{i}^{\mathrm{poly}}
for all
i=1,2
. Based on Proposition
4.51
, after the field extension, we need to show that
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
are algebraically disjoint over
R_{0}
. Let

\displaystyle L:=\mathrm{Frac}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\qquad M:=\mathrm{Frac}\,\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\qquad K:=\mathrm{Frac}\,R_{0}.

If
L
and
M
are algebraically disjoint over
K
, then the natural map
L\otimes_{K}M\longrightarrow LM
is injective. By
(
30
, Ch. VIII, §3-§4)
, we need to show that the transcendence basis of
M
and
L
are free over
K
, the intersection is clear and the extension
L/K
is regular. Consequently,
\ker\mu
remains zero, so
\delta_{\mathrm{alg}}=0
, and hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}_{R_{0}}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\mathrm{rank}_{R_{0}}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right).

Starting with the following definition:

Definition 4.54
.

The Poisson polynomial algebras
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
R_{0}
are integral domains. Then we define
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathcal{A}=\textbf{Alg}\left\langle F_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cup F_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right\rangle=\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
. The rank of
\mathcal{A}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{rank}\,\widehat{\mathcal{A}}-\delta_{\mathrm{alg}},

where
\delta_{\mathrm{alg}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Remark 4.55
.

(i) Since
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
is reduced, every subalgebra of
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
is reduced. In particular,
\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{im}\,\mu
is reduced. Thus,
\mathcal{A}
is already reduced, and no further reduction is required. Hence, we have
\mathcal{A}=\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
.

(ii) For any finite
R
-algebra
B
, we have
\mathrm{rank}_{R}B=\mathrm{rank}_{R}\sqrt{B}
. Indeed, a localization at the generic point eliminates nilpotent components in
B
:

\displaystyle K\otimes_{R}B\cong K\otimes_{R}\bigl(B/\mathrm{nil}(B)\bigr)\cong K\otimes_{R}\sqrt{B},

where
\mathrm{nil}(B)
is the nilpotent component in
B
. Hence,
\mathrm{rank}_{R}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\mathrm{rank}_{R}\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
.

(iii) By Definition
4.45
,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\bigl(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\bigr)/\ker\mu
, so
\ker\mu=0
gives an isomorphism
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\ \xrightarrow{\cong}\ \mathcal{A}
. Hence
\delta_{\mathrm{alg}}=\dim\ker\mu
.

We now show
\mathcal{A}\cong\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
by checking the condition imposed by Proposition
4.51
one by one. As we mentioned before, we show the algebraically disjoint property by checking the regular extension and clear intersection.

Lemma 4.56
.

Let
G
be a simply connected, semisimple, and compact Lie group, and let
G
act on
Z_{P}=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
. Define
L=\mathbb{R}(Z_{P})=\mathrm{Frac}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right)
and
K=\mathbb{R}(Z_{P})^{G}
. Then
K
is algebraically closed in
L
. Equivalently, the extension
L/K
is regular.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in L
be algebraic over
K
, and let
p(t)\in K[t]
be its monic minimal polynomial. Since
K
is
G
-invariant, the coefficients of
p(t)
is
G
-invariant. Hence, for any
g\in G
, the element
g\cdot\alpha
satisfies the same polynomial
p(t)
because the coefficients lie in
K=L^{G}
. That is,

\displaystyle p(g\cdot\alpha)=g\cdot p(\alpha)=g\cdot 0=0.

Therefore, the orbit
G\cdot\alpha=\{g\cdot\alpha:g\in G\}
is contained in the finite set of roots of
p(t)\in K[t]
. Let
\mathcal{R}=\{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m}\}\subset L
denote this set. To prove
\alpha\in K
, it suffices to show that
\alpha
is
G
-invariant. In other words, the map
G\to\mathcal{R}
given by
g\mapsto g\cdot\alpha
is constant. Fix a point
z\in Z_{P}
. We define the evaluation map
\Psi_{z}:G\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\Psi_{z}(g)=(g\cdot\alpha)(z)=\alpha(g^{-1}\cdot z).

Since
G
acts smoothly on
Z_{P}
, and
\alpha
is regular on the orbit of
z
, the map
\Psi_{z}
is continuous. The image
\Psi_{z}(G)
is contained in the finite set of real values
\{\beta_{1}(z),\dots,\beta_{m}(z)\}\subset\mathbb{R}
. The only connected subsets of a finite set are singletons. Thus,
\Psi_{z}
is constant, implying
(g\cdot\alpha)(z)=\alpha(z)
for all
g\in G
. Since
z
was chosen arbitrarily from a dense open set
U
, we conclude that
g\cdot\alpha=\alpha
as elements of the function field
L
. Therefore,
\alpha\in L^{G}=K
.
∎

Remark 4.57
.

In a more general setting, let
G
be a connected affine algebraic group, and let
G
act rationally on an irreducible
\mathbb{R}
-variety
X
. Define
L=\mathbb{R}(X)
and
K=\mathbb{R}(X)^{G}
. Then
L/K
is regular.

We now show that in our setting,
L\cap M=K
.

Lemma 4.58
.

Let
\phi:=a/b\in\mathrm{Frac}\,S(\mathfrak{g})
and
\psi:=c/d\in\mathrm{Frac}\,S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
be defined with
a,b\in S(\mathfrak{g})
and
c,d\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. Suppose
\phi(\mathrm{Ad}(G)u)=\psi(u)
holds on a non-empty Zariski open subset

U=\left\{(g,u)\in T^{*}M:b(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u)\neq 0,\ d(u)\neq 0\right\}

of
T^{*}M
. Then
\phi
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant as a rational function on
\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}}\subset\mathfrak{g}
, hence
\phi\in\mathbb{R}(\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}})^{G}=\mathrm{Frac}\big(\mathbb{R}[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}}]^{G}\big)
.

Proof.

To show that
\phi
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant, it is sufficient to show that for a generic
y
, we have
d\phi_{y}([X,y])=0
for any
X\in\mathfrak{g}
. Fix
(g,u)\in U
, define the one-parameter flow of
g
by
g(t)=g\exp(tY)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Clearly,
g=g(0)
. As
U
is open and contains
(g,u)
, the continuity of
t\mapsto(g(t),u)
implies that there exists a
\varepsilon>0
such that
(g(t),u)\in U
for all
|t|<\varepsilon
. Hence, by the assumption, in this neighbourhood, we must have
\phi(\mathrm{Ad}(g(t))u)=\psi(u)
. Differentiating the identity
\phi(\mathrm{Ad}(g(t))u)=\psi(u)
at
t=0
gives

(4.59)

\displaystyle 0=\left.\dfrac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\phi\left(\mathrm{Ad}(g\exp(tY)u)\right)=d\phi_{\mathrm{Ad}(g)u}\big(\mathrm{Ad}(g)[Y,u]\big)=B\left(\nabla\phi(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u),\mathrm{Ad}(g)[Y,u]\right).

With an
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant inner product
B
, the evaluation in (
4.59
) equals
B([\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\nabla\phi(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u),u],Y)
for all
Y\in\mathfrak{g}
. Thus,

(4.60)

\displaystyle[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\nabla\phi(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u),u]=0.

Let
y=\mathrm{Ad}(g)u
be generic. From the trivial commutator relations in (
4.60
), we therefore get
[\nabla\phi(y),y]=0
, i.e.,
\phi
has zero directional derivatives along the tangent to the adjoint orbit through
y
. Thus,
\phi
is constant on a Zariski open subset of each generic adjoint orbit in
\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}}
, so
\phi
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant.
∎

By Proposition
4.38
, we have
R_{0}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
inside
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
. Then we always have the following inclusion:

(4.61)

K=\mathrm{Frac}(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}})\,\subseteq\,L\cap M

inside
\mathrm{Frac}\,\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
. The statement of Lemma
4.59
below is the reverse inclusion
L\cap M\subseteq K
, hence
L\cap M=K
.

Lemma 4.59
.

With
L:=\mathrm{Frac}\,\left(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right)=\mathbb{R}(Z_{P})
,
M:=\mathrm{Frac}\,\left(\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\right)=\mathbb{R}(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
, and
K:=\mathrm{Frac}\left(R_{0}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle L\cap M=K.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.38
, inclusion
K\subset L\cap M
is immediate. For reverse inclusion, let
f\in L\cap M
. Then there exist
\phi\in\mathrm{Frac}\,S(\mathfrak{g})
and
\psi\in\mathrm{Frac}\,S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
such that, as rational functions on
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
,

\displaystyle f=\phi\circ\bar{P}=\psi\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}\quad\text{and}\quad f((g,u))=\psi(u).

writes
\phi=a/b
with
a,b\in S(\mathfrak{g})
and
\psi=c/d
with
c,d\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. In
U
, we have that
\left(\phi\circ\bar{P}\right)|_{U}=\left(\psi\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}\right)|_{U}
are regular. This leads to regular identity

(4.62)

a(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u)\,d(u)=c(u)\,b(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u).

Fix
u
in a non-empty Zariski open
\mathfrak{m}^{\circ}\subset\mathfrak{m}
such that
d(u)\neq 0
and
G_{u}^{\circ}:=\{\,g\in G:(g,u)\in U\,\}
is non-empty Zariski open in
G
. For all
g\in G_{u}^{\circ}
, (
4.62
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{a(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u)}{b(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u)}=\frac{c(u)}{d(u)}.

As the equality
\varphi(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u)=\psi(u)
holds on
U
, by Lemma
4.58
,
\varphi
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant on
\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
. It follows that the rational function
\phi=a/b
is constant on a Zariski open subset of the adjoint orbit
\mathrm{Ad}(G)u
. As
u
varies over
\mathfrak{m}^{\circ}
, the union of these orbits is Zariski dense in
\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}}
. Thus,
\phi
is
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant in
\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}}
as a rational function:

\displaystyle\phi\in\mathbb{R}(\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}})^{G}.

Here, by geometric invariant theory for semisimple groups, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}(\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}})^{G}=\mathrm{Frac}\big(\mathbb{R}[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}}]^{G}\big),\text{ where }\mathbb{R}[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}}]^{G}\cong S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\big/\big(I(\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}})\cap S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\big).

Therefore, there exist invariant polynomials
r,t\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
mapping to
\overline{r},\overline{t}\in\mathbb{R}[\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}}]^{G}
such that
\phi=\overline{r}/\overline{t}
in
\mathbb{R}(\overline{\mathrm{Ad}(G)\mathfrak{m}})
. Pulling back along
\bar{P}
yields

\displaystyle f=\phi\circ\bar{P}=\frac{\overline{r}\circ\bar{P}}{\overline{t}\circ\bar{P}}=\frac{r\circ\bar{P}}{t\circ\bar{P}}.

Since
r,t
are
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant on
\mathfrak{g}
, for all
(g,u)\in X
, we have

\displaystyle(r\circ\bar{P})((g,u))=r(\mathrm{Ad}(g)u)=r(u)=(r|_{\mathfrak{m}})(u),

and similarly for
t
. Hence,
r\circ\bar{P},\,t\circ\bar{P}
lies both in
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and in
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, i.e., in
R_{0}
. Therefore,
f\in\mathrm{Frac}R_{0}=K
, proving
L\cap M\subset K
.

In conclusion,
K=L\cap M
as required.
∎

Together with Lemma
4.56
and Lemma
4.59
, we conclude the following result:

Proposition 4.60
.

(
30
, Chapter VIII, §3-§4)

With
L,M,K
as in Lemma
4.59
, the fields
M
and
L
are algebraically disjoint over
K
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}_{K}(LM)=\mathrm{trdeg}_{K}L+\mathrm{trdeg}_{K}M.

In particular,
L\otimes_{K}M
is an integral domain.

By Lemma
4.56
and Lemma
4.59
, we have
L\cap M=K
. Hence, by Proposition
4.60
, the natural map
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\to LM=\mathrm{Frac}(\mathcal{A})
is injective and
\mathrm{trdeg}_{K}(LM)=\mathrm{trdeg}_{K}L+\mathrm{trdeg}_{K}M
. We now pull back everything from the rational field level to integral domains.
For the finitely-generated domains
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
R_{0}
, we obtain
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}+\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}-\mathrm{rank}\,R_{0}.

Together with all constructions above, we conclude the following result.

Theorem 4.61
.

Let
G
be a compact, simply connected semisimple Lie group, and let
A=\exp(\ker\mathrm{ad}(W))
be defined as above for a fixed
W\in\mathfrak{g}
. Then the tensor product of these algebras over
R_{0}
does not introduce new algebraic relations. Equivalently, the radical of the tensor product is trivial. Thus, the ring is reduced

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\sqrt{\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\,}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\,.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}+\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}-\mathrm{trdeg}\,R_{0}.

With all the information above, we conclude the spectrum of the union of two Poisson polynomial algebras
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
in the following corollary.

Corollary 4.62
.

Let
\mathrm{Res}_{W}:S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. Then there is an isomorphism of affine varieties

\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}})=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\times_{\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W}}\left(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W\right)//A,

with dimension
\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}=n-r+\rho_{A}
, where

n=\dim\mathfrak{g},r=\mathrm{rank}(G),s=\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W},\text{ and }\rho_{A}:=\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}=\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}.

By Remark
4.41
, we already have
R_{0}\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
. The next theorem updates that argument to
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})=R_{0}
on
U
. Here
U
is the regular stratum in
T^{*}M
defined in (
4.33
). In particular, Corollary
4.41
below verifies the factor centrality used in Lemma
4.42
. Starting with the presentation of
R_{0}
. Let
J_{1},\ldots,J_{s}\in\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W}
be algebraically independent such that
s=\mathrm{trdeg}R_{0}
with
R_{0}:=P^{*}(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W})
. (See the discussion of

\mathrm{Res}_{W}
and Theorem
4.64
.) These
J_{i}
generate a polynomial subalgebra
\mathbb{R}[J_{1},\ldots,J_{s}]\subset R_{0}
. In general,

R_{0}
may be a finitely generated algebra over this subalgebra. Equivalently, there exist additional generators
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}
and relations
I
such that

\displaystyle R_{0}\;\cong\;\mathbb{R}[J_{1},\ldots,J_{s},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}]\big/I.

In the regular torus case (e.g.,
A=T
and
W
regular), we take
m=0
and
I=0
such that
R_{0}=\mathbb{R}[J_{1},\ldots,J_{s}]
. Define the integral map

\displaystyle\pi_{1}:T^{*}M\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\quad\text{ by }\quad\pi_{1}^{*}(\widehat{f})=f\quad\text{ for any }f\in\mathcal{A},

where
\widehat{f}
is the coordinate function of
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}
corresponding to
f
. Equivalently, if we choose a generating list,

(4.63)

\displaystyle\Phi=\bigl(\underbrace{P^{*}h_{1},\ldots,P^{*}h_{n-r}}_{\text{generators in $P^{*}S(\mathfrak{g})$}};\;\underbrace{P^{*}C_{i_{1}},\ldots,P^{*}C_{i_{s}}}_{\text{generators in $R_{0}$}};\;\underbrace{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{1},\ldots,\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{\rho_{A}}}_{\text{generators in $\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}(S(\mathfrak{m})^{A})$}}\bigr).

Then
\pi_{1}=(\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{n-r+s+\rho_{A}})
. On the other hand, the inclusion
\iota:R_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
induces

\displaystyle\pi_{2}:\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0},\qquad\pi_{2}^{*}=\iota.

We define the composition maps as follows:

\displaystyle q:=\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1}:T^{*}M\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}.

On the regular locus
U
defined in (
4.33
), the differential
d\pi_{1}
has a constant rank, and
\ker dq
is a smooth distribution.

Remark 4.63
.

Let us remark that, at this stage, we only use the inclusion
R_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
to define

\displaystyle q:T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{1}\ }\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{2}\ }\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0},

where
\pi_{2}
is the morphism induced by
R_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
. We do not yet use any information about the Poisson center
\mathcal{B}=Z(\mathcal{A})
. In Theorem
4.64
, we will show that
\mathcal{B}=R_{0}
on the regular locus, so that afterward,
\mathrm{Spec}R_{0}=\mathrm{Spec}\mathcal{B}
and
q
coincide with the central projection in the sense of Definition
2.1
.

Theorem 4.64
.

Let
G
be a simply connected, compact and semisimple Lie group of dimension
n
and rank
r
. Fix a closed subgroup
A\subset G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}
and decompose
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
orthogonally with respect to the Killing form
B
. Let
U
be the regular stratum in
T^{*}M
defined in (
4.33
). Fix
W\in\mathfrak{g}
and put
\xi:=X-\varepsilon W\in\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W\subset\mathfrak{g}
. Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{W}:S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\to S(\mathfrak{m})^{A},\quad\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(X):=C(X-\varepsilon W),\qquad R_{0}:=P^{*}\big(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W}\big),

and put
s=\mathrm{trdeg}(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W})
. Then, on
U
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=R_{0}=P^{*}\big(\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{rank}\,R_{0}=s.

Moreover,

\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}=(n-r)+\rho_{A}=2\dim\mathfrak{m}-s,\qquad\rho_{A}=\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}.

In particular, we have the following:

(i) if
A=T
and
W
is regular, then
s=r
,
\rho_{T}=\dim\mathfrak{m}-r
,
\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}=2\dim\mathfrak{m}-r
, and
\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal{B}=r
;

(ii) if
A\neq T
with
T\subsetneq A
and
W
is irregular while
\xi
is regular, then
s<r
,
\rho_{A}=n-2\dim A+r-s
,
\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}=2\dim\mathfrak{m}-s
, and
\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal{B}=s
.

Proof.

(1)
Inclusion
R_{0}\subset\mathcal{B}
.
For
C\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
, by the definition of the Poisson center,
P^{*}C
Poisson commutes both with
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and, as shown in Lemma
4.21
, with
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. Thus,
P^{*}C\in\mathcal{B}
. If
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)=0
, then
P^{*}C\equiv 0
on
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
. If
\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)\neq 0
, then for all
(g,X)\in T^{*}M
, we deduce the following:

\displaystyle P^{*}C(g,X)=C(\mathrm{Ad}(g)\xi)=C(\xi)=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)(X)=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\big(\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C)\big)(g,X)\in R_{0}.

Hence
R_{0}\subset\mathcal{B}
.

(2)
Rank verification.

Fix
(g,X)\in U
and put
\xi:=X-\varepsilon W
. Choose
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n-r}\in\mathfrak{g}
such that
\{[Z_{i},\xi]\}_{i=1}^{n-r}
is a basis of
[\mathfrak{g},\xi]=T_{\xi}(\mathrm{Ad}(G)\cdot\xi)
, and define the linear functions
h_{i}(Y):=B(Z_{i},Y)
such that
\nabla h_{i}\equiv Z_{i}
is constant. Choose a transcendence basis
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{\rho_{A}}
of
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
and basic
G
-invariants
C_{i_{1}},\ldots,C_{i_{s}}\in S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}
with

\displaystyle\theta_{\ell}=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{i_{\ell}})\qquad\ell=1,\ldots,s,

such that
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{s}
are algebraically independent. Recall that

(4.64)

\displaystyle\Phi=\bigl(P^{*}h_{1},\ldots,P^{*}h_{n-r}\,;\,P^{*}C_{i_{1}},\ldots,P^{*}C_{i_{s}}\,;\,\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{1},\ldots,\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{\rho_{A}}\bigr).

To conclude the proof, we compute the Jacobian of
\Phi
.

We now write down the Hamiltonian vector fields with respect to
\omega_{\varepsilon}
. For
f_{\theta}:=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta
with
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
(so
f_{\theta}([g,X])=\theta(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(\xi))
), solving
df_{\theta}=\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,X_{f_{\theta}})
gives
20

(4.65)

\displaystyle X_{f_{\theta}}(g,X)=g_{*}|_{X}\left((\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},-\frac{1}{2}[(\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},X]_{\mathfrak{m}}-\varepsilon[W,(\nabla\theta(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}]\right).

For
I_{h}:=h\circ P
with
h\in S(\mathfrak{g})
, put
\zeta:=\nabla h(\mathrm{Ad}(g)\xi)
. Recall that from Lemma
4.9
, for a linear functional
P_{h}(Y)=\langle\eta,Y\rangle
with
\eta\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
Y\in\mathfrak{g}
, we obtain

(4.66)

\displaystyle d(h\circ P)_{(g,X)}\left(v,w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right)=B\left(\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}},w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right)-B([\zeta,w],v)

Then solving
dI_{h}=\omega_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,X_{I_{h}})
yields the following:

(4.67)

\displaystyle X_{I_{h}}(g,X)=g_{*}|_{X}\left(-(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\zeta)_{\mathfrak{m}}\ ,\ [\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\zeta,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}-\frac{1}{2}[(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\zeta)_{\mathfrak{m}},X]_{\mathfrak{m}}\right).

In particular, if
h
is
G
-invariant, then
\zeta=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\nabla h(\xi)
. Therefore,
\zeta=\nabla h(\xi)
, and the first component is
(\nabla h(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Restricting to base variations
(v,0)
tangent to
G\cdot\xi
, by the chain rule and (
4.66
), using
\nabla h_{i}\equiv Z_{i}
with
i=1,\ldots,n-r
, we deduce that

\displaystyle d(P^{*}h_{i})_{(g,X)}(v,0)=B\big(\nabla h_{i}(\mathrm{Ad}(g)\xi),\,\mathrm{Ad}(g)[v,\xi]\big)=-B\big([Z_{i},\xi],\,v\big)\text{ for all }v\in\mathfrak{m}.

Thus, if
\sum_{i}a_{i}\,d(P^{*}h_{i})_{(g,X)}(v,0)=0
for all
v\in\mathfrak{m}
and
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\left(\sum_{i}a_{i}[Z_{i},\xi]\right)_{\mathfrak{m}}=0
, and by the choice of
Z_{i}
, we get
a_{i}=0
for all
i
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\bigl(d(P^{*}h_{1}),\ldots,d(P^{*}h_{n-r})\bigr)=n-r.

For the fiber part, letting
\Theta=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{\rho_{A}})
, we have for
(0,\delta X)\in\mathfrak{m}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
,

\displaystyle d(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{j})_{(g,X)}(0,\delta X)=\left.d\theta_{j}\right|_{X}(\delta X).

Since
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{\rho_{A}}
are algebraically independent polynomials on
\mathfrak{m}
, the Jacobian criterion implies that, on a Zariski open subset of
U
,

(4.68)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\bigl(d(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{1}),\ldots,d(\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{\rho_{A}})\bigr)=\rho_{A}.

Combining the orbit and fiber parts yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,d\Phi=(n-r)+\rho_{A}\quad\text{on a Zariski open subset of }U.

The only tautological relations among the components of
\Phi
are the
s
identities

\displaystyle P^{*}C_{i_{\ell}}-\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{\ell}=0,\qquad\ell=1,\ldots,s.

Therefore, the space of linear relations among the columns of the Jacobian matrix of
\Phi
has the dimension
s
.

(3)
Inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subset R_{0}
and
\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal{B}=s
.

From step (2), on the Zariski open regular set
U
, computing the rank of the Jacobian matrix gives
\mathrm{rank}\,d\Phi=(n-r)+\rho_{A}
, and the only relations among the components are
P^{*}C_{i_{\ell}}-\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{\ell}=0
for all
\ell=1,\dots,s
. In particular, from (
4.67
) and (
4.65
), the Hamiltonian vector fields generated by the two polynomial blocks

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}:=\mathrm{span}\,\underbrace{\{X_{h_{j}\circ P}\}}_{\text{$n-r$ elements}}+\,\mathrm{span}\,\underbrace{\{X_{\theta_{k}\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}}\}}_{\text{$\rho_{A}$ elements}}

have a rank
(n-r)+\rho_{A}
on
U
, and their leaves coincide with the connected components of the generic fibers of
q:=\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1}:T^{*}M\to\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}
inside
U
. Moreover, by Lemma
4.36
,
\{\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
and from the assumption that
J_{\ell}:=P^{*}C_{i_{\ell}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{\ell}\in R_{0}
, each
J_{\ell}
is constant along the
\mathcal{D}
-leaves. Therefore,
\mathcal{D}\subset(\ker dq)
. On the other hand, using the rank identification deduced in (
4.68
), we obtain another side of the inclusion. Therefore,
(\ker dq)=\mathcal{D}
.

Now, if
f\in\mathcal{B}
, then
\{f,g\}_{\varepsilon}=0
for each component
g
of
\Phi
, so
df(X_{g})=\omega_{\varepsilon}(X_{f},X_{g})=0
for all
X_{g}\in\mathcal{D}
. Equivalently,
X_{f}\in\mathcal{D}=\ker dq
, i.e.,
f
is constant along the
q
-fibers on
U
. That is,
df|_{\ker dq}=0
. Thus,
f|_{U}
is constant along the
\mathcal{D}
leaves and therefore factors through
\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}
. Equivalently, there exists
\widetilde{F}\in R_{0}
such that

\displaystyle f|_{U}=\widetilde{F}\big(J_{1},\dots,J_{s}\big).

Since
U
is Zariski open dense and all functions involved are regular, the identity extends to all
T^{*}M
. Hence, there do not exist any other algebraic relations among the components of
\Phi
beyond
P^{*}C_{i_{\ell}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\theta_{\ell}
and
f\in R_{0}
. This proves
\mathcal{B}\subset R_{0}
.

Since
R_{0}\subset\mathcal{B}
is from Proposition
4.38
, we obtain
\mathcal{B}=R_{0}
in
U
. The algebraic independence of
\{J_{1},\ldots,J_{s}\}
on
U
implies
\mathrm{rank}\,\mathcal{B}=\mathrm{trdeg}\,\widetilde{F}(J_{1},\ldots,J_{s})=s
.

(4)
Dimension.
Let
\rho_{A}:=\mathrm{trdeg}\,S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. The Jacobian calculation shows that, modulo the
s
relations
u_{\ell}=0
, the differentials of the generators in
\Phi
are independent of
U
. Hence,
\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{trdeg}\mathcal{A}=(n-r)+\rho_{A}
. For generic
\xi\in(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{reg}}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}
is of dimension
r
as
\xi
is regular, and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{\xi}=(\mathfrak{a}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})\oplus(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}},\qquad\dim(\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})_{\mathfrak{m}}=s\text{ by Proposition }\ref{pro:dimension},

so
\dim(\mathfrak{a}\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\xi})=r-s
. Hence
\dim(A\cdot\xi)=\dim\mathfrak{a}-(r-s)
, and by the rank formula

\displaystyle\rho_{A}=\dim\mathfrak{m}-\dim(A\cdot\xi)=(n-\dim A)-\bigl(\dim A-(r-s)\bigr)=n-2\dim A+r-s.

Therefore,

\dim\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}=(n-r)+\rho_{A}=2(n-\dim A)-s=2\dim\mathfrak{m}-s.

The two stated specializations follow by substituting
\dim A=r
,
s=r
when
A=T
and
W
are regular, and by allowing
s\leq r
when
A\neq T
and
W
are irregular while
\xi
remains regular.
∎

Since
(\mathcal{A},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{A}})
is a finitely generated Poisson algebra,
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}
is a Poisson variety. Then the evaluation map
\pi_{1}:(T^{*}M,\omega_{\varepsilon})\rightarrow\left(\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
is Poisson. Moreover, another canonical projection
\pi_{2}:\left(\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\rightarrow\left(\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{0}\right)
is also Poisson, as
R_{0}
is a Poisson center. Together with everything above, by Definition
2.1
, we deduce the following superintegrable systems.

Theorem 4.65
.

Let
G
be a compact, simply-connected, semisimple Lie group, and let
A=\exp(\ker\mathrm{ad}(W))\supseteq T
. Suppose that
(T^{*}M,\omega_{\varepsilon})
is the twisted symplectic manifold. On the Zariski open dense regular locus
U\subset T^{*}M
, we then have the following arguments:

(i) if
W
is irregular and
T\subsetneq A
. Then the system of the Poisson chain

(4.69)

\displaystyle T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)\times_{R_{0}}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//A\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathrm{Spec}R_{0}

is superintegrable.

(ii) If
W
is regular, then
R_{0}=P^{*}\left(S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\right)
and

(4.70)

\displaystyle T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathfrak{g}\times_{\mathfrak{g}//G}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//T\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathfrak{g}//G

is superintegrable.

Remark 4.66
.

Theorem
4.65
can be viewed as a Poisson projection chain approach for the two natural families of integrals arising from a Hamiltonian
G
-action: the pullback of functions on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
along the magnetic moment map and the
G
-invariant functions. In our homogeneous magnetic setting, the second family admits a canonical algebraic model via
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
, and the common central part
R_{0}
controls the reduction to the second stage of the Poisson chain.

Finally, we will have a look at the relations between the following two superintegrable systems. Let us recap some notations applied in Subsection
3
. Let
\chi:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}//G
be the canonical quotient, and let

\displaystyle\chi_{T}:\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}//T,\qquad\rho:\mathfrak{g}//T\longrightarrow\mathfrak{g}//G

be the morphisms induced by
S(\mathfrak{g})^{G}\hookrightarrow S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}\hookrightarrow S(\mathfrak{g})
such that
\chi=\rho\circ\chi_{T}
. The slice
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
is
T
-invariant. Define

\displaystyle\overline{\chi}_{T}:(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//T\ \longrightarrow\ \mathfrak{g}//G,\qquad[u]_{T}\longmapsto\chi(u-\varepsilon W),

where
[u]_{T}
denotes the class of
u:=X\in(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//T
. Finally, the fiber product is defined by:

\displaystyle Y:=\mathfrak{g}\times_{\mathfrak{g}//G}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//T=\{(X,[u]_{T}):\chi(X)=\chi(u-\varepsilon W)\}.

Equip
Y
with the unique Poisson structure, making the projections
\mathrm{pr}_{1}:Y\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
and
\pi_{2}:Y\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}//G
Poisson. Applying the definition of the equivalent of two superintegrable systems and Remark
2.6
, we are able to define the following equivalent relations of superintegrable chains with different phase spaces.

Corollary 4.67
.

Consider the superintegrable system chains

\displaystyle T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{1}\ }Y\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{2}\ }\mathfrak{g}//G,\qquad\begin{matrix}\pi_{1}(g,X):=\left(P(g,X),[u]_{T}\right),\\
\pi_{2}\left(P(g,X),[u]_{T}\right):=(C_{1},\ldots,C_{r})\end{matrix}

and
\mathfrak{g}\xrightarrow{\ \chi_{T}\ }\mathfrak{g}//T\xrightarrow{\ \rho\ }\mathfrak{g}//G
. These two systems are spectrally equivalent in the sense of Definition
2.5
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{*}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{g}\times_{\mathfrak{g}//G}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{g}//G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{g}//T=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{g})^{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{g}//G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowright}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circlearrowright}

via the Poisson maps

(4.71)

\displaystyle\phi:=P:T^{*}M\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{*},\qquad\phi_{1}:=\chi_{T}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1}:Y\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}//T,\qquad\phi_{2}:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{g}//G},

which satisfy the commutation relations

\displaystyle\chi_{T}\circ\phi=\phi_{1}\circ\pi_{1},\qquad\rho\circ\phi_{1}=\pi_{2}.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.18
and Theorem
4.65
, these two chains are superintegrable. From the assumption above, we have

\phi=P
,
\phi_{1}:=\chi_{T}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1}
, and
\phi_{2}:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{g}//G}
. It is clear that all the morphisms defined in (
4.71
) are Poisson. Indeed,
\phi
by Definition
4.5
,
\phi_{1}
as a composition of Poisson maps, and
\phi_{2}=\mathrm{id}
is the identity map and hence Poisson. For
(g,X)\in T^{*}M
,

\displaystyle\chi\bigl(P(g,X)\bigr)=\chi\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}(g)(u-\varepsilon W)\bigr)=\chi(u-\varepsilon W)=\overline{\chi}_{T}([u]_{T}),

so
\pi_{1}
is well defined with
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\pi_{1}=P
. Hence,

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\circ\pi_{1}=(\chi_{T}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1})\circ\pi_{1}=\chi_{T}\circ P=\chi_{T}\circ\phi,

gives the communication of the left part of the diagram. In
Y
, we have
\chi\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1}=\pi_{2}
by the definition of the fiber product, while
\chi=\rho\circ\chi_{T}
in
\mathfrak{g}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho\circ\phi_{1}=\rho\circ\chi_{T}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1}=\chi\circ\mathrm{pr}_{1}=\pi_{2},

gives the communication of the right part of the diagram. Thus, the diagram is commutative. By Definition
2.5
, these two systems are spectrally equivalent.
∎

Remark 4.68
.

In general,
\phi
is a Poisson submersion, so the systems need not be equivalent in the diffeomorphic sense. Unless
P
is a local diffeomorphism, which fails in this case. See Lemma
4.17
. Hence, neither
\phi
nor
\phi_{1}
is a diffeomorphism. Hence, the systems are not equivalent in the strong sense.

5.
Superintegrable systems on the reductive homogeneous spaces for
G=\mathrm{SU}(3)

With the Poisson projection chain construction discussed in Section
4
, we will implement and verify these results in Section
5
on the cotangent bundle of the reductive homogeneous space
\mathrm{SU}(3)/A
. We will work out explicit generators, the twisted Poisson bracket, and the rank account. Throughout Section
5
, let
G=\mathrm{SU}(3)
. Recall that, from the classification of adjoint orbits of compact Lie groups
29
and
M=G/A
, they are classified into regular and irregular cases based on the position of the stabiliser in the root diagram. Consider the complexification of
\mathfrak{su}(3)
. The simple roots are given by
\alpha_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}
and
\alpha_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{3}
. Writing a diagonal element as
H=\mathrm{diag}(c_{1},c_{2},c_{3})
with
c_{1}+c_{2}+c_{3}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(H)=c_{1}-c_{2},\qquad\alpha_{2}(H)=c_{2}-c_{3},\qquad(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})(H)=c_{1}-c_{3}.

By Proposition
4.2
,
H
is regular if all three diagonal entries are pairwise distinct.

Section
5
is divided into two parts, in Subsection
5.1
, we begin by choosing a fixed
W\in\mathfrak{t}
as a regular value. That is, the reductive homogeneous space is the full flag manifold
\mathrm{SU}(3)/T
, and using Chevalley coordinates to describe the
T
-invariant polynomials and their closed Poisson algebra. In this way, superintegrability and its action-angle coordinates are established. Then, in Subsection
5.2
, with an irregular
W
, we will follow the same route to demonstrate superintegrability through the Poisson projection chains. In this case, rather than using the Chevalley basis, we implement the Gell-Mann basis to proceed with the computation. Both examples illustrate how the general moment map construction produces explicit first integrals and magnetic geodesic flows on homogeneous symplectic manifolds. Furthermore, angle-action coordinates are provided to conclude each subsection.

5.1.
Superintegrable systems on
\mathrm{SU}(3)/T

In Subsection
5.1
, we consider the full flag manifold of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. That is,
\mathrm{SU}(3)/T
with the maximal torus
T\subset\mathrm{SU}(3)
. The Weyl group is
\mathcal{W}\cong S_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{su}(3)
admits a Cartan decomposition
4
4

4

It is also a reductive decomposition, so the construction in Section
4
applies here.

\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
, where
\mathfrak{t}=\mathrm{Lie}(T)
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{3}\bigl(\mathbb{R}\,E_{\alpha_{k}}\oplus\mathbb{R}\,E_{-\alpha_{k}}\bigr),\qquad\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}.

To compute explicit brackets, choose a Chevalley basis
{H_{i},E_{\alpha},E_{-\alpha}}
for
\mathfrak{su}(3)
with
i=1,2
as the simple roots
\alpha_{i}
, and
\alpha_{3}
as the highest root. In the dual space
\mathfrak{su}^{*}(3)
, we have coordinate functions
p_{i}
and
x_{\alpha},x_{-\alpha}
corresponding to
H_{i}
and
E_{\pm\alpha_{i}}
that generate the symmetric algebra
S(\mathfrak{su}(3))
. The Lie-Poisson brackets are determined by: For simple roots
i,j=1,2
,

\displaystyle\{h_{i},h_{j}\}=0,\text{ }\{h_{i},x_{\alpha_{j}}\}=a_{ij}x_{\alpha_{j}}\text{ and }\{h_{i},x_{-\alpha_{j}}\}=-a_{ij}x_{-\alpha_{j}},\text{ }\{x_{\alpha_{i}},x_{-\alpha_{j}}\}=\delta_{ij}h_{i}.

Here,
a_{ij}
is the Cartan matrix with
a_{ii}=2
,
a_{12}=a_{21}=-1
for
A_{2}
. Let

\displaystyle H_{1}=\mathrm{i}\begin{pmatrix}1&&\\
&-1&\\
&&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad H_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&&\\
&1&\\
&&-2\end{pmatrix}

span the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{su}(3)
, where
\mathrm{i}=\sqrt{-1}
is a complex number. We also denote the root vector by
E_{\pm\alpha_{1}}=E_{12},E_{21}
,
E_{\pm\alpha_{2}}=E_{23},E_{32}
, and
E_{\pm\alpha_{3}}=E_{13},E_{31}
. Here
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in\Phi^{+}
. Then for any
X\in\mathfrak{su}(3)
, we have

\displaystyle X=h_{1}H_{1}+h_{2}H_{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\bigl(x_{k}E_{\alpha_{k}}+y_{k}E_{-\alpha_{k}}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{su}(3),\qquad z_{k}=x_{k}+\mathrm{i}\,y_{k},\text{ }k=1,2,3,

where
(h_{1},h_{2},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
are the real coordinates, and
z_{k}
is the complex coordinate corresponding to the root space
E_{\pm\alpha_{k}}
. We now fix a regular element
W=\frac{1}{2}(H_{1}+H_{2})\in\mathfrak{t}
. Fix the
G
-invariant inner product
B
in
\mathfrak{su}(3)
such that
B(E_{\alpha},E_{-\alpha})=1
,
B(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{m})=0
, and
B
is
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant. Throughout Subsection
5.1
, we use
B(X,Y)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(XY)
.
From the discussion in Section
4
,
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)\cong\mathrm{SU}(3)\times_{T}\mathfrak{m}^{*}
with the magnetic form
\omega_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+\varepsilon\,\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
, where the base form
\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
at
[e]:=eT\in M=\mathrm{SU}(3)/T
is
\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}([v_{1}],[v_{2}])=B(W,[v_{1},v_{2}])
for any
[v_{1}],[v_{2}]\in T_{[e]}M
.
The magnetic moment map is as follows:

\displaystyle P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr)\in\mathfrak{su}(3)^{*},\qquad P_{j}:=\langle P,\xi_{j}\rangle

for a fixed
B
-orthonormal basis
\{\xi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{8}
of
\mathfrak{su}(3)
, where
\xi_{j}
is the relabelling of the real coordinates of
\mathfrak{su}(3)
given above.

5.1.1.
Constructing superintegrable systems on
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)

Let us first consider the linear coordinate of
\mathfrak{su}^{*}(3)
in complex coordinates.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}(\mathbb{C})
with the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}
and its root space decomposition
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
with
\dim\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha}=1
. Choose root vectors
E_{\pm\alpha_{k}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha_{k}}
for the three positive roots
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
, and the Killing form
B
such that

(5.1)

\displaystyle B(E_{\alpha_{k}},E_{-\alpha_{\ell}})=\delta_{k\ell},\qquad B(\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha})=0,\qquad B(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{g}_{\beta})=0\text{ if }\alpha+\beta\neq 0.

Take
E_{\pm\alpha_{k}}
in
\mathfrak{sl}(3,\mathbb{C})
such that the normalization in (
5.1
) holds. Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(3)
be the compact real form with the conjugation
E_{-\alpha_{k}}=\overline{E_{\alpha_{k}}}
. Then every
\zeta\in\mathfrak{g}
admits a unique expansion

\displaystyle\zeta=H+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\Big(z_{k}\,E_{\alpha_{k}}+\overline{z_{k}}\,E_{-\alpha_{k}}\Big),\qquad H\in\mathfrak{t},\ z_{k}\in\mathbb{C},

and the coefficients are:

(5.2)

\displaystyle z_{k}:=z_{k}(\zeta)=B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha_{k}}),\qquad\overline{z_{k}}:=\overline{z_{k}}(\zeta)=B(\zeta,E_{\alpha_{k}})\quad k=1,2,3.

Proof.

By root decomposition and orthogonality,
B(\mathfrak{t}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathfrak{g}_{\pm\alpha})=0
and
B(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{g}_{\beta})=0
hold unless
\beta=-\alpha
. Using the normalization
B(E_{\alpha_{k}},E_{-\alpha_{\ell}})=\delta_{k\ell}
, we compute

\displaystyle B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha_{j}})=B\Big(H+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\big(z_{k}E_{\alpha_{k}}+\overline{z_{k}}E_{-\alpha_{k}}\big),\,E_{-\alpha_{j}}\Big)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}z_{k}\,B(E_{\alpha_{k}},E_{-\alpha_{j}})=z_{j}.

The formula for
\overline{z_{j}}
is analogous.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
with
\sigma(E_{\alpha})=-E_{-\alpha}
be a
B
-invariant conjugation such that
\sigma^{2}=\mathrm{id}
. Then

\displaystyle\overline{B(\zeta,E_{\alpha})}=B(\sigma(\zeta),\sigma(E_{\alpha}))=B(\zeta,-E_{-\alpha})=-B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha}).

Hence
B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha})=-\overline{B(\zeta,E_{\alpha})}
.

We first provide the generators in the algebras
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. Starting with the generators in
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. By Lemma
4.34
, we have
\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\dim\mathfrak{su}(3)=8
. Hence, we can select eight basis elements, resulting in eight coordinate functions denoted by
P_{k}=P_{\xi_{k}}
, where
k=1,\ldots,8
. In particular, the linearity of the generators ensures algebraic independence. Consequently, we define the Poisson polynomial algebra as

\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\mathbb{R}[P_{1},\ldots,P_{8}],\qquad\text{with}\ \deg P_{k}=1.

Summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.3
.

We have
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}(S(\mathfrak{su}(3))=\mathbb{R}[P_{1},\ldots,P_{8}]
with the commutator relations
\{P_{i},P_{j}\}_{1}=c_{ij}^{k}P_{k}
.

Let us now focus on the structure of the algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, specifically considering the root coordinates
z_{k}
. By Lemma
5.1
, the complex root coordinate functions can be expressed in the following way:

(5.3)

\displaystyle z_{k}(g,X)=\langle P(g,X),E_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle=\exp(-\mathrm{i}\langle\alpha_k,\phi(g)\rangle)\,(x_{k}+iy_{k}),\quad k=1,2,3,

where
\phi(g)\in\mathfrak{t}
satisfies
g=\exp(\phi(g))\in T
, and
\mathrm{i}=\sqrt{-1}
is a complex number. We refer the reader to
13
the Cartan centraliser for the complexified case
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
, where the Racah algebra is embedded in the Cartan centraliser of type
A_{n}

23
. For the general classification of the explicit expression of the Cartan commutant for
B_{n},C_{n}
and
D_{n}
, see
10
;
12
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The Poisson algebra
S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}
of the
T
-invariant polynomials with a Poisson structure
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{2}
induced by
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\varepsilon}
is minimally generated by the five polynomials as follows:

\displaystyle u_{k}=|z_{k}|^{2}\,,\text{ }k=1,2,3,\qquad v=\real(z_{1}z_{2}\bar{z}_{3}),\qquad w=\mathrm{Im}(z_{1}z_{2}\bar{z}_{3}),

subject to the single cubic relation
u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}=v^{2}+w^{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\left(\mathbb{R}[u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v,w]\Big/\langle u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}-v^{2}-w^{2}\rangle\right),\qquad\deg u_{k}=2,\,\deg v=\deg w=3.

The Poisson brackets among these generators are

\displaystyle\{u_{1},u_{2}\}_{2}=2v,\ \{u_{2},u_{3}\}=2v,\ \ \ \{u_{3},u_{1}\}_{2}=2v,

\displaystyle\{u_{1},v\}_{2}=u_{1}(u_{3}-u_{2})-c_{1}w,\quad\{u_{2},v\}_{2}=u_{2}(u_{1}-u_{3})-c_{2}w,

(5.4)

\displaystyle\{u_{3},v\}_{2}=u_{3}(u_{2}-u_{1})-c_{3}w,\quad\{u_{k},w\}_{2}=c_{k}v,

\displaystyle\{v,w\}_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\big(c_{3}u_{1}u_{2}-c_{2}u_{1}u_{3}-c_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\big),

where
1\leq k\leq 3
and
c_{k}=\varepsilon B(W,H_{\alpha_{k}})
.

Proof.

We first compute the
T
-invariant generators in
S(\mathfrak{m})
. By Lemma
5.1
, we deduce

(5.5)

\displaystyle u_{k}:=|z_{k}|^{2}=x_{k}^{2}+y_{k}^{2}\quad\text{ with }k=1,2,3,\qquad v+\mathrm{i}w:=z_{1}\,z_{2}\,\overline{z_{3}}.

We first show that the polynomials defined in (
5.5
) are
T
-invariant. It is obvious for
u_{k}
as
\alpha_{k}+(-\alpha_{k})=0
. Moreover, for
z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}
, we use the root identity
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}=0
since the total weight is zero. Hence, it is
T
-invariant. We then take its real and imaginary parts
v=\real(\cdot)
,
w=\mathrm{Im}(\cdot)
. A general
T
-invariant monomial is
z_{1}^{a_{1}}z_{2}^{a_{2}}z_{3}^{a_{3}}\overline{z_{1}}^{b_{1}}\overline{z_{2}}^{b_{2}}\overline{z_{3}}^{b_{3}}
with weight
(a_{1}-b_{1})\alpha_{1}+(a_{2}-b_{2})\alpha_{2}+(a_{3}-b_{3})\alpha_{3}
. That is, by the definition of the Cartan commutant

\displaystyle(a_{1}-b_{1})\alpha_{1}+(a_{2}-b_{2})\alpha_{2}+(a_{3}-b_{3})(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})=0,

which is equivalent to the linear system
(a_{1}-b_{1})+(a_{3}-b_{3})=0
and
(a_{2}-b_{2})+(a_{3}-b_{3})=0
. Hence,
a_{1}-b_{1}=-(a_{3}-b_{3})
and
a_{2}-b_{2}=-(a_{3}-b_{3})
. Writing
d:=a_{3}-b_{3}
, we can decompose any invariant monomial as a product of the powers of
|z_{k}|^{2}=u_{k}
(which accounts for the symmetric part) and
(z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}})^{d}
(which accounts for the weight
d
), together with its complex conjugate, if needed, to keep the coefficients real. Thus, the invariant ring is generated by
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
and
z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}
(hence, by
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v,w
). In addition, a routine computation shows that there is an algebraic relation between these five invariants:

(5.6)

\displaystyle u_{1}\,u_{2}\,u_{3}=|z_{1}|^{2}\,|z_{2}|^{2}\,|z_{3}|^{2}=|z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}|^{2}=v^{2}+w^{2}.

We then calculate the Poisson bracket relations from all the
5
generators
\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},w,v\}
. By Proposition
4.19
, the Poisson bracket defined on
S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}
is given by
\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\}_{2}(\xi):=-B\left(\xi,\bigl[(\nabla\theta_{1}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},(\nabla\theta_{2}(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}\bigr]\right)
. In other words, we need to evaluate the gradient of the coordinate functions. By Lemma
5.1
, the linear functional of coordinates is given by
z_{k}(\zeta)=B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha_{k}})
, and
\overline{z_{k}}(\zeta)=B(\zeta,E_{\alpha_{k}})
are linear functionals. A direct calculation shows that
\nabla z_{k}=E_{-\alpha_{k}}
and
\nabla\overline{z_{k}}=E_{\alpha_{k}}
. Therefore,

(A)

\displaystyle\nabla u_{k}=\nabla(z_{k}\overline{z_{k}})=\overline{z_{k}}\,E_{\alpha_{k}}+z_{k}\,E_{-\alpha_{k}}.

Writing
F:=z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}
such that
v=\frac{1}{2}(F+\overline{F})
,
w=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}(F-\overline{F})
,

(B)

\displaystyle\nabla F=z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,E_{-\alpha_{1}}+z_{1}\overline{z_{3}}\,E_{-\alpha_{2}}+z_{1}z_{2}\,E_{\alpha_{3}},\qquad\nabla\overline{F}=\overline{z_{2}}z_{3}\,E_{\alpha_{1}}+\overline{z_{1}}z_{3}\,E_{\alpha_{2}}+\overline{z_{1}}\,\overline{z_{2}}\,E_{-\alpha_{3}}.

From (A) and (B), we first deduce
(\theta)_{\mathfrak{m}}=\theta
for any
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}
.

We now compute the Poisson bracket
\{u_{1},u_{2}\}_{2}
. Using (A) and (B),

\displaystyle[\nabla u_{1},\nabla u_{2}]=z_{1}z_{2}\,[E_{-\alpha_{1}},E_{-\alpha_{2}}]+\overline{z_{1}}\,\overline{z_{2}}\,[E_{\alpha_{1}},E_{\alpha_{2}}]=\overline{z_{1}}\,\overline{z_{2}}\,E_{\alpha_{3}}-\,z_{1}z_{2}\,E_{-\alpha_{3}}.

Therefore, by Lemma
5.1
and its remark, we have
B(\zeta,E_{\alpha_{3}})=-z_{3}
and
B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha_{3}})=\overline{z_{3}}
, and hence

\displaystyle\{u_{1},u_{2}\}_{2}=-B\big(\zeta,\,[\nabla u_{1},\nabla u_{2}]\big)=-\Big(\overline{z_{1}}\,\overline{z_{2}}\,B(\zeta,E_{\alpha_{3}})-z_{1}z_{2}\,B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha_{3}})\Big)=\overline{z_{1}}\,\overline{z_{2}}\,z_{3}+z_{1}z_{2}\,\overline{z_{3}}=2\,\real(z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}})=2\,v.

Now, we focus on the Poisson bracket
\{u_{1},F\}_{2}
. Similar to what we did above, a direct computation shows that

\displaystyle[\nabla u_{1},\nabla F]

\displaystyle=\left[\,\overline{z_{1}}E_{\alpha_{1}}+z_{1}E_{-\alpha_{1}},\ z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}E_{-\alpha_{1}}+z_{1}\overline{z_{3}}E_{-\alpha_{2}}+z_{1}z_{2}E_{\alpha_{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{z_{1}}\,z_{1}z_{2}\,[E_{\alpha_{1}},E_{\alpha_{3}}]+\overline{z_{1}}\,z_{1}\overline{z_{3}}\,[E_{\alpha_{1}},E_{-\alpha_{2}}]+\overline{z_{1}}\,z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,[E_{\alpha_{1}},E_{-\alpha_{1}}]

\displaystyle\quad+z_{1}\,z_{1}\overline{z_{3}}\,[E_{-\alpha_{1}},E_{-\alpha_{2}}]+z_{1}\,z_{1}z_{2}\,[E_{-\alpha_{1}},E_{\alpha_{3}}]+z_{1}\,z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,[E_{-\alpha_{1}},E_{-\alpha_{1}}]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,\overline{z_{1}}}_{\;=\ \overline{z_{1}}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}}\,H_{\alpha_{1}}-\underbrace{z_{1}\overline{z_{1}}\,\overline{z_{3}}}_{\;=\ |z_{1}|^{2}\overline{z_{3}}}\,E_{-\alpha_{3}}+\underbrace{z_{1}\overline{z_{1}}\,z_{2}}_{\;=\ |z_{1}|^{2}z_{2}}\,E_{\alpha_{2}}.

Then the twisted Poisson bracket
\{u_{1},F\}_{2}
is calculated as follows:

\displaystyle\{u_{1},F\}_{2}=-\,B(\zeta,[\nabla u_{1},\nabla F])=-\Big(\overline{z_{1}}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,B(\zeta,H_{\alpha_{1}})-|z_{1}|^{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha_{3}})+|z_{1}|^{2}z_{2}\,B(\zeta,E_{\alpha_{2}})\Big).

Using
B(\zeta,H_{\alpha_{1}})=B(X,H_{\alpha_{1}})-\varepsilon\,c_{1}
and
B(\zeta,E_{-\alpha_{3}})=z_{3},\ B(\zeta,E_{\alpha_{2}})=\overline{z_{2}}
, we obtain

(5.7)

\displaystyle\{u_{1},F\}_{2}=-\,\overline{z_{1}}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,\left(B(X,H_{\alpha_{1}})-\varepsilon c_{1}\right)-|z_{1}|^{2}\big(|z_{2}|^{2}-|z_{3}|^{2}\big).

Finally, we compute the twisted Poisson bracket
\{u_{1},\overline{F}\}_{2}
. A similar calculation from (A) and (B) gives

\displaystyle[\nabla u_{1},\nabla\overline{F}]=-\,z_{1}\overline{z_{2}}z_{3}\,H_{\alpha_{1}}+|z_{1}|^{2}z_{3}\,E_{\alpha_{3}}-|z_{1}|^{2}\overline{z_{2}}\,E_{-\alpha_{2}}.

Hence,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\{u_{1},\overline{F}\}_{2}=z_{1}\overline{z_{2}}z_{3}\,\left(B(X,H_{\alpha_{1}})-\varepsilon c_{1}\right)-|z_{1}|^{2}\big(\,|z_{3}|^{2}-|z_{2}|^{2}\,\big).

To obtain the corresponding Poisson bracket, we then need to transform from
F,\overline{F}
to
v,w
. Since
v=\frac{1}{2}(F+\overline{F})
and
w=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}(F-\overline{F})
,

\displaystyle\{u_{1},v\}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\{u_{1},F\}_{2}+\{u_{1},\overline{F}\}_{2}\big),\qquad\{u_{1},w\}_{2}=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}\big(\{u_{1},F\}_{2}-\{u_{1},\overline{F}\}_{2}\big).

Adding (
5.7
) and (
5.8
) together cancels the terms
|z_{1}|^{2}
and yields

\displaystyle\{u_{1},v\}_{2}=-\,\real(\overline{z_{1}}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}-z_{1}\overline{z_{2}}z_{3}\Big)\left(B(X,H_{\alpha_{1}})-\varepsilon c_{1}\right)=-\,\varepsilon c_{1}\,\imaginary(z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}})+|z_{1}|^{2}\big(|z_{3}|^{2}-|z_{2}|^{2}\big).

Recognizing
w=\imaginary(z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}})
and
u_{k}=|z_{k}|^{2}
, we have

(5.9)

\displaystyle\{u_{1},v\}_{2}=u_{1}(u_{3}-u_{2})-c_{1}\,w.

Similarly,

(5.10)

\displaystyle\{u_{1},w\}_{2}=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}\big(\{u_{1},F\}_{2}-\{u_{1},\overline{F}\}_{2}\big)=\frac{c_{1}}{2}\big(\overline{z_{1}}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}+z_{1}\overline{z_{2}}z_{3}\big)=c_{1}v.

A cyclic permutation of
(1,2,3)
yields
\{u_{i},u\}_{2}
and
\{u_{i},w\}_{2}
. Finally,

\displaystyle\{v,w\}_{2}=\frac{1}{4\mathrm{i}}\big(\{F+\overline{F},F-\overline{F}\}_{2}\big)=\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}\,\{F,\overline{F}\}_{2}-B\big(\zeta,\,[\nabla F,\nabla\overline{F}]\big).

Here, we deduce that
B\big(\zeta,\,[\nabla F,\nabla\overline{F}]\big)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\Big(c_{3}u_{1}u_{2}-c_{2}u_{1}u_{3}-c_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\Big)
such that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\{v,w\}_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\Big(c_{3}u_{1}u_{2}-c_{2}u_{1}u_{3}-c_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\Big).

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

(i) Throughout Lemma
5.4
, we use normalization

(5.12)

\displaystyle c_{k}:=\varepsilon\,B\left(W,H_{\alpha_{k}}\right)\qquad k=1,2,3,

where
H_{\alpha_{k}}
are the coroots (with
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
) and
B
is the invariant bilinear form. With this choice, the Poisson twisted brackets appearing in (
5.7
), (
5.8
)-(
5.10
) are written with the symbols
c_{k}
and without an extra factor of
\varepsilon
. If one prefers to keep the magnetic term explicit, set instead

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{k}:=B\left(W,H_{\alpha_{k}}\right),

in which case the same relations become

\displaystyle\{u_{i},v\}_{2}

\displaystyle=u_{i}(u_{k}-u_{j})-\varepsilon\,\widehat{c}_{i}\,w,

\displaystyle\{u_{i},w\}_{2}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,\widehat{c}_{i}\,v,

\displaystyle\{v,w\}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\varepsilon\,\widehat{c}_{3}u_{1}u_{2}-\varepsilon\,\widehat{c}_{2}u_{1}u_{3}-\varepsilon\,\widehat{c}_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\right),

and similarly
\{u_{i},u_{j}\}_{2}=2v
. Thus, Lemma
5.4
is consistent with either convention. Hence, we adopt
c_{k}=\varepsilon B(W,H_{\alpha_{k}})
precisely to avoid an additional factor
\varepsilon
.

(ii) From the above discussion, we also deduce that the generators in the coordinate ring
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{T}
describe the coordinates on
\mathfrak{m}//T=\mathrm{Spec}\,S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}
. Note that the affine quotient
\mathfrak{m}//T
can be identified with the
5
-dimensional real hypersurface

(5.13)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}//T:=\{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{5}:u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}=v^{2}+w^{2}\}.

It contains a singularity in the locus
\{v=w=0\}
and
\{u_{i}u_{j}u_{k}=0\}
. The map
\kappa:\mathfrak{m}\to X
sending

X\mapsto(u_{1}(X),u_{2}(X),u_{3}(X),v(X),w(X))

is the canonical quotient map on invariants. Note that for any
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathfrak{m}
,
\pi(X_{1})=\pi(X_{2})
implies that there exists a
t\in T
such that
X_{2}=\mathrm{Ad}(t^{-1})X_{1}
. Composing
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}
with
\kappa
gives the composition
\kappa\circ\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}:T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)\to\mathfrak{m}//T
with the
X_{2}
values, which records exactly the generators in
S(\mathfrak{m})^{T}
. Geometrically, we could define
r_{k}:=|Z_{k}|=\sqrt{u_{k}}
and
\phi:=\mathrm{arg}\left(\frac{u_{1}u_{2}}{u_{3}}\right)
. Then

\displaystyle v=r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}\cos\phi\text{ and }w=r_{1}r_{2}r_{3}\sin\phi.

Hence, the local coordinate on
\mathfrak{m}//T
is given by
(r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},\phi)
.

The intersection consists of those elements of the
P^{*}(\mathfrak{su}(3))
and
T
-invariant generators after restriction to
\mathfrak{m}
. By Chevalley’s theorem, the
G
-invariant polynomials in
\mathfrak{su}(3)
are generated by the quadratic and cubic Casimirs

(5.14)

\displaystyle C_{2}(Y)=\sum_{i=1}^{8}Y_{i}^{2},\qquad C_{3}(Y)=\sum_{i,j,k}^{8}d_{i,j,k}Y_{i}Y_{j}Y_{k},

and their restrictions
R_{2}:=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{2})=u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3},
and
R_{3}:=\mathrm{Res}_{W}(C_{3})=2v
are clearly non-zero. Hence,

\displaystyle R_{0}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}\bigl(S(Z_{P})^{G}\bigr)=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\bigl(\mathbb{R}[R_{2},R_{3}]\bigr)=\mathbb{R}[J_{2},J_{3}],

where
J_{2}:=P^{*}C_{2}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}R_{2}
and
J_{3}:=P^{*}C_{3}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}R_{3}
. We now focus on the joint of two algebras. From the discussion in Section
4
, it is sufficient to study the algebra
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
be the same as defined in Lemma
5.3
and Lemma
5.4
, respectively. Then
\mathcal{A}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is given by

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\left(\frac{\mathbb{R}\bigl[P_{1},\ldots,P_{8},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v,w\bigr]}{\left\langle u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}-v^{2}-w^{2},C_{2}-\left(u_{1}+u_{2}+u_{3}\right),C_{3}-2v\right\rangle}\right).

In particular,
(\mathcal{A},\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{A}})
