is a Poisson algebra with the following commutator relations:

\displaystyle\{P_{k},P_{j}\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=c_{kj}^{i}\,P_{i},

\displaystyle\{P_{k},u_{k}\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\{P_{k},v\}_{\mathcal{A}}=\{P_{k},w\}_{\mathcal{A}}=0,

\displaystyle\{u_{i},u_{j}\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=2\,v,

(5.16)

\displaystyle\{u_{i},v\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=u_{i}\,(u_{k}-u_{j})-c_{i}\,w,

\displaystyle\{u_{i},w\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=c_{i}\,v,

\displaystyle\{v,w\}_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(c_{3}u_{1}u_{2}-c_{2}u_{1}u_{3}-c_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\bigr),

where
1\leq i,j,k\leq 3
and
c_{i}=\varepsilon B(W,H_{\alpha_{i}})
.

Proof.

Due to the property of left
G
-equivariance, it suffices to compute at
g=e
. Write
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
, and let
P_{Y}:=\langle P,Y\rangle
be a coordinate functional such that
P_{i}=P_{\xi_{i}}
. By Lemma
4.9
, we deduce that

(5.17)

X_{P_{Y}}(e,X)=\left.\dfrac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\left(e,X+t(Y)_{\mathfrak{m}}+tW-\frac{t}{2}[(Y)_{\mathfrak{m}},X]\right)=\Big(-(Y)_{\mathfrak{m}}\,,\,\frac{1}{2}[(Y)_{\mathfrak{m}},X]\,-\,[Y,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}\Big).

Now, to conclude (
5.6
), it is sufficient to show that
\{\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
. For any
\theta(\xi)\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, by the definition of the twisted Poisson bracket,
\{P_{Y},\theta(\xi)\}_{\varepsilon}=d(\theta(\xi))\big(X_{P_{Y}}\big)
. Together with the Hamiltonian vector field (
5.17
), the differential of
\theta
in the coordinate
\left(v,w-\frac{1}{2}[v,X]\right)
is given by

\displaystyle\{P_{Y},\theta(\xi)\}_{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle\,B\left(\nabla\theta(\xi),\,\Big(\frac{1}{2}[(Y)_{\mathfrak{m}},X]-[Y,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\big[-(Y)_{\mathfrak{m}},X\big]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,B\big(\nabla\theta(\xi),\,-[Y,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}\big).

The decomposition of
Y=(Y)_{\mathfrak{t}}+(Y)_{\mathfrak{m}}
shows
[Y,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}=[(Y)_{\mathfrak{t}},X]
as
[(Y)_{\mathfrak{m}},X]\in\mathfrak{t}\oplus(\text{sum of root lines})
has no
\mathfrak{m}
-component along the
T
-invariant gradient. Hence,

(5.18)

\{P_{Y},\theta(\xi)\}_{\varepsilon}=B\big(\nabla\theta(\xi),\,-[(Y)_{\mathfrak{t}},X]\big).

Now, for any
\theta\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
, compute (
5.18
) using the
A_{2}
root relations. First, for
u_{k}(\xi)=|z_{k}|^{2}
, as presented in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, we have
\nabla u_{k}(\xi)=z_{k}\,E_{\alpha_{k}}+\overline{z_{k}}\,E_{-\alpha_{k}}
. For any
H\in\mathfrak{t}
, we have

\displaystyle[H,X]=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\alpha_{i}(H)\,z_{i}\,E_{\alpha_{i}}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\alpha_{i}(H)\,\overline{z_{i}}\,E_{-\alpha_{i}}.

Hence, using
B(E_{\alpha_{i}},E_{-\alpha_{j}})=\delta_{ij}
and
B(E_{\pm\alpha_{i}},E_{\pm\alpha_{j}})=0
,

\displaystyle B\big(\nabla u_{k}(\xi),[H,X]\big)=-\,\alpha_{k}(H)\,|z_{k}|^{2}+\alpha_{k}(H)\,|z_{k}|^{2}=0.

Substituting
H=(Y)_{\mathfrak{t}}
into (
5.18
) gives
\{P_{Y},u_{k}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
.

Next, let
F:=z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}
and note that
v=\mathrm{Re}F
,
w=\mathrm{Im}F
. The gradient with respect to
B
is

\displaystyle\nabla F(\xi)=z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,E_{-\alpha_{1}}+z_{1}\overline{z_{3}}\,E_{-\alpha_{2}}+z_{1}z_{2}\,E_{\alpha_{3}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle B\big(\nabla F(\xi),[H,X]\big)

\displaystyle=\ \alpha_{1}(H)\,z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}\,z_{1}+\alpha_{2}(H)\,z_{1}\overline{z_{3}}\,z_{2}-\alpha_{3}(H)\,z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}

\displaystyle=\ \big(\alpha_{1}(H)+\alpha_{2}(H)-\alpha_{3}(H)\big)\,z_{1}z_{2}\overline{z_{3}}=0,

since
\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
. Taking real and imaginary parts yields
B\big(\nabla v(\xi),[H,X]\big)=B\big(\nabla w(\xi),[H,X]\big)=0
. With
H=(Y)_{\mathfrak{t}}
, (
5.18
) gives
\{P_{Y},v\}_{\varepsilon}=\{P_{Y},w\}_{\varepsilon}=0
. Since
P_{\xi_{i}}=P_{i}
, the three identities read

\displaystyle\{P_{j},u_{k}\}_{\varepsilon}=\{P_{j},v\}_{\varepsilon}=\{P_{j},w\}_{\varepsilon}=0\qquad j=1,\dots,8;\text{ }k=1,2,3.

Therefore, (
5.6
) is verified.
∎

We now construct the superintegrable system defined in
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)
. Define the map of affine real varieties

\displaystyle\pi_{1}:T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A},

(5.19)

\displaystyle(g,X)\quad

\displaystyle\longmapsto\,\bigl(P_{1}(g,X),\ldots,P_{8}(g,X),u_{1}(g,X),u_{2}(g,X),u_{3}(g,X),v(g,X),w(g,X)\bigr).

Here, by Corollary (
4.62
),
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}=\mathfrak{su}(3)\times_{\mathfrak{su}(3)//\mathrm{SU}(3)}(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)//T
. By construction, every coordinate of
\pi_{1}
lies in the Poisson commutative subalgebra
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
. Hence
\pi_{1}
is a Poisson morphism. Generic fibers of
\pi_{1}
have dimensions
\dim T^{*}M-\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathcal{A}=12-10=2
and are therefore isotropic. The canonical inclusion
R_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
induces an affine variety morphism

\displaystyle\pi_{2}:\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}\,\cong\,\mathbb{R}^{2}_{(J_{2},J_{3})},

given by

(5.20)

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(P_{1},\ldots,P_{8},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v,w)=\bigl(J_{2},J_{3}\bigr)=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{8}P_{k}^{2},\,2v\right).

The composite

\displaystyle T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)\,\xrightarrow{\,\pi_{1}\,}\,\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\,\xrightarrow{\,\pi_{2}\,}\,\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}

exhibits the following structure.

Proposition 5.7
.

Write
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
. Let
(h_{1},h_{2},x_{k},y_{k})_{k=1}^{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{8}
be the real coordinates given before, and let

\pi_{1}\colon T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A},\qquad\pi_{2}\colon\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}

be a chain of Poisson submersions, where

\mathcal{A}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}},\qquad R_{0}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\mathbb{R}[J_{2},J_{3}]

and
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
are the polynomial families. Then

(5.21)

\displaystyle T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)\xrightarrow{\,\pi_{1}\,}\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\,\pi_{2}\,}\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}

is a superintegrable system.

Proof.

Using Lemma
5.3
and Lemma
5.4
, we see that
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
are indeed Poisson polynomial algebra closed under the twisted Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\varepsilon}
. By Proposition
5.6
,
\mathcal{A}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
is a Poisson algebra with Poisson brackets as follows:

\displaystyle\{f\otimes\theta,f^{\prime}\otimes\theta^{\prime}\}_{\mathcal{A}}=\{f,f^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\theta\theta^{\prime}+ff^{\prime}\otimes\{\theta,\theta^{\prime}\}_{\varepsilon},\qquad f,f^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\,\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}},

and extended by bilinearity. By construction, each coordinate of the map

\displaystyle\pi_{1}:T^{*}M\to\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A},\qquad(g,X)\mapsto(P_{1},\ldots,P_{8},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v,w),

belongs to
\mathcal{A}
, and the pullback of the coordinate functions on
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}
reproduces the corresponding elements of
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
. The defining rule for
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\mathcal{A}}
therefore gives

\displaystyle\{f,g\}_{\mathcal{A}}\circ\pi_{1}=\{f\circ\pi_{1},g\circ\pi_{1}\}_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{for all}\,f,g\in\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A})\subset C^{\infty}(\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}),

so
\pi_{1}
is a Poisson map.

We now show that
d\pi_{1}
is surjective via direct computation. In other words, we show that
\mathrm{rank}\,d\pi_{1}(g,X)=10
on a Zariski open dense subset of
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)
. For each
k
, the differentiation of the coordinate functions is given by

(5.22)

\displaystyle dz_{k}(v,\delta X)=\langle[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})v,\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\xi]+\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\delta X\,,\,E_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle,

with
du_{k}=2\real(\bar{z}_{k}\,dz_{k})
, and similarly for
dv,dw
. Recall that
v
and
\delta X
represent the vertical and horizontal components of the tangent vector of
T^{*}M
. In the open regular set of
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)
with
\xi
being regular and
z_{1}z_{2}\neq 0
, the map
v\mapsto[v,\xi]
maps
\mathfrak{su}(3)/\mathfrak{t}
isomorphically onto
[\mathfrak{su}(3),\xi]
, and the directions
[\mathfrak{su}(3),\xi]
together with
\mathfrak{m}
generate
\mathfrak{su}(3)
. That is,
\mathfrak{su}(3)=[\mathfrak{su}(3),\xi]\oplus\mathfrak{m}
. Hence, the differentials
\{dP_{k}\}_{k=1}^{8}
span an
8
-dimensional subspace of covectors. In the same locus, the two differentials

(5.23)

\displaystyle du_{1}=2x_{1}dx_{1}+2y_{1}dy_{1},\quad du_{2}=2x_{2}dx_{2}+2y_{2}dy_{2}

are linearly independent with
u_{1}=x_{1}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}
and
u_{2}=x_{2}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}
in the root coordinates, and they are not contained in the span of
\{dP_{k}\}_{k=1}^{8}
.

To see this more clearly, we now introduce real coordinates
(\kappa_{j},x_{k},y_{k})
on
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)
and express the differential
d\pi_{1}
computed above by terms of its Jacobian matrix. Here,
(\kappa_{1},\ldots,\kappa_{6})
are real coordinates on
\mathrm{SU}(3)/T
. By the definition of the Jacobian matrix, we have

(5.24)

\displaystyle J_{\pi_{1}}(g,X)=\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial\kappa_{j}}(g,X)&\dfrac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial x_{k}}(g,X)&\dfrac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial y_{k}}(g,X)\\[6.0pt]
\dfrac{\partial u_{1}}{\partial\kappa_{j}}(g,X)&\dfrac{\partial u_{1}}{\partial x_{k}}(g,X)&\dfrac{\partial u_{1}}{\partial y_{k}}(g,X)\\[6.0pt]
\dfrac{\partial u_{2}}{\partial\kappa_{j}}(g,X)&\dfrac{\partial u_{2}}{\partial x_{k}}(g,X)&\dfrac{\partial u_{2}}{\partial y_{k}}(g,X)\end{pmatrix}_{10\times 12}.

Explicit calculations yield the following:

(1) Recall that the differentiation of
P
is given in (
5.3
). For the coordinates of the map of
P_{k}
, we have the following:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial x_{k}}(g,X)=\left\langle\mathrm{Ad}(g)E_{\alpha_{k}},\xi_{i}\right\rangle,\quad\frac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial y_{k}}(g,X)=\left\langle\mathrm{Ad}(g)E_{-\alpha_{k}},\xi_{i}\right\rangle.

Since
\{\mathrm{Ad}(g)(E_{\pm\alpha_{k}})\}_{k=1}^{3}
forms an orthonormal basis of the root space for the generic
g
, the
8\times 6
sub-block
\left(\dfrac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial x_{k}},\dfrac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial y_{k}}\right)
has full rank
6
.

(2) The derivatives of
P_{k}
with respect to the group parameters
\kappa_{j}
are computed as

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial\kappa_{j}}(g,X)=\left\langle[E_{\kappa_{j}},\mathrm{Ad}(g)(X-\varepsilon W)),\xi_{i}\right\rangle.

At generic points, the adjoint orbit of
X-\varepsilon W
has maximal dimension, which ensures at least two independent directions from these group variations.

(3) From (
5.23
), the differentials of
u_{1},u_{2}
are defined in a way that shows no dependence on the group parameters
\kappa_{j}
. Thus, these two rows are clearly linearly independent, giving us full rank
2
to
J_{\pi_{1}}
. Due to the generality assumption, the coordinates satisfy
x_{i},y_{i}\neq 0
.

Combining each step, the Jacobian
J_{\pi_{1}}(g,X)
in (
5.24
) is reduced to the following block form

\displaystyle J_{\pi_{1}}(g,X)=\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial\kappa_{j}}(g,X)&\frac{\partial P_{k}}{\partial(x_{k},y_{k})}(g,X)\\[6.0pt]
\textbf{0}_{2\times 6}&\mathrm{diag}(2x_{1},2y_{1},2x_{2},2y_{2},0,0)\end{pmatrix}_{10\times 12}.

Let us analyze
J_{\pi_{1}}
block by block. Since the
8\times 6
block
\partial P_{k}/\partial(x_{k},y_{k})
has a rank
6
from computation (1), and from computation (3), the
2\times 6
block for
(u_{1},u_{2})
is a rank
2
matrix, we have
6+2=8
. Additionally, the group derivatives
\partial P_{k}/\partial\kappa_{j}
provide at least two independent columns, due to the maximal dimension of the adjoint orbits at generic points. Thus, the total rank is explicitly computed from the calculations above. That is,
\mathrm{rank}\,J_{\pi_{1}}(g,X)=10
. By standard differential geometry, this confirms that the differential
d\pi_{1}(g,X)
is surjective onto the tangent space of
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\cong\mathbb{R}^{10}
. Hence, on a dense open Zariski subset,
\mathrm{rank}\,J_{\pi_{1}}(g,X)=10
, and
d\pi_{1}
is surjective. By the continuity of rank along the manifold,
\pi_{1}
is a Poisson submersion on an open dense set.

The inclusion
R_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
endows
R_{0}=\mathbb{R}[J_{2},J_{3}]
with the induced (trivial) Poisson bracket, since
J_{2},J_{3}
are Casimirs. The coordinate map

\displaystyle\pi_{2}:\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0},\qquad(p_{1},\ldots,p_{8},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},v,w)\longmapsto\Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{8}P_{k}^{2},\,2v\Bigr),

is therefore a Poisson map whose differential has constant rank
2
. Hence
\pi_{2}
is a Poisson submersion.

Finally,
\mathcal{A}
is generated by the
13
variables, subject to the three independent relations
u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}=v^{2}+w^{2}
,
J_{2}=\sum_{k}P_{k}^{2}
, and
J_{3}=2v
, so
\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathcal{A}=10
. Since
\dim T^{*}M=12
, the generic fiber of
\pi_{1}
has dimension
12-10=2
. By Definition
3.16
, the chain
T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}
is superintegrable, with
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
being Poisson, and such fibers are coisotropic in
(T^{*}M,\omega_{\varepsilon})
.
∎

5.1.2.
Construction of action-angle coordinates on
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)

To end this Subsection
5.2
, we now provide the action-angle coordinates for the superintegrable systems above. From Section
4
and Proposition
5.7
, the Poisson center of the superintegrable system in
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)
can be described by the pullback of the
\mathrm{Ad}(G)
-invariant polynomial in
\mathfrak{g}
via
P
. Then the action coordinates can be taken by the Casimir invariants as follows:

\displaystyle I_{1}:=J_{2}=C_{2}\circ P\text{ and }I_{2}:=J_{3}=C_{3}\circ P.

For the basic invariant polynomials in
\mathfrak{su}(3)
, we have
\nabla C_{2}(Y)=-2Y
and
\nabla C_{3}(Y)=6Y^{2}
.

We now find the explicit expression for the angle variables. In other words, we need to find angles
\left(\widetilde{\phi}_{1},\widetilde{\phi}_{2}\right)
such that

(5.25)

\displaystyle X_{J_{2}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\widetilde{\phi}_{1}},\quad X_{J_{3}}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\widetilde{\phi}_{2}}.

Equivalently, we need to construct the coordinate
\left(\widetilde{\phi}_{1},\widetilde{\phi}_{2}\right)
such that the following relation holds:
\left\{\widetilde{\phi}_{1},J_{2}\right\}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\widetilde{\phi}_{2},J_{3}\right\}_{\varepsilon}=1
and
\left\{\widetilde{\phi}_{2},J_{2}\right\}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\widetilde{\phi}_{1},J_{3}\right\}_{\varepsilon}=0
. By Definition
2.4
, these are
S^{1}
-valued functions. To determine the angle variable, we need to calculate the Hamiltonian vector fields for
I_{\ell}
with
\ell=1,2
as by definition, the angles are defined by the affine coordinates generated by the flows of the commuting Hamiltonians
I_{1},I_{2}
. From the Hamiltonian vector field formula in (
4.12
), we need to first provide the gradients of
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
. Using the definition of the gradient, we have
dh(X)(Y)=B(\nabla h(X),Y)=\left.\dfrac{dh}{dt}\right|_{t=0}h(X+tY)
for any
X,Y\in\mathfrak{su}(3)
and
h\in S(\mathfrak{su}(3))
. Then

\displaystyle B(\nabla J_{2}(Y),X)=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\nabla C_{2}(Y)X)=\mathrm{tr}(YX)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(YX+XY)=dC_{2}(Y)(X).

\displaystyle B(\nabla J_{3}(Y),X)=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\nabla C_{3}(Y)X\right)=-3\mathrm{tr}(Y^{2}X)=-dC_{3}(Y)(X)

Hence
\nabla C_{2}(Y)=-2Y
and
\nabla C_{3}(Y)=6Y^{2}
. Let
h
be either
C_{2}
or
C_{3}
. Since
Y
is arbitrary in
\mathfrak{su}(3)
,
[\nabla C_{\ell}(Y),Y]=0
. Therefore, along the
J_{\ell}
-flow, we must have
[\nabla C_{\ell}(P),P]=0
. By the definition of
P
, we have
\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\nabla C_{2}(P)=-2P
and
\left(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\nabla C_{2}(P)\right)_{\mathfrak{m}}=-2X
. Similarly,
\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\nabla C_{3}(P)=6\xi^{2}
. We then deduce the following lemma:

Lemma 5.8
.

With
\xi:=X-\varepsilon W
and using the magnetic symplectic form
\omega_{\varepsilon}
, the Hamiltonian vector fields at
(g,X)
are

(5.26)

\displaystyle X_{J_{2}}(g,X)

\displaystyle=g_{*}|_{X}\bigl(-2X,\;[-2\xi,X]_{\mathfrak{m}}\bigr),

(5.27)

\displaystyle X_{J_{3}}(g,X)

\displaystyle=g_{*}|_{X}\bigl((6\xi^{2})_{\mathfrak{m}},\;[6\xi^{2},X]_{\mathfrak{m}}-3[(6\xi^{2})_{\mathfrak{m}},X]_{\mathfrak{m}}\bigr).

Proof.

Recall that the Hamiltonian vector field of
P^{*}h
in (
4.19
) is given by

\displaystyle X_{h\circ P}=g_{*}|_{X}\left(v_{\eta},\;-\frac{1}{2}[v_{\eta},X]+w_{\eta}\right),

with
v_{\eta}=(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\nabla h(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
w_{\eta}=[\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\nabla h(\xi),X]_{\mathfrak{m}}-\frac{1}{2}[(\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})\nabla h(\xi))_{\mathfrak{m}},X]_{\mathfrak{m}}
. See Lemma
4.9
and the computation of
\,X_{I_{h}}
for
h\in S(\mathfrak{su}(3))
. Insert
\nabla C_{2}=-2(\cdot)
and
\nabla C_{3}=6(\cdot)^{2}
, we obtain (
5.26
)-(
5.27
).
∎

For a regular element
r=(r_{2},r_{3})\in\left(\mathfrak{su}(3)//\mathrm{SU}(3)\right)_{\mathrm{reg}}
, in a generic set of common levels

(5.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{r}:=I^{-1}(r)=\{(g,X)\in T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T):J_{2}(g,X)=r_{2},\ J_{3}(g,X)=r_{3}\}.

Locally, from the Liouville-Arnold theorem,
\mathcal{L}_{r}
is isomorphic to
T\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and the local action-angle coordinates are given by
(J_{2},J_{3},\phi_{2},\phi_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}
with
\{J_{i},\phi_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=\delta_{ij}
. In this case,
\phi_{i}
can be solved by the linear equations
\dfrac{d\phi_{i}}{dt}=r_{i}
, where
i=2,3
. As
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
are Poisson-commuting with each other under
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\varepsilon}
, and the generators in
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
are the first integrals, the Hamiltonian vector fields
X_{I_{j}}
preserve the right
T
-foliation and commute. In each
T
-orbit,
X_{I_{j}}
are therefore restricted to linear combinations of two fundamental vector fields
\widehat{H}_{1}
and
\widehat{H}_{2}
. Hence, we may define a
2\times 2
frequency matrix
\Omega(I)
such that
\widetilde{\phi}:=\Omega^{-1}(I)\phi
and
\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},I_{j}\}=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j=1,2
.

Define
Z:=\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})(\xi)\in\mathfrak{su}(3)
with
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
. We could redefine the coordinate in (
5.3
) by
z_{k}=\langle Z,E_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle=r_{k}\exp(\mathrm{i}\vartheta_k)
, where
\vartheta=\mathrm{arg}z_{k}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
is an angle. In what follows, we show that it is constant along the flow in
\mathcal{L}_{r}
. Recall that the right
T
-action is
(g,X)\cdot t(\sigma)=(g\,t^{-1}(\sigma),X)
with
t(\sigma)=\exp(\sigma)\in T
and
\sigma=\sigma_{1}H_{1}+\sigma_{2}H_{2}\in\mathfrak{t}
, where
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2}
. On root lines, the right action defined above is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}(t)(E_{\alpha_{k}})=\exp(\mathrm{i} \alpha_k(\sigma))E_{\alpha_{k}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad z_{k}\mapsto\exp(\mathrm{i} \alpha_k(\sigma))z_{k},\quad\vartheta_{k}\mapsto\vartheta_{k}-\alpha_{k}(\sigma).

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
and
Z:=\mathrm{Ad}(g^{-1})(\xi)\in\mathfrak{su}(3)
. Define the root-line coordinates (as in (
5.3
))

\displaystyle z_{k}=\langle Z,E_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle=r_{k}\,\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\vartheta_k),\qquad k=1,2,3,

with phases
\vartheta_{k}\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. For the right
T
-action

\displaystyle(g,X)\cdot t(\sigma)=(g\,t(\sigma)^{-1},X),\qquad t(\sigma)=\exp(\sigma),\quad\sigma=\sigma_{1}H_{1}+\sigma_{2}H_{2}\in\mathfrak{t},

we have the phase differentials satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\delta\vartheta_{1}\\
\delta\vartheta_{2}\\
\delta\vartheta_{3}\end{pmatrix}=-\,\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\[-2.0pt]
-1&2\\[2.0pt]
1&1\end{pmatrix}}_{\Theta}\begin{pmatrix}\delta\sigma_{1}\\
\delta\sigma_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Then the geometric
T
-angle one-forms

\displaystyle d\phi_{1}:=d\sigma_{1}=-\frac{1}{3}(d\vartheta_{1}+d\vartheta_{3}),\qquad d\phi_{2}:=d\sigma_{2}=-\frac{1}{3}(d\vartheta_{2}+d\vartheta_{3})

satisfy

\displaystyle d\phi_{j}(\widehat{H_{k}})=\delta_{jk}\qquad\text{ for any }j,k=1,2,

where
\widehat{H_{k}}
are the fundamental vector fields of the right action generated by
t_{k}(s)=\exp( sH_k)
. In particular, after fixing additive constants on each right
T
-orbit,

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\equiv-\frac{\vartheta_{1}+\vartheta_{3}}{3}\;(\mathrm{mod}\ 2\pi),\qquad\phi_{2}\equiv-\frac{\vartheta_{2}+\vartheta_{3}}{3}\;(\mathrm{mod}\ 2\pi),

so
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
parametrise the right
T^{2}
-orbits with unit speeds.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{H_{j}}
be the fundamental vector fields of the right
T
-action generated by
t_{j}(s)=\exp( s H_j)
with
j=1,2
. That is, the right
T
-orbit curves through
(g,X)
are

\displaystyle\gamma_{j}(s):=(g,X)\cdot t_{j}(s)=\left(g\exp(- s H_j),\mathrm{Ad}(t_{j}(s))X\right).

Then,
\widehat{H_{j}}
at
(g,X)
is the differentiation of the curve
\gamma_{j}
at
s=0
. By the definition of the angle coordinate, we would like to construct the one-form
d\sigma_{j}
on
\widehat{H}_{j}
such that

\displaystyle d\sigma_{j}(\widehat{H_{k}})=\delta_{jk}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad d\phi_{j}(\widehat{H_{k}})=\delta_{jk}.

Hence,
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
parametrise the right
T
-orbits with unit speeds. For any polynomial
f\in\mathcal{O}\left(T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/T)\right)
, we define

(5.29)

\displaystyle\delta_{\sigma}f:=\left.\dfrac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}f((g,X)\cdot t(\sigma(s)))=df(\hat{\zeta}),

where
\hat{\zeta}
is the fundamental vector field at
(g,X)
for the right
T
-action. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let
z_{k}(t)=\langle Z(t),E_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle
with
z_{k}(0)=\langle Z,E_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle=z_{k}
. Using the
\mathrm{Ad}
-equivalent of
B
and
[Y,E_{\alpha_{k}}]=\alpha_{k}(Y)z_{k}
, the differential of the coordinate (
5.3
) is given by

\displaystyle\left.\dfrac{dz_{k}}{dt}\right|_{t=0}=\left.\dfrac{d\langle Z(t),E_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle}{dt}\right|_{t=0}=\langle[Z(t),Y],E_{\alpha_{k}}\rangle=\langle Z(0),[Y,E_{\alpha_{k}}]\rangle=\alpha(Y)z_{k},

where
Y\in\mathfrak{t}
. Also, note that we can express
z_{k}=r_{k}\exp(\mathrm{i}\vartheta_k)
with
r_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
. This implies
\left.\dfrac{d\vartheta_{k}}{dt}\right|_{t=0}=-\mathrm{i}\alpha_{k}(Y)\in\mathbb{R}
. Now, take
Y=\delta\left(\sigma_{1}H_{1}+\sigma_{2}H_{2}\right)
. So, the vector field that moves along the right
T
-orbit with the parameter
s
is the same as the Hamiltonian vector field with a constant generator
Y
. Hence, using the fact that
\alpha_{k}(Y)=\left(\alpha_{k}(H_{1})\delta\sigma_{1}+\alpha_{k}(H_{2})\delta\sigma_{2}\right)
, we deduce the following:

(5.30)

\displaystyle\left.\dfrac{d\vartheta_{k}}{dt}\right|_{t=0}=-\left(\alpha_{k}(H_{1})\delta\sigma_{1}+\alpha_{k}(H_{2})\delta\sigma_{2}\right).

On the other hand, in the simple coroot basis
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})^{T}
, using (
5.29
), the directional derivation along the vector
(\delta\sigma_{1},\delta\sigma_{2})
is given by

(5.31)

\displaystyle\left.\dfrac{d\vartheta_{k}}{dt}\right|_{t=0}=\dfrac{\partial\vartheta_{k}}{\partial\sigma_{1}}\delta\sigma_{1}+\dfrac{\partial\vartheta_{k}}{\partial\sigma_{2}}\delta\sigma_{2}.

Comparing (
5.30
) with (
5.31
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\delta\vartheta_{1}\\
\delta\vartheta_{2}\\
\delta\vartheta_{3}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\[-2.0pt]
-1&2\\[2.0pt]
1&1\end{pmatrix}}_{:=\Theta}\begin{pmatrix}\delta\sigma_{1}\\
\delta\sigma_{2}\end{pmatrix},

and one fixes a left inverse

\displaystyle L:=\frac{1}{3}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1\\[2.0pt]
0&1&1\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{so that}\qquad L\,\Theta=I_{2}.

Here
\delta\vartheta_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\dfrac{\partial\vartheta_{i}}{\partial\sigma_{j}}d\sigma_{j}
. Define the geometric
T
-angle one-forms by

\displaystyle d\phi_{1}:=d\sigma_{1}=-\frac{1}{3}\bigl(d\vartheta_{1}+d\vartheta_{3}\bigr),\qquad d\phi_{2}:=d\sigma_{2}=-\frac{1}{3}\bigl(d\vartheta_{2}+d\vartheta_{3}\bigr),

and, after fixing additive constants on each right
T
-orbit,

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\equiv-\frac{\vartheta_{1}+\vartheta_{3}}{3}\quad(\mathrm{mod}\ 2\pi),\qquad\phi_{2}\equiv-\frac{\vartheta_{2}+\vartheta_{3}}{3}\quad(\mathrm{mod}\ 2\pi)

as required.
∎

For a regular value
r=(r_{2},r_{3})
of
(J_{2},J_{3})=(I_{1},I_{2})
, the common level
\mathcal{L}_{r}
is a (local) right
T
-orbit, as stated by the Liouville-Arnold theorem. By Lemma
5.9
, we have geometric angles
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
on the right
T
-orbits with
d\phi_{j}(\widehat{H_{k}})=\delta_{jk}
. By construction, the Hamiltonian flows generated by
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
preserve the right
T
-orbits and
\{X_{I_{1}},X_{I_{2}}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
. In each torus, the Hamiltonian vector fields
X_{I_{j}}
are constant linear combinations of the fundamental fields:

\displaystyle X_{I_{j}}=\Omega_{j1}(I)\,\widehat{H_{1}}+\Omega_{j2}(I)\,\widehat{H_{2}},\qquad j=1,2,

with a
2\times 2
frequency matrix
\Omega(I)
depending only on the actions
I:=(I_{1},I_{2})
. We then deduce the angle-action coordinate in
\mathcal{L}_{r}
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
r=(r_{2},r_{3})
be a regular value of
I=(I_{1},I_{2})=(J_{2},J_{3})
and set
\mathcal{L}_{r}:=I^{-1}(r)
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{r}
is (locally) a right
T
-orbit, the Hamiltonian fields
X_{I_{1}},X_{I_{2}}
commute and are tangent to
\mathcal{L}_{r}
, and there exists a
2\times 2
frequency matrix
\Omega(I)
such that

(5.32)

\displaystyle X_{I_{j}}=\Omega_{j1}(I)\,\widehat{H_{1}}+\Omega_{j2}(I)\,\widehat{H_{2}},\qquad\Omega_{jk}(I)=d\phi_{j}(X_{I_{k}})=\{\phi_{j},I_{k}\}_{\varepsilon}.

Defining the rescaled angles
\widetilde{\phi}:=S(I)\,\phi
with
S(I):=\Omega(I)^{-1}
, we have

(5.33)

\displaystyle\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=0,\qquad\left\{I_{i},I_{j}\right\}_{\varepsilon}=0,\qquad\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},I_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=\delta_{ij},

equivalently,
X_{J_{2}}=\partial/\partial\widetilde{\phi}_{1}
and
X_{J_{3}}=\partial/\partial\widetilde{\phi}_{2}
.

Proof.

Recall that
J_{2}=C_{2}\circ P
and
J_{3}=C_{3}\circ P
are Poisson-commute and
P
is a Poisson map. Hence,
\{J_{2},J_{3}\}_{\varepsilon}=P^{*}\{C_{2},C_{3}\}=0
. As
P
is right
T
-invariant, so are
J_{2},J_{3}
, and their flows preserve right
T
-orbits. On a regular common level
\mathcal{L}_{r}
, right
T
-orbits are contained in
\mathcal{L}_{r}
. From the assumption that
\Omega_{jk}(I)
is evaluated by applying the Hamiltonian vector fields to the angle one-forms as follows
(
1
, Chapter 10)
:

(5.34)

\displaystyle\Omega_{jk}(I)=d\phi_{j}(X_{J_{k}})=\{\phi_{j},J_{k}\}_{\varepsilon}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\dot{\phi}=\begin{pmatrix}\{\phi_{1},I_{1}\}_{\varepsilon}\\[2.0pt]
\{\phi_{2},I_{1}\}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d\phi_{1}(X_{I_{1}})\\[2.0pt]
d\phi_{2}(X_{I_{1}})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\Omega_{11}(I)\\[2.0pt]
\Omega_{12}(I)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\dot{\phi}=\begin{pmatrix}\{\phi_{1},I_{2}\}_{\varepsilon}\\[2.0pt]
\{\phi_{2},I_{2}\}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\Omega_{21}(I)\\[2.0pt]
\Omega_{22}(I)\end{pmatrix},

i.e., the angle
\phi
admits constant speed
\Omega(I)
on each torus
\dot{\phi}=\Omega(I)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
on each Liouville torus. Thus,
\dot{\phi}=\Omega(I)
along the flows in the torus. Define
\widetilde{\phi}=S(I)\phi
with
S(I):=\Omega(I)^{-1}
as the (action-dependent) linear rescaling. To conclude that
\widetilde{\phi}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\phi}_{2}
are angle coordinates, it suffices to prove that (
5.33
) holds. A direct computation shows that

\displaystyle\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},I_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=d\left(\sum_{a}S_{ia}(I)\phi_{a}\right)(X_{I_{j}})=\underbrace{\sum_{a}S_{ia}(I)\,d\phi_{a}(X_{I_{j}})}_{\text{$\sum_{a}S_{ia}(I)\Omega_{aj}=\delta_{ij}$}}+\sum_{a,k}\frac{\partial S_{ia}}{\partial I_{k}}(I)\,\underbrace{dI_{k}(X_{I_{j}})}_{\{I_{k},I_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=0}\,\phi_{a}=\delta_{ij}.

To see
\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=0
on each torus, note that for
k=1,2
,

\displaystyle\{\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\}_{\varepsilon},\,I_{k}\}_{\varepsilon}=\{\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},I_{k}\}_{\varepsilon},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}+\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},\{\widetilde{\phi}_{j},I_{k}\}_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon}=\{\delta_{ik},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}+\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},\delta_{jk}\}_{\varepsilon}=0,

so
\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}
is constant along the commuting flows. Since
I_{1},I_{2}
Poisson-commute and flows commute,

\displaystyle\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},\widetilde{\phi}_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=0,\qquad\{I_{i},I_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=0,\qquad\{\widetilde{\phi}_{i},I_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=\delta_{ij}.

Equivalently, in each Liouville torus,

\displaystyle X_{J_{2}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\widetilde{\phi}_{1}},\qquad X_{J_{3}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\widetilde{\phi}_{2}}

as required.
∎

In summary, we take two Casimir pulled back by
P
as the action coordinates
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
. As shown in Lemma
5.9
, their Hamiltonian flow preserves the right
T
-foliation. On a regular common level surface
\mathcal{L}_{r}
in (
5.28
), it restricts to a single
T
-orbit. Hence, the invariant tori are
2
-dimensional. Together with Proposition
5.10
, the coordinate of the action angle in
\mathcal{L}_{r}
is characterised by
\left(I_{1},I_{2},\widetilde{\phi}_{1},\widetilde{\phi}_{2}\right)
. Let us also remark that, in
26
, although the regular symplectic leaves in the Poisson variety
\mathfrak{su}(3)\times_{\mathfrak{su}(3)//\mathrm{SU}(3)}\mathfrak{m}//T
are
6
-dimensional, the action-angle construction is attached to the
T
-foliation, which results in a
2
-dimensional Liouville torus and therefore two angles.

5.2.
Superintegrable systems on
\mathrm{SU}(3)/A
.

In this Subsection
5.2
, we again take
\mathrm{SU}(3)
, and consider the set of traceless
3\times 3
anti-Hermitian complex matrices as its Lie algebra, denoted by
\mathfrak{su}(3)
. We use the Gell-Mann (GM) basis
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{8}
for
\mathfrak{su}(3)
with structure constants
[\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}]=2\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{8}c_{ij}^{k}\lambda_{k}
as it simplifies component calculations in the irregular case. The explicit matrix representations of these basis elements are

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=

\displaystyle\,\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\text{ }\lambda_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}&0\\
\mathrm{i}&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\text{ }\lambda_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\text{ }\lambda_{4}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&0&0\\
1&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix};

\displaystyle\lambda_{5}=

\displaystyle\,\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-\mathrm{i}\\
0&0&0\\
\mathrm{i}&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\text{ }\lambda_{6}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\text{ }\lambda_{7}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&-\mathrm{i}\\
0&\mathrm{i}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\text{ }\lambda_{8}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&-2\\
\end{pmatrix}.

For further details, including the explicit form of the GM basis
\lambda_{i}
and its transformation from the Chevalley basis, see, for example,
(
8
, Chapter II)
. Now, let

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\lambda_{3},\qquad H_{2}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\lambda_{3}-\sqrt{3}\lambda_{8}\right)

\displaystyle E_{12}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}+\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2}),\text{ }E_{21}=\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{2});

(5.35)

\displaystyle E_{13}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{4}+\mathrm{i}\lambda_{5}),\text{ }E_{31}=\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{4}-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{5});

\displaystyle E_{23}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{6}+\mathrm{i}\lambda_{7}),\text{ }E_{32}=\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{6}-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{7})

Throughout this subsection,
\mathrm{i}=\sqrt{-1}
is a complex number. We note that this basis transformation is computationally convenient. One can simply check the commutator relations, for example, by

\displaystyle[E_{12},E_{21}]=H_{1},\qquad[E_{23},E_{32}]=H_{2},\qquad[E_{13},E_{31}]=H_{1}+H_{2}.

Under the basis (
5.35
), we may write
H=c_{1}\lambda_{3}+c_{2}\lambda_{8}=\mathrm{diag}\left(c_{1}+\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{3}},-c_{1}+\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{3}},-\frac{2c_{2}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)
for some
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. By the definition of regularity, there exists a root
\alpha
such that
\alpha(H)=0
. A direct computation shows that the following element

(5.36)

\displaystyle W=\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{3}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\lambda_{8}=\mathrm{diag}(2,-1,-1)

is irregular such that the centraliser is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}:=\ker\mathrm{ad}W=\mathrm{span}\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{8}\}.

Moreover,
T\subsetneq A
. The orthogonal complement, with respect to the Killing form
B
, is
\mathfrak{m}:=\mathfrak{a}^{\perp}=\mathrm{span}\{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{5},\lambda_{6},\lambda_{7}\}
, where

B(X,Y):=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(XY).

With the irregular element in (
5.36
), we now construct the stabiliser. By definition,
\mathrm{SU}(3)=\{A\in M_{3}(\mathbb{C}):\,\det A=1\}
, which represents the group action on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Work with the defining representation of
G=\mathrm{SU}(3)
in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
and fix
W=\mathrm{diag}(2,-1,-1)
. Since
W
is normal, we have
\mathbb{C}^{3}\cong E_{2}\oplus E_{-1}
, where
E_{2}
is a one-dimensional
2
-eigenspace and
E_{-1}
is a two dimensional
-1
-eigenspace. The corresponding matrix relative to this decomposition yields
1\times 1
and
2\times 2
block matrices. The centraliser

\displaystyle A=C_{G}(W)=\{a\in\mathrm{SU}(3):\,aW=Wa\}

consists of those unitary matrices that preserve each eigenspace of
W
. Hence, a simple computation shows that
A
is block-diagonal on this basis:

(5.37)

\displaystyle a=\begin{pmatrix}s&0\\[2.0pt]
0&U\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{with }s\in\mathrm{U}(1),\ U\in\mathrm{U}(2).

The condition
\det a=1
forces
s\cdot\det(U)=1
, i.e.,
a=\mathrm{diag}\bigl(\det(U)^{-1},\,U\bigr)\in\mathrm{SU}(3)
. Hence, in the matrix form,

\displaystyle A=C_{\mathrm{SU}(3)}(W)=\left\{\mathrm{diag}\left(\det(U)^{-1},\,U\right):\,U\in\mathrm{U}(2)\right\}\cong\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1)).

In Lie algebra setting, we therefore have a reductive decomposition
\mathfrak{su}(3)=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{u}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1))\oplus\mathfrak{m}
, where

(5.38)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0&u_{1}\\
0&0&u_{2}\\
-\overline{u}_{1}&-\overline{u}_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}:u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

Thus, in what follows, we identify
\mathfrak{m}
with
\mathbb{C}^{2}
by associating
(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
with the traceless matrix in (
5.38
) with
u_{1}=x_{4}-\mathrm{i}x_{5}
and
u_{2}=x_{6}-\mathrm{i}x_{7}
.

We now build the twisted symplectic structure on it. Recall that we have the following affine slice
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W=\{X-\varepsilon W:X\in\mathfrak{m}\}
, which can therefore be written as

\displaystyle\xi=x_{4}\lambda_{4}+x_{5}\lambda_{5}+x_{6}\lambda_{6}+x_{7}\lambda_{7}-\varepsilon W,\qquad x_{4},\ldots,x_{7}\in\mathbb{R}.

Under the choices of matrix representation given in (
5.38
), this can be explicitly written as a 3
\times
3 traceless skew-Hermitian matrix as follows:

(5.39)

\displaystyle\xi:=X-\varepsilon W=\begin{pmatrix}-2\varepsilon&0&x_{4}-\mathrm{i}x_{5}\\[6.0pt]
0&\varepsilon&x_{6}-\mathrm{i}x_{7}\\[6.0pt]
x_{4}+\mathrm{i}x_{5}&x_{6}+\mathrm{i}x_{7}&\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{su}(3).

Near the identity, choose a section

\displaystyle\sigma:U\subset\mathfrak{m}\xrightarrow{\,\exp\,}\exp(U)\subset G.

Elements of
G/A
are represented by
m=\sigma(y)
with
y=(y_{4},\ldots,y_{7})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Identify
\mathfrak{m}^{*}
with
\mathfrak{m}
in the Killing form
B
. A point in the local trivialization of
T^{*}M\cong(G\times\mathfrak{m})/A
is therefore given by:

(5.40)

\displaystyle(g,X):=\bigl(\sigma(y),\,X\bigr),\qquad X=\sum_{i=4}^{7}x_{i}\lambda_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}.

The canonical Liouville 1-form is
\varsigma=B(X,\,\sigma^{-1}d\sigma)
. Adding the magnetic 2-form
\varepsilon\,\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
with
W=\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{3}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\lambda_{8}
, the resulting symplectic 2-form is

\displaystyle\omega_{\varepsilon}=d\varsigma+\varepsilon\,B(W,\,[\sigma^{-1}d\sigma,\sigma^{-1}d\sigma]).

In the chosen coordinates
(x_{4},\ldots,x_{7},y_{4},\ldots,y_{7})
, the non-vanishing Poisson brackets are

\displaystyle\{x_{i},y_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=2\,\delta_{ij},\qquad\{x_{i},x_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=-2\varepsilon\,c_{ij}^{k}B(W,\lambda_{k}),\qquad\{y_{i},y_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=0

for any
i,j=4,\ldots,7
.

5.2.1.
Building superintegrable systems on
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/A)

Recall that the magmatic moment map is
P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr)\in\mathfrak{su}^{*}(3)
. Evaluating the representative
\xi
of the orbit
Z_{P}=\mathrm{Ad}(G)(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
, we obtain the eight linear generators as follows:

\displaystyle P_{i}:=\ell_{i}\circ P(g,X)=\ell_{i}\bigl(x_{4}\lambda_{4}+\ldots+x_{7}\lambda_{7}-\varepsilon W\bigr),

where
\ell_{i}(P)=\langle P,\lambda_{i}\rangle
. A direct trace calculation yields

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle\,=P_{2}=0,\quad P_{3}=\frac{3\varepsilon}{2},\quad P_{4}=-x_{4},

\displaystyle P_{5}

\displaystyle\,=-x_{5},\text{ }P_{6}=-x_{6},\text{ }P_{7}=-x_{7},\text{ }P_{8}=\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\varepsilon}{2}.

Thus, the linear coordinates
(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6},x_{7})
are
(-P_{4},-P_{5},-P_{6},-P_{7})
. Define the coordinate functions in
\mathfrak{su}(3)
,

\displaystyle P_{i}(Y)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(Y\lambda_{i}),\quad i=1,\ldots,8.

In particular,

\displaystyle P(g,X)=\sum_{i=1}^{8}P_{i}(g,X)\lambda_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{P_{3}}{2}+\frac{P_{8}}{2\sqrt{3}}&-\frac{1}{2}(P_{1}-\mathrm{i}P_{2})&-\frac{1}{2}(P_{4}-\mathrm{i}P_{5})\\
-\frac{1}{2}(P_{1}+\mathrm{i}P_{2})&-\frac{P_{3}}{2}+\frac{P_{8}}{2\sqrt{3}}&-\frac{1}{2}(P_{6}-\mathrm{i}P_{7})\\
-\frac{1}{2}(P_{4}+\mathrm{i}P_{5})&-\frac{1}{2}(P_{6}+\mathrm{i}P_{7})&-\frac{P_{8}}{2\sqrt{3}}\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad R(g,X)=P_{4}^{2}+\ldots+P_{7}^{2}.

Thus

\displaystyle Z_{P}=\{P((g,X)):(g,X)\in T^{*}M\},\quad\dim Z_{P}=7.

In the following example, we will provide the commutator relations of the first integrals in
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
under the twisted Poisson bracket.

Example 5.11
.

Let
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{8}
be the GM basis defined above with
[\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}]=2\mathrm{i}\,c_{ij}^{k}\,\lambda_{k}
. Choose
B(X,Y):=-\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{tr}(XY)
. Then
P_{i}=\langle P,\lambda_{i}\rangle
and

(5.41)

\displaystyle\{P_{i},P_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=\langle P,[\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}]\rangle=\langle P,2\mathrm{i}\,c_{ij}^{k}\,\lambda_{k}\rangle=2\mathrm{i}\,c_{ij}^{k}\,\langle P,\lambda_{k}\rangle=2\mathrm{i}c_{ij}^{k}\,P_{k}.

For instance, taking the structure constant to be
c_{12}^{3}=1
. Then the twisted Poisson brackets are given by

\displaystyle\{P_{1},P_{2}\}_{\varepsilon}=2\mathrm{i}P_{3},\qquad\{P_{1},P_{3}\}_{\varepsilon}=-2\mathrm{i}P_{2},\qquad\{P_{2},P_{3}\}_{\varepsilon}=2\mathrm{i}P_{1},

and similarly for the other nonzero structure tensor
c_{ij}^{k}
:

\displaystyle c_{14}^{7}=c_{24}^{6}=c_{25}^{7}=c_{34}^{5}=c_{51}^{6}=c_{63}^{7}=\frac{1}{2},\qquad c_{45}^{8}=c_{67}^{8}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},

with antisymmetry in the lower indices giving the rest of the relations. These identities match (
5.44
).

Remark 5.12
.

All
\varepsilon
-dependence of the magnetic deformation is absorbed into the shift
P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(X-\varepsilon W)
. The calculation above uses only the
\mathrm{Ad}
-equivariance of
P
and the fact that
X_{P_{\xi}}
is the fundamental left field. Recall that by Proposition
4.19
, the bracket induced from
P
is therefore the ordinary Lie-Poisson bracket in
\mathfrak{g}
, without explicit
\varepsilon
in (
4.16
). Thus, we verified again, on
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}
, we have
\{P_{i},P_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}
exactly as in the
\varepsilon=0
case, whereas the
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
brackets carry the
\varepsilon
terms.

Recall that from (
5.39
), we have the matrix form
\xi=\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon\,\mathbf{1}_{2}&z\\[2.0pt]
z^{*}&-2\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}
. A direct block multiplication gives the following:

\displaystyle\xi^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbf{1}_{2}+zz^{*}&-\varepsilon z\\[2.0pt]
-\varepsilon z^{*}&z^{*}z+4\varepsilon^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\xi^{2})=6\varepsilon^{2}+2\left(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}\right),\text{ and }C_{2}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{tr}(\xi^{2})=3\varepsilon^{2}+\left(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}\right),

where
C_{2}(\xi)
is the restricted Casimir from
S(\mathfrak{su}(3))
into
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
. Using trace normalizations, the quadratic and cubic Casimir are given by
C_{2}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\xi^{2})
and
C_{3}(\xi)=-\mathrm{tr}(\xi^{3})
. Recall that from (
5.14
), these Casimir can be rewritten by
C_{2}:=\sum_{i=1}^{8}P_{i}^{2}
and
C_{3}:=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{8}d_{ijk}P_{i}P_{j}P_{k}
. Under the real coordinate expression, we further rewrite
C_{2}(\xi)
into the following form:

\displaystyle C_{2}(\xi)=x_{4}^{2}+x_{5}^{2}+x_{6}^{2}+x_{7}^{2}+3\varepsilon^{2}.

Define the
A
-invariant quadratic monomial by
R:=x_{4}^{2}+x_{5}^{2}+x_{6}^{2}+x_{7}^{2}
. Then
C_{2}(\xi)=R+3\varepsilon^{2}
. A straightforward symbolic computation gives

(5.42)

\displaystyle C_{3}(\xi)=3\varepsilon\bigl(2\varepsilon^{2}+x_{4}^{2}+x_{5}^{2}-2x_{6}^{2}-2x_{7}^{2}\bigr).

Write
w:=x_{4}^{2}+x_{5}^{2}
and
t:=x_{6}^{2}+x_{7}^{2}
such that
R=w+t
. Then (
5.42
) becomes

(5.43)

\displaystyle C_{3}(\xi)=3\varepsilon\bigl(2\varepsilon^{2}+w-2t\bigr)=3\varepsilon\bigl(2\varepsilon^{2}+R-3t\bigr).

Since
w-t
is not
A
-invariant, the cubic Casimir does not survive the restriction map
\mathrm{Res}_{W}:S(\mathfrak{su}(3))^{G}\rightarrow S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. Therefore, by the definition of the restriction mapping, we deduce

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\mathrm{Res}_{W}=\mathbb{R}[R]\,\Longrightarrow\,s=1.

Substituting the restricted Casimir above, we eliminate the slice coordinates
x_{4},\ldots,x_{7}
and obtain a single cubic relation among the eight moment coordinates

\displaystyle\Phi(P):=C_{3}(\xi)-3\varepsilon\Bigl(2\varepsilon^{2}+P_{4}^{2}+P_{5}^{2}-2P_{6}^{2}-2P_{7}^{2}\Bigr)=0.

No quadratic relation appears as
C_{2}(\xi)
is free on
\mathrm{Ad}(\mathrm{SU}(3))(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}S(\mathfrak{su}(3))\cong\mathbb{R}[P_{1},\ldots,P_{8}]\big/\langle\Phi\rangle\text{ and }\mathrm{rank}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=7.

Concluding from this result, we present the following proposition.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
G=\mathrm{SU}(3)
,
A\subset G
be the same as defined above. Let
P:T^{*}M\rightarrow\mathfrak{su}(3)^{*}
given by
P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)\bigl(X-\varepsilon W\bigr)
be the magnetic moment map for the left action, and for
\xi\in\mathfrak{su}(3)
set

\displaystyle P_{\xi}(g,X):=B\bigl(P(g,X),\xi\bigr),\qquad P_{k}:=P_{\xi_{k}}

with
k=1,\dots,8
. Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}S(\mathfrak{su}(3))=\mathbb{R}[P_{1},\dots,P_{8}]\big/\left\langle\Phi\right\rangle\quad\text{ with }\deg P_{k}=1,

and the Poisson commutator relations in coordinates are

(5.44)

\{P_{i},P_{j}\}_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{k=1}^{8}c_{ij}^{k}\,P_{k},\qquad[\xi_{i},\xi_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{8}c_{ij}^{k}\,\xi_{k}.

We now compute the generators of
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. In other words, it is equivalent to deducing the generators of
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. By construction, the adjoint orbit
\mathrm{SU}(3)/\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1))
is identified with a homogeneous space
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. Here, we will use the standard tangent space description for the Grassmannians of Hermitian spaces. For the
2
-dimensional eigenspace of
W
, set a line
E\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that
[E]\in\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. In particular, there exists a canonical identification as follows:
T_{[E]}M\cong\mathrm{Hom}(E,E^{\perp})\cong E^{\perp}\otimes E^{*}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
. With respect to the same block decomposition, every
X\in\mathfrak{m}
is unique in form of

\displaystyle X=\begin{pmatrix}0&z^{*}\\[2.0pt]
z&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{for a unique column }z\in\mathbb{C}^{2},

so
\mathfrak{m}
is naturally identified with
\mathbb{C}^{2}
through the off-block columns
\begin{pmatrix}0\\
z\end{pmatrix}
and
\begin{pmatrix}z^{*}\\
0\end{pmatrix}
, where
z^{*}=\overline{z}^{T}
and
\overline{z}
is the conjugation of the complex. Hence, under the identification
\mathfrak{m}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the induced linear action of
A
on
\mathfrak{m}
is

\displaystyle z\longmapsto\rho(A)z:=\det(U)\,Uz\qquad(U\in\mathrm{U}(2)).

Recall that from (
5.37
), an element
a\in A
is a block diagonal matrix

\displaystyle a=\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\
0&\det(U)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad U\in\mathrm{U}(2).

For
a=\mathrm{diag}(\det(U)^{-1},U)\in A
, a direct computation shows that, through conjugation actions, we have

(5.45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}(a)(X)=aXa^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\det(U)^{-1}\,z^{*}U^{-1}\\[2.0pt]
\det(U)\,Uz&0\end{pmatrix}.

The adjoint action on the slice is
X-\varepsilon W\mapsto a(X-\varepsilon W)a^{-1}
. The usual matrix multiplication with the block form

X-\varepsilon W=\begin{pmatrix}D&z\\
z^{*}&\varepsilon\end{pmatrix},\quad D=\begin{pmatrix}-2\varepsilon&0\\
0&\varepsilon\end{pmatrix},

gives

(5.46)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}(a)(\xi)=a\,\xi\,a^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\[2.0pt]
0&\det(U)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon\,\mathbf{1}_{2}&z\\[2.0pt]
z^{*}&-2\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}U^{-1}&0\\[2.0pt]
0&\det(U)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon\,\mathbf{1}_{2}&\det(U)\,Uz\\[2.0pt]
\det(U)^{-1}z^{*}U^{-1}&-2\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}.

This can be summarised into the following proposition.

Proposition 5.14
.

Fix
G=\mathrm{SU}(3)
,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(3)
, the Killing form
B
, and

\displaystyle W=\mathrm{diag}(2,-1,-1),\qquad A=C_{G}(W)\cong\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1)).

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
be the reductive
B
-orthogonal decomposition with
\mathfrak{a}=\ker(\mathrm{ad}_{W})
. Identify
\mathfrak{m}
with
\mathbb{C}^{2}
by

\displaystyle z=(z_{1},z_{2})^{T}\longleftrightarrow X(z)=\begin{pmatrix}0&z^{*}\\[2.0pt]
z&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{m},

such that for
a=\mathrm{diag}(\det(U)^{-1},U)\in A
with
U\in\mathrm{U}(2)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ad}(a)\bigl(X(z)\bigr)=X\bigl(\rho(U)z\bigr),\qquad\rho(U)=\det(U)\,U.

We now focus on finding the generators of the finitely generated polynomial algebra
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. For any
F\in S(\mathfrak{m})
, set
f(z,\overline{z}):=F\circ X\in\mathbb{R}[z_{1},z_{2},\bar{z}_{1},\bar{z}_{2}]
. By definition, for any
a\in A
, we have
(a\cdot F)(X):=F\bigl(\mathrm{Ad}(a^{-1})X\bigr)
. In other words, by Proposition
5.14
, we deduce

(5.47)

\displaystyle(a\cdot f)(z,\bar{z})=f\bigl(\rho(U)^{-1}z,\ \overline{\rho(U)^{-1}z}\bigr).

We then process the coordinate computation by first illustrating the application of Proposition
3.5
in this example.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
A=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1))
act on the off-block column
z=(z_{1},z_{2})^{T}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
by the unitary representation

(5.48)

\displaystyle\rho(U)z=\det(U)\,Uz\qquad\text{ for any }U\in\mathrm{U}(2).

Suppose that
f=f(z,\overline{z})\in S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. Then, for every
H\in\mathfrak{u}(2)
, we have the linear first-order PDE

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{j}}\,\bigl((H+\mathrm{tr}H\,\mathbf{1}_{2})z\bigr)_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}}\,\bigl((\overline{H+\mathrm{tr}H\,\mathbf{1}_{2}})\,\overline{z}\bigr)_{j}=0,

where
\mathbf{1}_{2}
is a
2\times 2
identity matrix. Equivalently,

\displaystyle B(\partial_{z}f,\;(H+\mathrm{tr}H\,\mathbf{1}_{2})z)+B(\partial_{\overline{z}}f,\;(\overline{H+\mathrm{tr}H\,\mathbf{1}_{2}})\,\overline{z})=0,

where
\partial_{z}f=\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial z}
and
\partial_{\overline{z}}f=\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\overline{z}}
.

Proof.

Fix
H\in\mathfrak{u}(2)
and consider the one-parameter subgroup

\displaystyle a(t)=\begin{pmatrix}\exp(tH)&0\\[2.0pt]
0&\det(\exp(tH))^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in A,\qquad\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)=\det\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)\,\exp(tH).

Since
f
is
A
-invariant, for all
t
near
0
,

\displaystyle f\Bigl(\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z,\;\overline{\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z}\Bigr)=f(z,\overline{z}).

Differentiate at
t=0
and using the chain rule, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}f\Bigl(\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z,\overline{\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z}\Bigr)

(5.49)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial z_{j}}\,\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\bigl(\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z\bigr)_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\overline{z}_{j}}\,\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\,\overline{\bigl(\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z\bigr)_{j}}.

We then compute the derivative of
\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z
at
t=0
in (
5.49
). By implement the definition of
\rho
, this gives that

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\Bigl(\det(\exp(tH))\,\exp(tH)\Bigr)=\Bigl(\mathrm{tr}H\,\mathbf{1}_{2}+H\Bigr).

Hence,

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\bigl(\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z\bigr)=(H+\mathrm{tr}H\,\mathbf{1}_{2})z,\qquad\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\overline{\bigl(\rho\bigl(\exp(tH)\bigr)z\bigr)}=(\overline{H+\mathrm{tr}H\,\mathbf{1}_{2}})\,\overline{z}.

Substituting these into the chain rule identity and using Lemma
5.15
yields precisely the displayed PDE.
∎

Now, with the following proposition, we have a geometric derivation on finding the generator of
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
R(z):=||z||^{2}=z^{*}z=|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}=x_{4}^{2}+x_{5}^{2}+x_{6}^{2}+x_{7}^{2}
. Then
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}=\mathbb{R}\bigl[R\bigr]
, and consequently

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\bigl(S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\bigr)\cong\mathbb{R}[R].

Proof.

By Lemma
4.7
,
S(\mathfrak{g})^{A}
restricting along
\tau_{-\varepsilon W}
produces an isomorphism
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}\cong S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. Thus, it suffices to compute
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}
. Under the given identification,
A
acts unitarily on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
through
\rho
and the real polynomial ring on
S(\mathfrak{m})\cong\mathbb{R}[z_{1},z_{2},\overline{z}_{1},\overline{z}_{2}]
. Moreover, recall that from (
5.46
), conjugation by
a=\mathrm{diag}(U,\det(U)^{-1})\in A
gives
\mathrm{Ad}(a)(\xi)=\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon\,\mathbf{1}_{2}&\det(U)\,Uz\\[2.0pt]
\det(U)^{-1}z^{*}U^{-1}&-2\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}
. The first Fundamental Theorem for
\mathrm{U}(2)
shows that the generators in
\mathbb{R}[z,\overline{z}]^{\mathrm{U}(2)}
are generated by the Hermitian pairings
z^{*}z
. Equivalently, we deduce directly as follows. Since
\mathbb{R}[z_{1},\overline{z}_{2}]
is a graded algebra, we decompose it into bihomogeneous components:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}[z,\overline{z}]=\bigoplus_{p,q\geq 0}\,\mathcal{P}^{p,q},\qquad\text{ where }\mathcal{P}^{p,q}=\mathrm{span}\{\,z^{\alpha}\overline{z}^{\beta}:\ |\alpha|=p,\ |\beta|=q\,\}.

As a
\mathrm{U}(2)
-module,
\mathcal{P}^{p,q}\cong\mathrm{Sym}^{p}(\mathbb{C}^{2})\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{q}(\overline{\mathbb{C}^{2}})
.
By Schur’s lemma, the invariant subspace
\bigl(\mathcal{P}^{p,q}\bigr)^{\mathrm{U}(2)}
is
0
unless
p=q
, and when
p=q
, it is one-dimensional, spanned by
(z^{*}z)^{p}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}[z,\overline{z}]^{\mathrm{U}(2)}=\mathbb{R}[z^{*}z]=\mathbb{R}[R],

as claimed. Translating back to
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
gives
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}=\mathbb{R}[R]
.

In particular, as
R=C_{2}-3\varepsilon^{2}
on
\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
, hence
R
is
A
-invariant and generates
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
. Pulling back along
\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}:T^{*}M\to\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W
yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\bigl(S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}\bigr)=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\left(\mathbb{R}\bigl[R\bigr]\right)

as claimed.
∎

Using the polynomial ansatz mentioned in Section
3
, the generators in
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}
can also be obtained via direct computation.

Remark 5.17
.

Choose the basis of
\mathfrak{u}(2)
given by
H_{1}=\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1}
,
H_{2}=\mathrm{i}\sigma_{2}
,
H_{3}=\mathrm{i}\sigma_{3}
,
H_{0}=\mathrm{i}\textbf{1}_{2}
, where
\sigma_{k}
,
k=0,1,2,3
, are the Pauli matrices and
\textbf{1}_{2}
is a
2\times 2
identity matrix. Writing
z_{1}=x_{4}+\mathrm{i}x_{5}
,
z_{2}=x_{6}+\mathrm{i}x_{7}
, the corresponding fundamental vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
with coordinates
(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6},x_{7})
are

\displaystyle V_{1}

\displaystyle=-x_{7}\,\partial_{x_{4}}+x_{6}\,\partial_{x_{5}}-x_{5}\,\partial_{x_{6}}+x_{4}\,\partial_{x_{7}},

\displaystyle V_{2}

\displaystyle=x_{6}\,\partial_{x_{4}}+x_{7}\,\partial_{x_{5}}-x_{4}\,\partial_{x_{6}}-x_{5}\,\partial_{x_{7}},

\displaystyle V_{3}

\displaystyle=-x_{5}\,\partial_{x_{4}}+x_{4}\,\partial_{x_{5}}+x_{7}\,\partial_{x_{6}}-x_{6}\,\partial_{x_{7}},

\displaystyle V_{0}

\displaystyle=-3x_{5}\,\partial_{x_{4}}+3x_{4}\,\partial_{x_{5}}-3x_{7}\,\partial_{x_{6}}+3x_{6}\,\partial_{x_{7}}.

By Proposition
3.5
, finding the
A
-invariant polynomial
f
is equivalent to solving the linear system of first-order PDEs

\displaystyle V_{k}(f)=0\quad\text{ with }k=0,1,2,3.

Hence,
S(\mathfrak{m})^{A}=\ker\bigcap_{k=0}^{3}V_{k}
. The quadratic monomial
R=x_{4}^{2}+x_{5}^{2}+x_{6}^{2}+x_{7}^{2}=||z||^{2}
satisfies
V_{k}(R)=0
for all
k
, and conversely, solving the system in the polynomial ansatz forces
f
to be a polynomial in
R
. Hence,
S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A}=\mathbb{R}[R]
.

As illustrated in Proposition
5.16
, in the irregular case,
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
consists of only one
A
-invariant generator, in contrast to the
4
generators in the regular case. Indeed, this aligns with the fact that an adjoint orbit of type
A_{2}
has an irregular stabiliser with dimension
4
, which yields a smaller family of first integrals.

Since
C_{2}=R+3\varepsilon^{2}
and
R
are
G
-invariant,
\mathbb{R}[R]
lies inside
\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
. Hence,
R_{0}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=P^{*}\mathbb{R}[R]=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbb{R}[R]
. With all the construction above, we see that
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\mathbb{R}[P_{1},\ldots,P_{8}]/\langle\Phi\rangle\otimes_{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbb{R}[R]}\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathbb{R}[R]
is verified by Theorem
4.65
. All Poisson brackets follow from
\{P_{i},P_{j}\}_{\mathcal{A}}=2c_{ij}^{k}P_{k}
,
\{P_{i},R\}_{\mathcal{A}}=0
, and
\{R,R\}_{\mathcal{A}}=0
. This further implies that Poisson commutative base algebra
\mathcal{B}=R_{0}
. Next, by a direct rank computation, we show that
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/A),\mathcal{A}
and
R_{0}
forms a superintegrable system with Poisson projection chains.

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
G=\mathrm{SU}(3)
and
A=\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(2)\times\mathrm{U}(1))
, and let
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}},\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}
,
R_{0}
, and
\mathcal{A}
be the same as defined in Lemma
5.13
and Proposition
5.16
. Then there exist two Poisson projections
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
such that the following chain

(5.50)

\displaystyle T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}Z_{P}=\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}

is superintegrable.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.13
and Proposition
5.16
, we deduce
\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}=\mathbb{R}[P_{1},\ldots,P_{8}]\big/\langle\Phi\rangle,\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}=R_{0}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\mathbb{R}[R]
and
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{R}[P_{1},\ldots,P_{8}]\big/\langle\Phi\rangle

\otimes_{\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\mathbb{R}[R]}\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\mathbb{R}[R]
. Consequently,
\mathcal{A}=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\subset\mathcal{O}(T^{*}M)
is a Poisson algebra, with the inclusion being a Poisson algebra morphism. It follows that the map
\pi_{1}
induced by the evaluation of generators is a Poisson morphism. Moreover, since
\mathcal{B}=R_{0}=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}\mathbb{R}[R]
carries the trivial Poisson bracket, the structure map
\pi_{2}:Z_{P}\to\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
is Poisson as well. We now check the dimension of the spectrum of these algebras. It follows that

\displaystyle\dim T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/A)=8=\dim\mathcal{A}+\dim R_{0}.

To conclude that the chain (
5.50
) is superintegrable, it remains to show that
d\pi_{1}
and
d\pi_{2}
are surjective. By the left-equivariance of
P
, we may compute
d\pi_{1}
at
g=e
. Take
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
. Using the differential of the moment map (
4.13
), for
(u,v)\in\mathfrak{m}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
, we have

\displaystyle dP_{(e,X)}(u,v)=[u,\xi]+v.

Hence, for
1\leq i\leq 7
,

\displaystyle dP_{i}(u,v)=B([u,\xi]+v,\lambda_{i})=\underbrace{B(v,\lambda_{i})}_{\text{fiber}}+\underbrace{B([u,X],\lambda_{i})}_{\text{base via }X}-\varepsilon\,\underbrace{B([u,W],\lambda_{i})}_{\text{base via }W}.

Choose algebraic coordinates on
Z_{P}
given by
(P_{1},\ldots,P_{7})
. The corresponding coordinates
(x_{4},x_{5},x_{6},x_{7};y_{4},y_{5},y_{6},y_{7})
on
T^{*}_{[}e]M
are given by writing
X=-\sum_{j=4}^{7}x_{j}\lambda_{j}
and
u=\sum_{j=4}^{7}y_{j}\lambda_{j}
, such that
P_{j}|_{g=e}=-x_{j}
for all
j
. The reductive commutator relations
[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}]\subset\mathfrak{a}
and
[\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{a}]\subset\mathfrak{m}
imply that the Jacobian of
\pi_{1}
at
(e,X)
has the block form

\displaystyle J_{\pi_{1}}(e,X)=\begin{pmatrix}\textbf{0}_{3\times 4}&A(X)_{3\times 4}\\[2.0pt]
-\mathbf{I}_{4\times 4}&-\varepsilon\,B_{4\times 4}\end{pmatrix},

where
A(X)
is the
3\times 4
matrix of the linear map
u\mapsto\big(B([u,X],\lambda_{1}),B([u,X],\lambda_{2}),B([u,X],\lambda_{3})\big)
. Under the GM basis, we are able to check explicitly that

\displaystyle A(X)=\begin{pmatrix}-x_{7}&x_{6}&-x_{5}&x_{4}\\[2.0pt]
-x_{6}&-x_{7}&x_{4}&x_{5}\\[2.0pt]
-x_{5}&x_{4}&x_{7}&-x_{6}\end{pmatrix},

whose
3\times 3
column minors are

\displaystyle\det A_{(1,2,3)}=x_{7}\sum_{j=4}^{7}x_{j}^{2},\quad\det A_{(1,2,4)}=-x_{6}\sum_{j=4}^{7}x_{j}^{2},\quad\det A_{(1,3,4)}=x_{5}\sum_{j=4}^{7}x_{j}^{2},\quad\det A_{(2,3,4)}=-x_{4}\sum_{j=4}^{7}x_{j}^{2}.

Here
A_{(i,j,k)}
means the matrix formed by taking
i
th,
j
th and
k
th column of it. In particular,
\mathrm{rank}\,A(X)=3
for every
X\neq 0
. Since the lower-left block of
J_{\pi_{1}}(e,X)
is
-\mathbf{I}_{4\times 4}
, elementary row/column operations give

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\,J_{\pi_{1}}(e,X)=4+\mathrm{rank}\,A(X)=7\qquad(X\neq 0).

By left-equivariance, this rank is the same at
(g,X)
for all
g\in G
. Hence,
d\pi_{1}
is surjective on the Zariski open dense subset
\{(g,X)\in T^{*}M:X\neq 0\}
.

We now focus on the Jacobian of
\pi_{2}
. Identify
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{B}\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
with coordinate
R
. By Proposition
5.16
,
R=\sum_{j=4}^{7}P_{j}^{2}
in
Z_{P}
, from which
dR=\sum_{j=4}^{7}2P_{j}\,dP_{j}
. Therefore, in relation to
(P_{1},\ldots,P_{7})
, the Jacobian matrix for
\pi_{2}
is given by

\displaystyle J_{\pi_{2}}=\bigl(0,0,0,\,2P_{4},2P_{5},2P_{6},2P_{7}\bigr),

and
\mathrm{rank}\,J_{\pi_{2}}=1
, where
(P_{4},\ldots,P_{7})\neq 0
. At
g=e
, this locus is
\{X\neq 0\}
since
P_{j}=-x_{j}
for
j=4,\ldots,7
. By
G
-equivariance, we can translate to all
g\in G
. Thus,
d\pi_{2}
is surjective on the same Zariski open dense subset, and as observed above,
\pi_{2}
is Poisson because
\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{R}[R]
carries the trivial Poisson bracket. At the exceptional locus
X=0
, we have
A(0)=0
. Therefore,
\mathrm{rank}\,J_{\pi_{1}}=4
and
\mathrm{rank}\,J_{\pi_{2}}=0
.
∎

5.2.2.
Construction of action-angle coordinates on
T^{*}(\mathrm{SU}(3)/A)

To conclude Subsection
5.2
, similar to what we did in Subsection
5.1
, we present the fact that the superintegrable system (
5.50
) admits an angle-action coordinate. Fix a regular value
b\in\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{B}=\textbf{Alg}\langle R\rangle
. In the dense open stratum,

\displaystyle U:=\{z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}\neq 0\}\subset Z_{P},

the fiber
(\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1})^{-1}(b)\cap U
is a right
T
-orbit. By Lemma
5.9
, define the geometric right-
T
angles on
U
(mod
2\pi
) by

\displaystyle\phi_{1}\equiv-\frac{\vartheta_{1}+\vartheta_{3}}{3},\qquad\phi_{2}\equiv-\frac{\vartheta_{2}+\vartheta_{3}}{3},\qquad d\phi_{1}=-\frac{1}{3}(d\vartheta_{1}+d\vartheta_{3}),\quad d\phi_{2}=-\frac{1}{3}(d\vartheta_{2}+d\vartheta_{3}),

such that for the fundamental right-action vector fields
\widehat{H_{j}}
with
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle d\phi_{i}(\widehat{H_{j}})=(L\Theta)_{ij}=\delta_{ij},

and therefore
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
parameterize each right
T
-fiber. Similarly to what we did in Subsection
5.1
, we will provide the explicit expression of the frequency matrix. For a one-dimensional base
\mathcal{B}=\textbf{Alg}\langle J\rangle\cong\mathbb{R}[J]
, recall that the frequency matrix
\Omega
in (
5.34
) is defined by

(5.51)

\displaystyle\Omega(J):=\begin{pmatrix}\Omega_{1}\\
\Omega_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\{\phi_{1},J\}_{\varepsilon}\\[2.0pt]
\{\phi_{2},J\}_{\varepsilon}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d\phi_{1}(X_{J})\\[2.0pt]
d\phi_{2}(X_{J})\end{pmatrix}.

Using the phase representation (
5.30
),

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}=-\frac{1}{3}\bigl(\{\vartheta_{1},J\}_{\varepsilon}+\{\vartheta_{3},J\}_{\varepsilon}\bigr),\qquad\Omega_{2}=-\frac{1}{3}\bigl(\{\vartheta_{2},J\}_{\varepsilon}+\{\vartheta_{3},J\}_{\varepsilon}\bigr).

On the regular locus
U
, we have
d\vartheta_{k}=\imaginary\bigl(dz_{k}/z_{k}\bigr)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\{\vartheta_{k},J\}_{\varepsilon}=d\vartheta_{k}(X_{J})=\imaginary\left(\frac{\{z_{k},J\}_{\varepsilon}}{z_{k}}\right),\qquad k=1,2,3.

Choose a row vector
u=(u_{1},u_{2})
with
u\cdot\Omega(J)=1
. Note that for
\Omega(J)\neq 0
, there exists a normalization of the frequency as follows:

\displaystyle u=\frac{\Omega(J)^{\top}}{||\Omega(J)||^{2}}=\frac{(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2})}{\Omega_{1}^{2}+\Omega_{2}^{2}}.

Then the angular coordinate is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}:=u_{1}\phi_{1}+u_{2}\phi_{2},

which also satisfies
\left\{\widetilde{\phi},J\right\}_{\varepsilon}=1
.

6.
Conclusion

In this work, we have presented an approach for constructing (super)integrable geodesic flows on a class of symmetric symplectic manifolds using a Poisson projection chain construction. In particular, using invariant polynomial functions in Lie algebras and their Poisson-commutative properties, we obtained explicit first integrals for magnetic geodesic flows in
T^{*}(G/A)
, where
G/A
is an adjoint orbit. An important observation from our work is the algebro-geometric construction of the integrals of motion, which provides a unifying perspective on various classical approaches (such as the argument shift method and the Gelfand-Cetlin-type constructions) within a single coherent framework in the language of Poisson projection chains. The example of the full flag manifold
\mathrm{SU}(3)/T
demonstrates that our method can produce the set of integrals required for superintegrability in nontrivial cases, including situations with irregular elements in the underlying Lie algebra. All integrals of motion in this example are given by explicit polynomial functions in the phase space, illustrating the effectiveness of our constructive approach.

Note that the approaches developed in Section
4
can be extended to other symmetric or homogeneous spaces beyond the specific case of a semisimple, simply connected, and compact Lie group. Concretely, let
G
be a real reductive Lie group and let
A\subset G
be a closed reductive subgroup such that there is an
A
-invariant splitting
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
. In
M=G/A
, equip
T^{*}M
with the magnetic form
\omega_{\varepsilon}=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}+\varepsilon\,\pi^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{KKS}}
, with
W\in\mathfrak{g}
fixed by
\mathrm{Ad}(A)
. Using the same maps as in Section
4
,

\displaystyle P(g,X)=\mathrm{Ad}(g)(X-\varepsilon W),\qquad\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}(g,X)=X-\varepsilon W,

we again obtain the two polynomial blocks as follows

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=P^{*}S(\mathfrak{g}),\qquad\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}:=\pi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}S(\mathfrak{m}-\varepsilon W)^{A},\qquad R_{0}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\cap\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}.

Their mixed Poisson brackets vanish, and in the regular stratum
U\subset T^{*}M
,where
\xi=X-\varepsilon W
is regular, the same field-intersection argument shows
\mathrm{Frac}(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}})\cap\mathrm{Frac}(\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}})=\mathrm{Frac}(R_{0})
. Hence, the fiber tensor product is injected into the composition of the two fraction fields and is reduced,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{A}}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}\;\hookrightarrow\;\mathrm{Frac}(\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}})\,\mathrm{Frac}(\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}),\qquad\mathrm{trdeg}\,\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}+\mathrm{trdeg}\,\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}-\mathrm{trdeg}\,R_{0}.

In particular, the Poisson projection chain persists under such a generalisation:

\displaystyle T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\;\pi_{1}\;}\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\;\pi_{2}\;}\mathrm{Spec}R_{0},\qquad\mathcal{A}:=\mathfrak{F}_{1}^{\mathrm{poly}}\otimes_{R_{0}}\mathfrak{F}_{2}^{\mathrm{poly}}.

Another direction for future work is to examine quantum analogues of these superintegrable systems, investigating how the classical polynomial integrals correspond to commuting quantum operators (sometimes referred to as missing label operators in representation theory). These connections could enhance our comprehension of how symmetries, integrability, and algebraic geometry interplay in theoretical physics. In particular, we will systematically analyse, for each superintegrable system built from our Poisson projection chains, the symplectic foliation and the pullback behaviour of generic fibers along the canonical maps. Concretely, we will study the chain of Poisson morphisms
T^{*}M\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{1}\ }\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{2}\ }\mathrm{Spec}\,R_{0}
, describe the symplectic leaves in
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathcal{A}
, and track how generic
\pi_{2}
-fibers pull back under
\pi_{1}
to produce isotropic fibers in
T^{*}M
. This viewpoint clarifies how leaf dimensions and ranks vary between different choices of
\mathcal{A}
and along chains of subgroups, which also provide the further geometric structures in the phase space via stratifications and reductions.
